PREFACE 


Tuis series of books owes its existenee to the generosity of 
Messrs. Hart, Schaffner & Marx, of Chicago, who have 
shown a special interest in trying to draw the attention of 
American youth to the study of economic and commercial 
subjects. For this purpose they have delegated to the un- 
dersigned committee the task of selecting or approving of 
topics, making announcements, and awarding prizes an- 
nually for those who wish to compete. | 

For the year 1926 there were offered: 

In Class A, which included any American without re- 
striction, a first prize of $1000, and a second prize of $500. 

In Class B, which included any who were at the time 
undergraduates of an American college, a first prize of $300, 
and a second prize of $200. 

Any essay submitted in Class B, if deemed of sufficient 
merit, could receive a prize in Class A. 

The present volume, submitted in Class A, was awarded 
First Prize. 


J. LAURENCE LAUGHLIN, Chairman 

University of Chicago 
Joun B. CLARK 

Columbia University 
EpwIn F. Gay 

Harvard University 

THEODORE E. BuRTON 

Washington, D.C, 
WEsLEyY C. MITCHELL 

Columbia University 


AUTHOR'S PREFACE 


Our knowledge of the economic processes, long summarized 
in a valuable body of ‘principles’ or ‘laws,’ has experienced 
the promise of much enrichment in recent years as the result 
of a host of inductive studies of concrete economic phenom- 
ena. Such studies need not aim at new large generalizations. 
Apart from their value in special fields, their worth is amply 
established if they but serve to refine, to clarify, or to enrich 
by specific illustration, the body of economic theory that has 
thus far been the fruit of a priort reasoning most largely. 
The aim of the present study is to take a place with countless 
other empirical investigations, the joint product of which, it 
is hoped, will be an expansion of our knowledge of the eco- 
nomic processes. 

One of the outstanding characteristics of contemporary 
industry is the striking mobility of economic resources. The 
enormous advances of the past century in the means of com- 
munication and in the arts and sciences have greatly ac- 
celerated the rate of economic change. New products and 
new methods of production appear almost daily; older lines 
change rapidly in importance. Consumption habits and 
industrial practices, alike, display a remarkable flexibility, 
ready ever to stimulate and to accommodate the new and to 
modify relations among the old. | 

Changes in industry appear to take place Pentaie diate. 
for they are brought about by thousands of individuals and 
groups, each acting more or less independently of others, and 
each motivated and guided by the hope of profits. Initiative 
in business is controlled by no central institution, but is open 
to all who can command the necessary productive resources. 
This initiative, and hence, the resources, are attracted to all 
lines of enterprise that offer relatively large returns. The 
existence of different and changing rates of profits in differ- 


x AUTHOR’S PREFACE 


ent fields operates constantly to regulate the employment of 
productive resources by causing them to be diverted from 
less to more profitable channels. In consequence, the prevail- 
ing distribution of productive agents among the various in- 
dustries, products, and services is always tentative, and is 
achieved by a process of constant readjustment. 

While changes in industry are motivated chiefly by profits, 
they are largely conditioned by the actual readiness of land, 
labor, capital, and business initiative to shift from one em- 
ployment to another. Perfect mobility in these agents is an. 
important postulate of traditional economic theory; and the 
practical limitations upon such mobility are responsible for 
various apparent anomalies in the world of competitive 
business, such as continuing discrepancies in the compara- 
tive prices, wages, profits, and interest rates of different 
countries, sections, trades, and industries. In modern in- 
dustry, important changes are peculiarly conditioned by the 
mobility of capital. The large-scale industrial technique of. 
our times requires the employment of enormous quantities of 
resources for mediate uses — for tools, machines, buildings, 
products in process, reserves against fluctuations, etc. The 
commercial production of an important new good, the auto- 
mobile, for example, is conditioned, therefore, by the avail- 
ability of free economic resources to be.employed for such 
mediate uses in its connection; is conditioned, in other 
words, by the availability of free capital. 

The nature of capital as an economic category has been 
somewhat variously conceived and far more variously articu- 
lated; but the purposes of the present study do not require an 
extended examination of the theoretical content of this con- 
cept. In accordance with a prevailing view, it is sufficient to 
say that capital in general is best conceived as consisting of 
economic resources devoted to mediate or instrumental uses, 
as contrasted with resources employed for current consump- 
tion; and that, for some purposes, capital may be regarded as 


a fund of economic goods permanently dedicated to mediate 
employment. 


AUTHOR’S PREFACE xi 


The availability of capital for new or expanding industries 
may be said to depend, in general, upon the rate at which the 
supply of capital is increased and upon the efficiency of the 
institutions and practices that bring together owners of 
capital and entrepreneurs. These broad conditions depend 
in turn upon many other factors, some of which are fre- 
quently ignored. Many writers, for example, are content to 
discuss the processes of capital mobility solely in terms of the 
liquid funds marshalled and directed by the organized 
financial markets and by business men. In so doing, they 
suggest a simplicity and directness that are not easily ob- 
servable in the facts of industry. Capital in the form of 
money is, indeed, capital par excellence, for in this form it is 
synonymous with the power to employ virtually all kinds of 
economic resources in ‘roundabout,’ ‘time-consuming’ 
methods. At any given moment, however, the bulk of a com- 
munity’s capital is not to be found in the form of money, but 
in the form of concrete capital goods. How capital so em- 
bodied is constantly redistributed to meet the changing needs 
of industry, and the factors that variously influence this 
mobility, constitutes a set of processes that transcend the 
operations of the money markets. 

The purpose of the present study is to illustrate several 
aspects of these processes by reference to the history of the 
American automobile industry. Stated more broadly, it is 
purposed to answer, for a single industry and within re- 
stricted scope, the following question: Given the existing 
organization of industry, and given a new important product, 
just how have the producers of this product come to com- 
mand the economic resources required for its extensive pro- 
duction? z 

In confining our attention to the American automobile 
industry, we do not presume that its development has been 
perfectly typical of that of all new industries. The auto- 
mobile industry, nevertheless, lends itself peculiarly well to 
the purposes of this study. In the first place, the industry is 
scarcely thirty years old. Its birth and development, there- 


xii AUTHOR’S PREFACE 


fore, have taken place under the most modern conditions of 
financial and industrial practice. In the second place, the 
industry has attained a position of great importance. Where- 
as, prior to 1899, its operations were too insignificant to re- 
ceive separate notice in the Census of Manufactures, in 1925 
it ranked first in value of products among all American manu- 
facturing industries. Finally, unlike the characteristic financ- 
ing of other large-scale developments of recent years, such as 
public utility enterprises, the growth of the American auto- 
mobile industry has taken place without great reliance upon 
capital derived from the public sale of securities. A study of 
the means whereby its expanding capital requirements have 
been met should direct attention, therefore, to the less overt 
and less recognized processes of capital mobility. 

Our attention will be limited largely to a consideration of 
the sources and growth of the capital employed by eight of 
the leading producers of automobiles in the United States. 
Little attempt will be made to examine the sources of the 
purchasing power and capital of consumers of automobiles; 
or of the capital employed by producers of raw materials and 
finished components purchased by automobile manufactur- 
ers; or of the capital employed by selling and distributing 
companies, gasoline and oil companies, garages, and other 
lines of enterprise ancillary or incidental to the production 
and use of motor vehicles. In thus delimiting the scope of our 
study, we nevertheless leave for examination a very large 
industry, the rapid provisioning of the capital of which should 
offer abundant empirical illustration of the chief ways in 
which a growing industry may finance its expansion. 

» In prosecuting the present investigation, I have been 
forced to turn most largely to primary sources: there is a 
marked paucity of published data of comprehensive scope 
dealing with the automobile industry; and only in recent 
years, with the growth of widespread ownership of the se- 
curities of automobile companies, have financial and operat- 
ing data been made at all accessible. As against the difficul- 
ties of investigation, on the other hand, I have experienced 


AUTHOR’S PREFACE xiii 


the most generous treatment at the hands of the leading 
officials of the companies included in this study. It is due to 
their aid in allowing me access to various original or official 
records that this study has achieved such measure of, ade- 
quacy as it may be judged to have attained. Special mention 
is due to Messrs. Pierre S. Du Pont, Meyer L. Prentis, 
Thomas S. Merrill, Frank F. Kolbe, A. B. C. Hardy, A. P. 
Warriner, and Howard Chapin, of the General Motors 
Corporation; W. H. Alford and the late James J. Storrow, of 
the Nash Motors Company; Roy D. Chapin, A. Barit, and 
E. W. Sheldrick, of the Hudson Motor Car Company; Alvan 
Macauley, M. A. Cudlip, and A. C. Bennett, of the Packard 
Motor Car Company; and Ransom E. Olds and George L. 
Brown, of the Reo Motor Car Company, among others, for 
such courtesies. Messrs. Norman G. Shidle and K. W. Still- 
man, editors of ‘Automotive Industries’; O.  P. Pearson, 
statistician of the National Automobile Chamber of Com- 
merce; Senator James Couzens, formerly vice-president of 
the Ford Motor Company; and the Detroit and Washington 
representatives of the Automotive Division of the Bureau of 
Foreign and Domestic Commerce, have given me valuable 
assistance. Specific acknowledgment of the aid of these and 
other individuals, as of published sources, is made in the foot- . 
notes wherever possible. 

The original draft of this essay was accepted as a doctoral 
dissertation at the University of Michigan in 1925. The 
subject had been suggested by Professor David Friday, and 
the study was worked out under the direction of Professors 
E. E. Day, Fred M. Taylor, Charles H. Cooley, I. L. Sharf- 
man, and R. L. Masson. The aid of these and other teachers 
and colleagues at Michigan is gratefully acknowledged. Since 
its submission in the Hart, Schaffner & Marx competition in 
1926, the study has been expanded and rewritten under the 
editorial supervision of Professor Sharfman, who has thus 
added to my numerous obligations to him. I am indebted to 
Mr. Frank F. Kolbe, assistant treasurer of the General 
Motors Corporation, for his critical reading of the original

manuscript, and tomy colleague, Professor Chester F. Kuhn, 
for reading the proof of the present essay. 


LAWRENCE H. SELTZER 


Detroit, MICHIGAN

A Financial History 
of the 
American Automobile Industry 


CHAPTER I 
INTRODUCTORY 


THE swift rise of the motor vehicle from a crude and ex- 
pensive toy to an important element in the everyday living 
of millions of persons is a striking chapter in recent indus- 
trial history. Little more than twenty years ago the ap- 
pearance of an automobile on a city street was an odd occur- 
rence, arousing much the same amused curiosity as at- 
tends a circus marvel. ‘Get a horse!’ was the cry directed at 
the driver; and it was as a freakish ‘horseless carriage’ that 
the automobile first became known. Few there were who 
regarded it as anything more than a new plaything of the 
wealthy. 

Swifter than horse or bicycle, the automobile became the 
center of a new racing sport. Speed, endurance, and various 
mechanical tests, conducted with great newspaper publicity, 
aroused widespread interest. Demand for the vehicles in- 
creased. Numerous small producers were soon recruited 
from many quarters. Then, with great suddenness, the 
automobile was transformed from an unimportant luxury 
into an instrument of rapidly expanding use. Stimulated by 
the appearance of efficient low-priced vehicles, after 1908, 
demand grew apace; production quickly followed. As 
against an output of 65,000 motor vehicles in 1908, that of 


4 INTRODUCTORY 


1914 exceeded half a million; that of 1916 was more than 
1,600,000. Despite many confident predictions to the con- 
trary, the industry continued, with only brief recessions, its 
remarkable growth. By 1922, its output had passed 2,500,- 
000; and in the four years ended in 1926, American motor 
vehicle production aggregated 16,427,549 cars and trucks 
valued at wholesale at more than $11,000,000,000. On 
January I, 1927, there were more than 22,000,000 motor 
vehicles registered in the United States." 

To-day, the purchase and operation of motor vehicles 
absorbs perhaps one-tenth or more of the total annual in- 
come of the American people. Our retail purchases of pas- 
senger cars and trucks amounted to about $4,000,000,000 in 
1926, as in 1925; and our aggregate expenditures for gasoline 
and oil, tire and parts replacements, repair services, garage 
rentals, license fees and taxes, and other items incidental 
to the use of motor vehicles have been estimated at more 
than another $4,000,000,000 annually.? It is interesting to 
observe, by way of comparison, that our annual expendi- 
tures for railroad transportation have been estimated at 
$5,500,000,000; for shelter, $4,500,000,000; for bread and 
foodstuffs, $2,400,000,000; and for fuel and light, at $2,900,- 
000,000.3 

Motor-vehicle manufacture outranks all other American 
manufacturing industries in wholesale value of product.‘ 
Automobiles rank first in value among American exports of 
manufactured goods, and are preceded only by raw cotton 


*See Facts and Figures of the Automobile Industry, 1927, National 
Automobile Chamber of Commerce. 

 Ibid.; cf. also Moody's Industrials, 1926, p. xxxiv (which estimates 
American automobile expenditures at eleven ‘billions annually); C. C. 
Hanch, The Safety Zone in Automobile Financing, published by the 
National Association of Finance Companies; J. C. Long, Motor Trans- 
port and Our Radical Frontier, in the Journal of Public Utility and Land 
Economics, January, 1926; Automotive Industries, vol. 56, no. 7, Pp. 219; 
Caldwell & Slosson, Science Remaking the Modern World, p. 30. 

3 Moody's Industrials, 1927, p. xxxiv. 

4 Census of Manufactures, 1925. 


INTRODUCTORY 5 


and mineral oils, in value, among all the commodity exports 
of the United States.t American car and truck factories 
represented a capital investment of $2,000,000,000 in 1926; 
they employed more than 375,000 workers; and, directly 
and indirectly, the automobile industry as a whole was 
estimated to employ more than 3,743,000 workers.? The 
important relation of automobile manufacture to other 
American industries is suggested by the proportions of their 
total output absorbed by automobile production in 1926: 
iron and steel, 14 per cent; plate glass, 50 per cent; rubber, 
85 per cent; upholstery leather, 63 per cent; nickel, 28 per 
cent; aluminum, 25 per cent; tin, 21 per cent; copper, 13 
per cent; hardwood, 11 per cent.3 Nearly 900,000 carloads 
were required to transport automobile parts and completed 
vehicles on American railroads during 1926.4 

Indicative of the enormous financial importance of the 
industry, apart from the data already cited, we may note 
that $4,000,000,000 of credit was required in 1925 to finance 
wholesale and retail purchases of finished vehicles alone; 5 
that the securities of automobile companies listed on the 
New York Stock Exchange appreciated more than a billion 
dollars in market value during 1925;° that the net profits of 
the General Motors Corporation in 1926 (more than $186,- 
000,000) exceeded those of any other American business 
enterprise; and that a firm of investment bankers, Horn- 
blewer & Weeks, offered a billion dollars in 1925, and again 
in 1926, for the capital stock of the Ford Motor Company.’ 
In the opinion of Messrs. Foster and Catchings, the growth 
of the automobile industry has been the greatest single

cause of American prosperity during the past fifteen 
years." 

The phenomenal expansion of the automobile industry is 
less surprising when we consider that, more than an ordinary 
addition to our stock of useful contrivances, the motor 
vehicle has provided a new, flexible, and widely serviceable 
means of transportation. The routes and means of travel 
have always been factors of supreme economic and social 
significance; and they have always claimed a substantial 
proportion of economic effort. Waterways are of limited and 
uneven distribution; railed transportation is restricted by its 
rigid routes and by its great capital costs; the common 
roads, until the advent of the automobile, did not afford 
rapidity of locomotion. The motor vehicle, swift, flexible, 
and relatively low in capital cost, accompanied, as it has 
been, by a tremendous extension and improvement of the 
common roads, supplements all other forms of transporta- 
tion with extraordinary efficiency, and supplies many special 
types of transportation service hitherto largely unavail- 
able. 

The private passenger automobile is, in effect, an enor- 
mously useful tool for personal locomotion. Unlike the rail- 
road and the trolley, it offers flexible response to individual 
requirements; in contrast with horse-drawn vehicles, it 
provides much of the speed and ease of railed transportation. 
Employed for business or pleasure, it economizes time and 
energy and effectively reduces distance. There is no way of 
measuring accurately how far it pays for itself by enhancing 
productive efficiency alone, though there is little doubt that 
it has contributed greatly to recent increases in urban and 
suburban real estate values.? In any event, the value placed 
upon its service is best attested by actual expenditures: the 
wholesale value of the American passenger car output dur-

ing the ten years ended in 1926 aggregated 17.3 billions of 
dollars; and passenger cars constituted 89 per cent of the 
total American motor-vehicle output during this period.? 
The motor vehicle’s combination of speed, ease, and 
flexibility makes it capable of rendering distinctive services 
in connection with freight haulage and public transportation. 
The motor truck has all but entirely supplanted the horse- 
drawn vehicle for the delivery of goods from store, ware- 
house, factory, and ship and rail terminal. In nearly all 
large cities motor omnibuses are ‘feeding’ and otherwise 
supplementing electric traction service; in many smaller 
cities, local street-car service has been entirely replaced by 
motor-bus lines; and many communities hitherto unserved 
by regular transportation facilities now possess organized 
motor service. Both competing with and supplementing 
railroad service in interurban transportation, the motor 
vehicle possesses the advantages of low capital costs, low 
overhead expenses, flexible routes, elimination of terminal 
reloading, frequent stops, relative economy in less-than- 
carload lots, etc. Requiring only common roads, the con- 
struction and improvement of which have assumed the 
proportions of a major industry in the United States,’ 
motor-vehicle freight and passenger service may be quickly 
introduced and as quickly shifted, extended, or withdrawn. 
Indeed, a growing use of the motor vehicle is that of replac- 
ing ‘short lines’ of railroads, many of which have long been 
overburdened with high overhead costs, and of creating 
‘feeders’ for their main lines. In 1926, sixty steam railroads 
were employing motor busses to supplement their passenger 
service, sixty-one were using motor trucks for terminal 
operations and for store door delivery, and eleven were 
employing motor trucks to replace local freight trains. So

extensive have the routes of motor transportation lines be- 
come that there is now scarcely a point on a paved road in 
the United States at which motor transportation cannot be 
had to near and far centers; and several measures are now 
before Congress providing for the regulation of interstate 
motor transportation. 

Other new or more specialized transportation and power 
services have been made available by various applications 
of the internal combustion motor. The potentialities of the 
aeroplane are daily becoming matters of less distant signi- 
ficance. The automotive farm tractor is an outstanding 
addition to the newer stock of agricultural aids. Motor- 
truck transport, ambulances, and armored tanks, were of 
great importance during the World War. The construction 
industries have combined the motor truck with various 
hoisting, hauling, and dumping devices. The taxicab has 
become a ubiquitous servant in our cities. Even gasoline- 
driven locomotives have in some places been introduced. 

As has been the case with other great inventions, the 
social and economic effects of the motor vehicle transcend 
its immediate services. The isolated American farm of the 
past is giving way to organized rural communities, built 
about the consolidated rural school, the rural social center, 
and the country church.? The motor truck has increased the 
number of marketing centers open to the individual farmer, 
has made distant farms more accessible to markets, and has 
increased the radius of profitable direct marketing in the 
vicinity of cities. Between 1918 and 1926, the number of 
horses employed on American farms declined by approxi- 
mately forty per cent, reflecting in part the increased use of

motor truck and tractor.? As this tendency continues, thous- 
ands of acres of land now used for raising hay and oats will 
be released for other crops, and the quantity and irksome- 
ness of human labor on farms will be greatly reduced.? The 
motor vehicle has encouraged enlargement of the physical 
area of our citieS and a multiplication of the number of resi- 
dential suburbs, bringing thousands of acres of land into 
more valuable employment, and effecting great improve- 
ment in housing and working conditions. An important 
adjunct to the means of recreation, the automobile is rapidly 
abridging the social as well as the physical gap between 
country and city. In breaking up many neighborhood 
groups and in overcoming distance, the automobile has 
possibly caused some weakening in the sense of local unity 
_and responsibility. The widespread use of the motor vehicle 
has been accompanied by a general social atmosphere of 
haste and change: many elements contribute to this vague 
but pervasive tenor of modern life; the automobile is no 
doubt one of these. 

The very processes of automobile manufacture in the 
United States have been of widespread influence. In no 
other industry have mass production, standardization of 
product, minute specialization of labor, the automatic 
machine-tool, and clock-like organization of factory opera- 
tions, been employed to the same extent. The physical and 
psychological effects of these methods upon the workers 
have been the subject of much debate; but their immediate 
industrial superiority is causing their extension to many 
other fields. 

The automobile has raised new problems. The congestion 
of traffic in our larger cities and the enormous loss of life 
and limb in automobile accidents are causing great sums to 
be spent for traffic regulation, street-widening, and radical

readjtistment of city-plans. Our expenditures for rural high-

to less than $25,000,000 in 1914, are now absorbing a billion 
dollars annually. The perpetration of crime, no less than the 
performance of reputable tasks, is facilitated by the motor 
vehicle. To the importance of the automobile, is largely due 
the prevailing intense international rivalry for the world’s 
oil and rubber resources. 

It is beyond the scope of this study to Pare more than 
passing attention to some of the ulterior effects of the 
motor vehicle. It is sufficient here to observe that a product 
that has supplied a new and powerful means of transporta- 
tion, and that, directly and indirectly, absorbs perhaps 
one-tenth of our total national income and the energies of 
nearly one-tenth of our working population, cannot help 
but exercise a profound influence in many spheres. 

The economic development of the American automobile 
industry reflects many of the best aspects of competitive 
enterprise. Enormous profits (sometimes large losses) have 
gone hand in hand with falling prices and improved pro- 
ducts. Competition has enforced the prompt adoption of 
progressive advances in design and manufacture. The 
technical efficiency of American automobile production has 
established standards of practice and achievement that 
have aroused widespread admiration and emulation.' The 
less desirable effects of quasi-autocratic control over labor 
have been mitigated in this industry by relatively high 
wages and by intelligently planned working conditions. The 
corporate form of business organization, and, as we shall 
find, the existence of numerous independent contributory 
enterprises, have facilitated the distribution of capital and 
risk burdens; while, despite the impersonal form of corporate 
organization, the potent motive forces of personality have 
been able to play conspicuous réles.2, Due to the remarkable

economies of large-scale operations, the bulk of American 
automobile production has been concentrated into relatively 
few hands. Among these, there has been considerable co- 
operation, such as intercorporate standardization of parts, 
pooling of patents, and interchange of business information. 
Competition, nevertheless, has become ever more intense. 
A study of the development of this industry, therefore, 
interesting in itself, should illustrate many of the essential 
processes and tendencies of competitive enterprise. If, 
as may be alleged, the virtues of competitive enterprise in 
the automobile industry are to be attributed to the develop- 
mental character of the latter up until the present time, 
while the near future may witness a gathering tendency to- 
ward monopolistic control, then, as much as ever, the history 
of the industry is of substantial significance to the student 
of our economic institutions. 

The growth of the automobile industry has required 
enormous quantities of productive resources: plant and 
equipment for manufacturing, raw and refined materials, 
labor, and a willingness and ability to assume risks. How 

were these requirements met? How was the growth of the 
industry financed? It is the primary purpose of this book 
to throw some light on this question. Under the existing 
organization of industry, there is no man or group of men, 
no formal institution, with power to allot to a new product 
the productive resources that it requires. The new product 
- must fight for its resources. It must divert old and new pro- 
‘ductive factors from other channels. Its weapons are higher 
capital returns, higher wages, and higher prices for materi- 
als. It must likewise divert old and new purchasing power 
from other goods. Now finance is essentially the pecuniary 
aspect of the process of mobilizing economic resources for 
the various products and processes of economic life. In 
treating of the financial history of the American automobile 
industry, therefore, we are attempting to discover just how 
material resources came to be provided for its growth. 
Specific knowledge of this sort is sadly wanting. To know

just how profits operate, and just how capital is mobilized 
and distributed for various industries, gives us an under- 
standing of economic matters that illumines many questions 
of public policy.

CHAPTER II 
THE DEVELOPMENT OF THE AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


1. INVENTION 


LIKE other complex artifacts, the automobile was evolved 
rather than invented. The efforts of the earliest ‘inventors’ 
of the motor vehicle flowed from a long succession of dis- 


coveries and innovations; and the modern product rests no — 
less upon the developed arts of manufacture than upon the | 


achievements of the numerous inventors who have contrib- 
uted directly to its design. The automobile is the product of 
the labors and learning of many men, directed at diverse 
objects, and pooled through the channels of communi- 
cation. 

Wind-driven or ‘sailing’ carriages are said to have been 


employed ‘from immemorial times on the plains of China — 
and Spain.’* Mechanical road transport may be said to have © 


started its present development in the eighteenth century, 


following the improvements of Watt and others in the sta-— 


tionary steam engine; and the steam-driven road vehicle 
naturally preceded the steam railroad. In 1760, Captain 
Joseph Nicholas Cugnot, a French military engineer, con- 
structed a steam-driven wagon designed to haul heavy can- 
non — probably the first self-propelled road vehicle ever 
built; ? in 1787, Oliver Evans, of Philadelphia, obtained a 
patent from the State of Maryland for a steam-propelled 


carriage; in 1801, Richard Trevithick, in England, con-— 


structed the first steam-driven stage-coach. Preceding and 


* Attributed to Bishop Wilkins, writing in 1648, by Talbot, Motor 
Cars and Their Story, p. 3, Cassell & Co., Ltd. 

* For a fairly non-technical account of the development of steam and 
gasoline vehicles, see Doolittle, Romance of the Automobile Industry; 
Talbot, Motor Cars and Their Story; Jenkins, Motor Cars; and Souvestre, 
Histoire de l’automobile; from all of which much of the present account is 
abstracted. 


— 


DEVELOPMENT OF THE AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 15 


accompanying the development of the railroad, a number of 
steam-driven omnibuses began to offer regular transporta- 
tion in England; and by 1836, a single such enterprise, oper- 
ated by Walter Hancock, was transporting 12,000 passen- 
gers a year. 

The early steam-driven road vehicles rapidly gave way to 
the railroad, however. Their fuel and water requirements 
made them of enormous weight (three to four tons un- 
loaded) ; their huge metal-rimmed wheels (six feet in diam- 
eter) operated on the uneven dirt roads of the time; they 
were constructed of wood and of crudely made metal; and 
they did not have the benefit of modern lubricants nor of 
modern spring suspension. The excessive vibration and 
friction caused the vehicles to deteriorate rapidly; they did 
great damage to the roads; and they caused fright and an- 
noyance to horses and populace. As a result of 'the latter 
factors, and of the bitter opposition of horse-haulage inter- 
ests, toll-gate keepers in England imposed prohibitive fees 
for the passage of the vehicles over turnpikes and bridges. 
Despite a favorable report of its Select Committee, Parlia- 
ment refused in 1833 to intercede in behalf of the operators 
of steam carriages; and in 1861 and 1865, a series of strin- 
gent restrictions were enacted which, among other things, 
stipulated that every mechanically propelled vehicle oper- 
ating on the common roads carry three drivers, limit its 
speed to four miles an hour, and be preceded by a man bear- 
ing a red flag.t These measures, which did much to retard 
English motor-vehicle experimentation, were not removed 
until 1896.2 In America, as in England and France, the rail- 
road absorbed most of the energies of creative workers in the 
field of land transportation for many years. 

The discovery of large quantities of petroleum in the 
United States, coupled with advances in distillation methods 
that brought kerosene and gasoline into wide use after the 


t See Rolls, Encyclopedia Britannica, vol. xvii, p. 914 ff. 
‘2 Ibid. They had been held, in 1881, to apply to gasoline, as well as 
to steam vehicles 


“16 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


middle of the nineteenth century, opened up new possibil- 
ities for self-propelled road vehicles. Because of their great 
energy per pound of weight, the new liquid fuels were infi- 
nitely superior to coal and wood for the generation of power 
on light vehicles. Almost simultaneous with the appearance 
of improved steam carriages burning kerosene’was the ap- 
plication of internal combustion hydro-carbon motors, first 
used for stationary power purposes, to the road vehicle. 
Siegfried Markus, whose gasoline-propelled carriage was 
displayed at the Vienna Exhibition of 1875, is said to have 
produced his first model in 1865; Lenoir, in 1863, had con- 
structed a vehicle employing street gas; hence Markus has 
been designated by the Austrian and German automobile 
clubs as ‘the inventor of the automobile,’ while a French 
commission has claimed this distinction for Lenoir.? 

Lenoir had used an electric spark to ignite the gas in his 
engine. In 1876, Otto, in Germany, invented an engine in 
which the gas was compressed before ignition, vastly in- 
creasing its propulsive power. In 1885, Gottlieb Daimler, 
an employee of Otto, and, in 1886, Carl Benz, patented 
motor vehicles of greatly improved design. In 1887, Le- 
vassor, of the French firm of Panhard & Levassor, who had 
acquired rights to the Daimler patents, introduced so many 
improvements in general chassis construction and in power 
transmission, that he, too, like Markus, Lenoir, Daimler, 
Benz, Otto, and others, has been called ‘the father of the 
automobile.’ ? 

The present-day motor vehicle is the product of far more 
than the labors of these early inventors. Without the rubber 
tire, to take a single example, the automobile, however 
otherwise improved, was subject to enormous traction diffi- 
culties; difficulties which did much to transform the early 
steam Carriages into locomotives and trains designed for use 
on prepared rails. The character and importance of the 
modern automobile rest upon literally thousands of later ad- 

* Talbot, Motor Cars and Their Story. Introduction. 

* Cf. Doolittle, Jenkins, Talbot, and Souvestre, op. cit, 


ee 


DEVELOPMENT OF THE AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 17 


vances in design and construction, and upon great improve- 
ments in the arts of production. 


2. AMERICAN BEGINNINGS 


The United States lagged behind the Old World, at first, 
in the technical development and commercial production of 
the motor vehicle. Stationary gasoline motors had been in- 
troduced into this country soon after their appearance 
abroad; and on May 8, 1879, George B. Selden, of Roches- 
ter, New York, had filed application for the first American 
patent on the hydro-carbon motor vehicle. But Selden 
waited many years before constructing a vehicle based upon 
his patent model, and during most of this time American 
invention made little progress. When, in 1895, the Selden 
‘patent was finally issued, the inventor was unable to procure 
financial support for the exploitation of his patent.t Other 
American inventors, however, building largely upon Euro- 
pean practice, had now entered the field. Charles E. Duryea 
produced a gasoline automobile in 1892; Henry Ford and 
Ransom E. Olds each completed one in 1893; Elwood 
Haynes and the Apperson brothers in 1894, and a dozen 
other individuals and groups produced experimental models 
or began commercial manufacture before the end of the 
century. 

The ascendency of the gasoline automobile was threatened, 
for a few years, by the improved kerosene-burning steam 
vehicle and by the lately invented electric carriage,? pro- 
duction of both of which had been-undertaken by a num- 
ber of bicycle, wagon, and machinery manufacturers. The 
steam car was far less noisy than the early one- and two- 
cylinder gasoline vehicles; and the promise of the electric 
vehicle was furthered by the large development, at this 
time, of electric traction and lighting systems and by wide- 
spread rumors of startling improvements in electrical ap- 


* Doolittle, p 
- 2 Produced 7 pik (1884), Ward (1886), Volk svat Morrison 
(1891), and others. 


18 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


paratus of all kinds.* In 1900, at the first American auto- 
mobile show, nearly two-thirds of the show space was de- 
voted to steam cars, and most of the remaining space, to 
electrics. Almost immediately thereafter, however, the gas- 
oline automobile eclipsed its rivals. As against the steam 
car, it offered greater freedom from mechanical care and 
readier starting; as against the electric, it offered greater 
distance before refueling and greater power and endurance 
— important factors in view of American road conditions 
and distances. By 1904, American production of gasoline 
automobiles was more than six times the combined output 
of steam and electric vehicles.” 

While the earliest American automobile producers were 
being torn between the steam, electric, and gasoline vehi- 
cles, France, concentrating largely upon the gasoline auto- 
mobile, had taken the lead in the new industry.3 Imports of 
European automobiles into the United States began in 1895, 
and served to stimulate American activity. But the provi- 
sion of a 45 per cent ad-valorem duty on such importations 
(under the heading of ‘manufactured metal’ in the Dingley 
tariff), combined with the rapid growth of the domestic in- 
dustry, enabled American producers virtually to monopo- 
lize the domestic market almost from the start. By 1907, 
the value of American automobile exports was exceeding 
that of imports; and in the years following, exports in- 
creased progressively, while imports soon dwindled into in- 
significance.4 

The automobile was at first regarded as an interesting

novelty — it did, indeed, serve as a circus attraction in 1896. 
Then, following the widespread development of automobile 
racing, which brought the vehicle into great public promi- 
nence, its practical value was established by numerous .dis- 
tance and endurance contests; contests which educated the 
producers no less than potential consumers. More than any 
of these factors, however, the production of low-priced auto- 
mobiles, which had begun as early as 1900,% served to stimu- 
late public interest in the new product and to spur demand 
and production. 


3. EARLY SOURCES OF CAPITAL 


There was no lack of initiative even at the very beginning 
of American automobile manufacture. By 1899, fifty-seven 
producing establishments were already in operation, and by 
1904, this number had increased to 121.7, Few of the pro- 
ducers were endowed with more than meager resources; but 
both technological and financial factors made entrance into 
the business extremely easy, and expansion of output did not 
wait upon the direct commitment of large amounts of capital. 
Indeed, as will be shown in detail in subsequent pages, the 
growth of the American automobile industry was character- 
istically the expansion of originally small enterprises that 
obtained the bulk of their increasing capital from reinvested 
profits. 

The early. producers met the capital requirements of auto- 
mobile production, in largest measure, by shifting the burden 
to owners of already existing capital equipment. The auto- 
mobile was a new product chiefly as an assembled unit: its 
component parts were much akin to those of a number of 
other products. As a result of the previous development 
in the United States of the technique of standardized in- 
terchangeable parts manufacture — a technique which had 


* The Olds Motor Works produced 1400 cars selling at $650 each in 
1900. (From data obtained by the writer from Mr. A. B. C. Hardy, 
president of the existing company in 1924, in an interview in Lansing, 
Michigan, on December 24, 1924.) 


2 Census of Manufactures, 1921. . 


20 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


been extensively employed by the sewing machine, bicycle, 
wagon, and other American industries — numerous estab- 
lishments were in existence equipped with the tools, ma- 
chines, and technical skill for the manufacture of wood and 
metal sub-products.t Specialized automobile factories were 
therefore not essential. Orders for parts were given to scores 
of wood- and metal-working enterprises; carriage-makers 
were available for the manufacture of ‘bodies’; and the pro- 
ducers of rubber goods and electrical equipment soon added 
their contributions. The chief business of the automobile 
producer, after the design of his product and the placing of 
orders for parts, was the assembling of the major components 
and the sale of the completed vehicle. The process of as- 
sembling was a short one; it required neither large plant nor 
elaborate equipment; and lands and buildings were as often 
rented as purchased. 

Thus, the bulk of the fixed capital required for the produc- 
tion of automobiles was not provided by the producers them- 
selves, but by the parts-makers; and the provision of this 
capital did not wait upon the willingness of its owners to 
assume the new and large risks of automobile production 
proper. In part, the automobile diverted capital from other 
goods; to some extent, however, the needs of the new pro- 
duct were met by a more efficient utilization of capital: idle | 
utilities residing in the equipment of machine-shops and 
other enterprises were brought into use through additional 
orders coming from the new industry. 

The working capital burdens of automobile manufacture 
were similarly shifted and distributed. The practice of pur- 
chasing parts made to order minimized the current financial 
requirements for wages. The process of assembling was not 
only short, but was often shorter than the credit period of 
thirty to ninety days allowed by the parts-makers. And the 
demand for automobiles was such that, in exchange for 


* See W. L. Thorp, Integration of Industrial Operation, Census Mono- 
graph no. 3, p. 86 ff., Government Printing Office, in this connection, 
for the example offered by the carriage and wagon industry. 


DEVELOPMENT OF THE AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 21 


exclusive territorial rights for dealers and distributors, the 
automobile producers were able (1) to exact advance cash 
deposits of twenty per cent or more upon all orders; (2) to 
require full payment immediately upon delivery of the com- 
pleted vehicle (sight draft being attached to the bill of lad- 
ing); and (3) to ship cars immediately upon production, ir- 
respective of current retail sales, according to a prearranged 
schedule with each selling establishment. Thus, Senator 
James Couzens, who was an active officer of the Ford Motor 
Company from its inception in 1903 until 1919, replying to 
some questions of the present writer, declared: ! 

I recall that by a rule of the industry generally deposits from 
dealers were a requisite to their securing cars and this in itself ac- 
cumulated quite a fund which could be used in manufacture. Of 


course, parts manufacturers extended credit and were pay in ac- 
cordance with the usual terms in the industry. 


Similarly, Mr. Roy D. Chapin, chairman of the board of 
directors of the Hudson Motor Car Company, and one of the 
pioneers of the industry, explained in substantially the fol- 
lowing language the conditions under which the industry 
operated as late as 1909, when he, with a few others, founded 
the Hudson company:? 

It is said that the Ford Motor Co. started with $28,000. We 
started with much less than that... . 

Dealers’ deposits often paid half the sum necessary to bring out a 
full year’s production; and if the assembling were efficiently directed, 
drafts against the finished cars could be cashed as rapidly as the bills 
from parts-makers came in. 

We sold directly to Ameeitore from ‘ie first. We looked for in- 
dividuals of substantial capital and financial responsibility who 
would be sure to fulfill their engagements. 


4. ORIGINS OF SOME EARLY LEADERS 


The remarkably favorable factors just outlined, combined 
with the corporate form of business organization, which per- 
* In a letter dated February 2, 1924. 


2In an interview with the writer in Detroit, December 11, 1924, 
The quotation was transcribed from notes taken during the interview. 


22 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


mitted the limited liability of stockholders and the easy 
division of the residual capital and risk burdens, enabled 
scores of small enterprises to enter upon automobile produc- 
tion. Such entrance was stimulated by the rapid growth in 
demand and by the large profits of successful production. 
Some of the automobile inventors, sponsored by small 
groups or financed by local subscription to capital stock, 
quickly entered the field; a number of manufacturers of 
bicycles, wagons, and carriages ventured timidly into the 
business by turning over portions of their facilities to the 
automobile; machine-shop enterprises, after a more or less 
brief experience with parts manufacture, undertook auto- 
mobile production proper; associates and employees of suc- 
cessful producers broke away to found independent com- 
panies. 

In 1899, Samuel L. Smith, a Detroit capitalist who had 
grown wealthy in the lumber industry, was seeking openings 
for his two sons. His attention was drawn to the efforts of 
Ransom E. Olds, of Lansing, Michigan, to obtain support for 
the exploitation of his one-cylinder ‘Oldsmobile.’ With 
Smith’s aid, the Olds Motor Works was thereupon started in 
Detroit.t When, because of disagreement with the Smiths, 
Olds retired from the Company in 1905, some citizens of 
Lansing organized the Reo Motor Car Company, offered 
Olds a controlling interest for his services, and thus induced 
him to lead another automobile manufacturing enterprise.? 

Henry Ford was financed by a small group of Detroiters in 
1902 under the name of the Detroit Automobile Company. 
Disagreeing with his associates, he joined another group for 
a short time, organized as the Henry Ford Automobile Com- 
pany. Unsatisfied still, he succeeded in enlisting the aid of 
Alexander Y. Malcomson, a Detroit coal merchant, and was 
thus enabled to form the Ford Motor Company in June, 
1903, with a total cash capital of $28,000.3 


t From data obtained from Mr. Olds in an interview with the writer in 
Lansing on December 24, 1924. 


2 Ibid, 3 See Chapter III, below. 


DEVELOPMENT OF THE AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 23 


William E. Metzger, a Detroit bicycle dealer, and others 
of the group that had originally financed Ford, induced the 
Leland & Faulconer Machine Company, which was making 
parts for various producers, to join them in a new enterprise, 
the Cadillac Motor Car Company, which proceeded to manu- 
facture a car designed by Alanson P. Brush, one of the Le- 
land & Faulconer employees. Brush later left the Company 
to start another automobile enterprise under his own name. 

The Buick Motor Car Company was formed in 1903 by 
Benjamin Briscoe, a parts-maker, who had acquired rights 
to a model designed by David Buick. A year later, the Com- 
pany was taken over by a Flint, Michigan, wagon enter- 
prise. The year following, it came into control of William C. 
Durant, then a Flint carriage manufacturer, who, a few 
years later, made it the nucleus of the General Motors Com- 
pany. : 

Briscoe, in the meantime, became interested in another 
model, designed by J. D. Maxwell, one of the dozen promi- 
nent graduates of the two Olds enterprises. The Maxwell- 
Briscoe Motor Company was organized in 1904; an invest- 
ment, first of $10,000, and then another of $100,000, was 
procured from the elder J. P. Morgan; after a few years of 
successful operation, this Company was made the nucleus of 
the short-lived United States Motor Company, out of the 
ashes of which came the Maxwell and then the present 
Chrysler companies. 

The Packard was first produced by J. W. Packard, of 
Warren, Ohio, manufacturer of electric cables, in 1900. 
Henry P. Joy, a Detroit capitalist, purchased one of the 
early models, and then persuaded some of his friends to join 
him in acquiring a controlling interest in the business and in 
moving it to Detroit, where it was established in 1903. The 
Studebaker carriage and wagon organization, after first 
choosing the electric vehicle, entered the gasoline field in 
1908 as distributing agent for the Everitt-Metzger-Flanders 


* See Briscoe, ‘The Inside Story of General Motors,’ published in the 
Detroit Saturday Night of January 15, 22, 29, and February 5, 1921. 


24 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


Company, which it absorbed shortly after. The Overland, 
originally the product of an Ohio buggy manufacturer, be- 
came important after 1908, when it was taken over by John 
N. Willys, previously a bicycle and motor-car salesman. 
Four graduates of the Olds enterprises, Roy D. Chapin, 
Howard E. Coffin, R. B. Jackson, and F. O. Bezner, inter- 
ested a Detroit merchant, J. L. Hudson, in a new design in 
1908; with $10,000 of cash so acquired, together with smaller 
amounts otherwise collected, the Hudson Motor Car Com- 
pany was organized a few months later. The blueprints of 
R. C. Hupp, coupled with the modest support of a small 
group of Detroit business men, similarly became the basis of 
the Hupp Motor Car Company. 

Elwood Haynes and the Apperson brothers, sponsored by 
local groups, had undertaken commercial production soon 
after the completion of their original models. The Winton 
(1898), the Pierce-Arrow (1901), the Jeffery, later Nash 
(1903), the Thomas Flyer, and a few others, grew out of 
bicycle-manufacturing enterprises; the Peerless issued from 
the Peerless Wringer Company; the White, first steam and 
then gasoline, from the White Sewing Machine enterprise. 

In ways such as these, then, modestly launched, for the 
most part, did automobile-producing companies spring up. 


5. EARLY FAILURES 


If entrance into the automobile manufacturing business 
was easy, failures, too, were swift and numerous. Even 
before the industry was well started, it had already been 
prolific of misguided promotions and unsuccessful manu- 
facturing ventures. Many of these originated in that heyday 
of financial promotion generally, the few years on either side 
of the turn of the century, when, as in many other industries, 
numerous newly projected enterprises issued large amounts 
of securities based, it would seem, on little that was more 
tangible than a general optimism. The automobile compa- 
nies thus originated quickly became mere names: the Woods 
Motor Vehicle Company ($10,000,000 capitalization); the 


DEVELOPMENT OF THE AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 25 


Automobile Company of America ($5,000,000); the Auto- 
mobile Forecarriage Company ($5,000,000); the Horseless 
Vehicle Company, International Automobile & Vehicle 
Company, Pneumatic Carriage Company, Automatic Air 
Carriage Company, and others.? 

A few large promotions persisted for some years. The 
Electric Vehicle Company, which had acquired ownership 
of the Selden patent in 1899, issued more than $20,000,000 
of securities by 1902; in 1907, it went into receivership, to be 


reorganized in 1909 as the Columbia Motor Car Company, 


with its capitalization scaled down to little more than 
$2,000,000; this company likewise failed, and was finally 


liquidated.? Similarly, the Pope Manufacturing Company, - 


which had ventured into the electric vehicle business from 
that of bicycle manufacture, failed in 1907; failed again 
after a reorganization; and, finally, what was left was ab- 
sorbed by the Willys-Overland Company. Of all the auto- 
mobile manufacturing enterprises sufficiently important to 
be listed in the ‘Manual of Statistics’ for 1902, none survives 
to-day except as a vestigial element incorporated in some 
other enterprise. 

As American production got under way, the large profits 
of successful enterprises were widely advertised, and became 
the basis of extravagant promises on the part of sanguine or 
unscrupulous promoters. Hence, new waves of promotion, 
initiating scores of companies, took place irregularly for 
some years. If the number of all entrants into the industry 
be compared with the number of survivors, the mortality 
rate will be judged to have been high indeed. As against the 
Census figure of 265 automobile producers in 1909, the 
magazine ‘Motor,’ in July, 1909, contained a list of 639 
firms that had ‘engaged’ in the industry up to that time. 
Most of these firms, however, had never attained to the stage 
of commercial manufacture. A recent study indicates that 
seventy-seven enterprises had produced automobiles on a 
commercial scale between 1902 and 1909, inclusive; and that 
_ * Doolittle, op. cit., p. 288 ff. 2 Ibid, 3 Ibid. 


r 


26 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


of these, only eight had retired from business during the 
interval.t In other words, failures, while numerous, were 
largely confined to the promotion stages. 

The successful producers flourished rapidly. Their ex- 
pansion was facilitated by the same fortunate practices that 
encouraged prolific entrance into the industry; and the bulk 
of their large profits was promptly reinvested in the business. 
Increases in their output, stimulated both by the rapidly 
growing general demand and by the cumulative growth in 
the reputations of particular ‘makes,’ did not require im- 
mediate and large additions to their plants nor to their 
working capital. The facilities of parts-makers steadily in- 
creased; competition among distributors and dealers for the 
profitable sales rights permitted the continuance of the 
practices of advance dealer deposits and cash sales; and the 
parts-makers allowed the boon of open accounts. It was in 
this way that the Buick, Cadillac, Ford, Maxwell, Olds, Reo, 
and other producers, each starting with surprisingly small 
resources, grew in size and importance. 

American automobile production, little obstructed by 
limitations of capital, expanded rapidly. The output of 2500 
motor vehicles valued at $4,750,000 in 1899 was increased to 
130,986 vehicles valued at $193,800,000 in 1909; ? and at the 
end of the latter year, the United States was already exceed- 
ing Europe in physical and financial volume of automobile 
production and in total number of automobiles in use.

The growth of the American automobile industry was ac- 
companied by a rising tendency toward localization of manu- 
facture. Charts I and II, drawn from the Census of 1910, 
reflect the early prominence of the States of Michigan, Ohio, 
New York, and Indiana; and Table 17, below, shows the

a surprising number of the pioneer inventors and manu- 
facturers were among their residents: Henry Ford, Ransom 
E. Olds, David Buick, Charles King, Henry M. Leland, and 
others, of Michigan; Alexander Winton, the White family, 
the Peerless group, and others, of Ohio. Stimulating both 
invention and manufacture of motor vehicles in these States 
was the prior and contemporaneous existence of various re- 
lated industries. In Michigan, early iron and copper mining, 
and the State’s great timber resources, among other factors, 
had resulted in the development of stove, brass, shipbuild- 
ing, carriage- and wagon-manufacture, and other wood- and 
metal-working industries, among which was the production 
of gasoline and steam engines for stationary power uses. 
Similar industries, with more emphasis upon steel pro- 
ducts, including, particularly, the bicycle, had developed 
in Ohio. Most of these industries, moreover, were not in- 
tegrated, but comprised large numbers of independent parts- 
making, assembling, and sales enterprises. In consequence 
inventive and manufacturing talent familiar with the ma- 


DEVELOPMENT OF THE AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 29 


chine technique, and factories equipped with the physical 
facilities and traditions of parts manufacture, were readily 
available in both States for automobile production. And 
in Michigan in particular, were found a surprisingly large 
number of men willing to risk capital and energy in the new 
industry. 

More immediately important than any of these influences, 
however, was the fact of the initial successes of a large num- 
ber of Michigan and Ohio automobile producers. While en- 
trants from other States were more numerous in the aggre- 
gate and accounted for the larger part of American automo- 
bile production for a few years, Michigan, conspicuously, pos- 
_ sessed the most successful enterprises: the Buick, Ford, Olds, 
Cadillac, Packard, and other Michigan companies led the 
field as early as 1907. Their success attracted the attention 
of the men of capital and energy about them, and of would- 
be automobile producers located elsewhere. It stimulated the 
local development of specialized contributory and ancillary 
enterprises and of technical talent and skilled labor. Thus, 
the successful producers soon gave to their locations an im- 
mediate and real superiority for automobile manufacture. 
And, since the growth of the leading producers was cumula- 
tive, it was their localities which soon contributed the bulk 
of American automobile output. 

In an attempt to discover specific causes for the early 
localization of American automobile manufacture, the writer 
has interviewed a score or more of individuals who have been 
identified with the industry from its early years; the opinion 
of Mr. Roy D. Chapin reflects that of many others. Mr. 
Chapin declared: ? 


Three men, Henry Ford, Ransom E. Olds, and Charles King were 
responsible for the origin and development of Detroit and the sur- 


*In April, 1926, a subsidiary of the American Car & Foundry Com- 
Jany, with the entire country to choose from, decided to locate its motor 
dus plant in Detroit, far removed from the main plants of the parent 
company. 

* In an interview with the writer in Detroit, Michigan, December II, 
1924. | 


30 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


rounding territory as the center of the automobile industry. The 
cars they made and the companies they established were successful, 
while many of those made in other cities were not. The Detroit suc- 
cesses stimulated more local effort and attracted others from other 
districts. 

The existence of the desired types of skilled labor in this vicinity 
had something, though not much, to do with the Detroit location. 
In fact, we had to import our really skilled labor from the east in the 
early years. 

The banks here played an important part. There was a great deal 
of prejudice in other parts of the country on the part of bankers, par- 
ticularly in the east. They lacked the business sense that was needed. 
The Detroit bankers had it and were not afraid of our sight drafts. 


That the localization of industry is quite commonly deter- 
mined by the adventitious success of some single or small 
group of producers within a restricted district of a much 
larger area of possible location is reflected by the following 
freely arranged excerpt from the Twelfth Census of the 
United States: * 


In most cases it will be found that the original establishment of an 
industry ina locality was largely a matter of chance. . . . Somewhere 
within the possible area .. . an enterprising man started the pioneer 
establishment of a certain industry. . .. His success proves that the 
economic conditions are favorable — that he is within the possible 
area of thatindustry. But it does more, it creates a local bias toward 
this particular industry. This bias affects all three classes necessary 
to its expansion: entrepreneurs, capitalists, and laborers. 


7. THE RIskKs OF AUTOMOBILE PRODUCTION 


Commercial bankers, from the beginning, had viewed the 
automobile industry with great distrust; and its rapid de- 
velopment soon aroused their positive hostility. Writers and . 
lecturers in the banking fraternity cited the growing popu- 
larity of the automobile as an ominous sign of extravagance, 
luxury, and waste; the automobile was pictured as a siren, 
luring men to mortgage their homes, to liquidate their in- 
vestments, to reduce their savings accounts, to divert pur- 
chasing power from all legitimate channels of consumptio 


t Vol. vil, pp. 210-14 (1900). 


DEVELOPMENT OF THE AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 31 


and investment. Depression in the bond market, reces- 
sions in various branches of trade, and other ilis were laid to 
the motor car. 

The hostile conservatism of the bankers, shared, in part, 
by other sections of public opinion, was accentuated by what 
appeared to be chaotic conditions among the automobile 
producers. New promotions, some of them fraudulent and 
most of them unsuccessful, appeared with great frequency. 
Each new company claimed revolutionary improvements in 
design, forcing older producers to make similar claims with 
respect to each succeeding model, and causing a continuous 
state of unsettlement within the industry.?, Large numbers 
of automobile companies were born to but a few short months 
of life, and dozens were swept away in the panic of 1907.% 
The fact that it was possible to undertake and to expand pro- 

duction on a minimum of owned capital encouraged trading 
on the thinnest of equities. In the sense that few automobile 
producers possessed sufficient reserves of working capital to 
tide them over possible periods of diminished sales, the whole 
industry may be said to have been undercapitalized. Rela- 
tively small and temporary difficulties in production or sales 
therefore constituted a serious threat to all producers. 

On the other hand, there was every incentive for success- 
ful companies to expand their volume of business. Profits 
were large, and permitted substantial reinvestment of earn- 
ings. Increased volume of production lowered manufactur- 
‘ing costs by permitting large-scale production of standard- 
ized parts, either by the manufacturer directly or by parts- 
makers working to order. A larger volume of output cumu- 
atively increased the market for any particular company by 
permitting an increase in the number and fuller territorial 

« Cf., for an excellent example, an article by Franklin Escher, ‘The 
Auto and the Bond Market,’ in the Bankers’ Magazine, vol. 81, p. 508 ff. 
(October, 1910). 


2See Briscoe, ‘The Inside Story of General Motors,’ in the Detrott 
Saturday Night of January 15, 22, 29, and February 5, 1921. 


3 Doolittle, op. cit., p. 38; and Dalton, ‘When To-morrow’s Roll is 
Called,’ in Motor, January, 1925, p. 72 ff. 


32 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


distribution of dealers and service agencies, as well as by 
adding to the maker’s reputation. And the technological 
and financial practices prevailing in the industry, to which 
allusion has been made, minimized the capital requirements 
of expanded production. 

Nevertheless, expanded output brought its perils. There 
was great temptation to employ the profits of a successful 
year for fixed plant and equipment, despite the availability 
of the plant facilities of parts-makers, for the latter’s profits 
were large. The demand for automobiles generically was un- 
doubtedly great, but the tentative technical character of the 
product, both real and fancied, caused great fickleness on the 
part of the buying public with respect to particular ‘makes’ ; 
hence the success of any single year’s model of any producer 
was uncertain. Commitments for parts and materials, 
nevertheless, had to be made; a single bad year, therefore, 
might easily be a fatal blow. The situation of the Maxwell- 
Briscoe Company, for example, was thus described by Mr. 
Briscoe: ? 

Our business grew very fast in spite of our lack of good, or even 
fair facilities. ... With me, however, there was always the thought 
that if we should ever experience a ‘silly season,’ should ever drop so 
far behind competition as to make it difficult for us to sell our 


product ‘off the fire,’ we... would find ourselves in a very un- 
fortunate financial position. 


These conditions and others, which led a number of auto- 
mobile producers to attempt a combination as a means of 
pooling the risks of automobile manufacture, were further 
described by Mr. Briscoe as follows: 


In the spring of 1908, the conditions that confronted the auto- 
mobile industry were thought by some of us to besomewhat ominous, 
especially for such concerns as had large fixed investment in plants, 
machinery, tools, etc. Not a few of the concerns of the day were run 
by what might have been called ‘manufacturing gamblers.’ 

In many cases, the management had adopted methods that were 
described as ‘plunging.’ In fact we all plunged, and conditions came 


* “The Inside Story of General Motors,’ in the Detroit Saturday Night of 
January 15, 22, 29, and February 5, 1921. ‘ 
i 


DEVELOPMENT OF THE AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 33 


about which were thought by some of us to predicate disaster. The 
influence of several companies was always exciting and sometimes 
disturbing. The business of even the sanest among the manufactur- 
ers was influenced by them, for they had to submit to be crowded into 
business risks which they would not have entered into had they not 
been fearful that some other concern would gain a few points on 
them.... . 

There was even a greater menace to the industry in the piratical 
attitude of what were called ‘skimmers.’ Concerns which did not 
have a worthy car or any manufacturing ability, but with large 
stock issues to sell, and by ingenious exploitation would succeed 
in stirring up the trade and the public, creating the impression 
that the automobile business was an unstaple one, that the older con- 
cerns were robbers, and that they, through some newly discovered 
combination of geniuses, were enabled to sell gold dollars for fifty 
cents, in automobiles. 

These practices had a considerable effect on the trade, preventing 
a proper and conservative public view of the business, and bringing, 
through misrepresentation, a great deal of discredit upon the in- 
dustry. 

Again there were many parts-makers who, by extending accom- 
modations and by taking long chances with small automobile manu- 
facturers, threatened a demoralization by encouraging into the busi- 
ness uncercapitalized concerns and inexperienced makers, and al- 
though such companies were generally short-lived, this condition 
hurt while it lasted; in any event, many such companies were bound 
to get a large share of the local business. Concerns of this type were 
springing up everywhere, and even though their scope was limited . 
to their own localities, the business which they did in the aggregate 
was very considerable. 

Taking all in all, therefore, in this year of 1908, many of us thought 
that the industry was beset with difficulties and so came the desire 
70 some of us to form a combination of the principal concerns in the 
ndustry, not with the desire to sell all of the automobiles that were 
-o be sold, but rather for the purpose of having one big concern of 
such dominating influence in the automobile industry, as for instance, 
che United States Steel Corporation exercises in the steel industry, 
0 that its very influence would prevent many of the abuses that we 
relieved existed. ... 

... This was the basis of my thought at the time we of the Max- 
vell-Briscoe Motor Co. had over-traded ... all of these things had 
yrought me to the conclusion that some change must be made and 
‘hat to effect a combination would probably be the most desirable 
‘hing to do. 


34 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


I was in this frame of mind, therefore, when I accepted Mr. 
Durant’s invitation to visit him in Flint. 


8. THE ForD-BuICK-REO-MAXWELL COMBINATION PROJECT 


Briscoe’s interview with Durant, just alluded to, took 
place early in the summer of 1908, and resulted in a project 
for a large combination of automobile manufacturers, with 
the Buick, Ford, Reo, and Maxwell-Briscoe companies as a 
nucleus. The Buick Motor Car Company had been taken 
over by William C. Durant, previously a wagon-manu- 
facturer, three years before, and in 1908 it was the largest 
producer of automobiles in the United States.t The Ford 
Motor Company, the next largest, and the Reo and Maxwell- 
Briscoe companies were likewise very prominent producers; 
and the combination of these four alone might have given 
their owners, as Briscoe hoped, a predominant position in 
the industry. 

Briscoe, who had manufactured parts for the Ford and the 
two Olds enterprises, arranged for a conference between 
Durant, Ford, James Couzens (of the Ford Motor Com- 
pany), Olds, and himself. At this preliminary meeting, held 
in Detroit, it was tentatively decided to organize a new 
company that would purchase the assets of the four others 
by the issue to them of preferred stock in amounts equal to 
the appraised value of their assets, and by the issue of com- 
mon stock on a basis later to be determined.?, Numerous 
conferences in New York followed, in the offices of Ward, 
Hayden & Satterlee; the last-named, a son-in-law of J. P. 
Morgan, Sr., was attorney for the Maxwell-Briscoe Com- 
pany. Ford and Couzens had been reluctant to place the 
project in the hands of Satterlee, preferring their own New 
York attorney, Job Hedges; and this adverse choice of at- 
torney is believed by Briscoe to have caused the hesitancy 

* The Buick Motor Car Company produced 8487 automobiles in 1908 
as compared with 6181 produced by the Ford Motor Company. 

The present account of these and the subsequent negotiations is 


based upon Briscoe’s articles of January 15, 22, 29, and February 5, 
1921, in the Detroit Saturday Night. 


{ 
4 
y 


DEVELOPMENT OF THE AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 35 


that they displayed throughout the negotiations. Finally, 
audits and appraisals having been completed, a tentative 
agreement providing for the distribution of both classes of 
stock in the projected combination was arranged. At. the 
final meeting held for the consummation of the negotiations, 
however, Couzens unexpectedly announced that his com- 
pany had decided to enter the combination only on condition 
that it receive at least $3,000,000 in cash. R. E. Olds then 
insisted that his company must be accorded similar treat- 
ment. As neither Couzens (whose demands might have been 
met had they not involved similar demands on the part of 
Olds) nor Olds could be persuaded to modify his position, the 
whole project was relinquished. 


9g.- THE GENERAL Motors CoMBINATION 


Durant then proposed to Briscoe that the Buick and Max- 
well-Briscoe companies combine to form the nucleus of an- 
other group of automobile producers. With the aid of 
George W. Perkins, of J. P. Morgan & Co., who agreed to 
underwrite $500,000 of the $1,500,000 of new capital desired, 
a charter was drawn up for a company to be called the Inter- 
national Motors Company. The legal aspects of the new 
negotiations were turned over to Francis L. Stetson, one of 
the Morgan attorneys. It was Stetson, according to Briscoe, 
who prevented the consummation of the new project. When 
the question of obtaining the prior approval of the Buick 
stockholders was raised, Durant is said to have replied that, 
while no general announcement had been made, there was no 
question about the ratification of the project whenever he 
chose to present it. Stetson then objected that some of the 
Buick stock had been changing hands during the course of 
the negotiations, and that the sellers should be given oppor- 
tunity to repurchase their stock in view of the new project. 
(It cannot be said whether or not this was a direct intima- 
tion that Durant had been privately purchasing Buick stock 
during the progress of the conferences.) Durant is said to 
have replied that he was following the advice of his own 


36° THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


Michigan attorney, John J. Carton, of Flint, and that he in- 
tended to continue to do so. Whereupon Stetson declared 
that he would advise the Morgan firm to drop all connec- 
tion with the project. Durant was in favor of proceeding 
with the combination in any event, but Briscoe, who was 
under obligation to the Morgan interests for their aid in the 
formation of the Maxwell-Briscoe Company, was obliged to 
withdraw. 

Durant nevertheless proceeded on his own account to ef- 
fect the organization of the General Motors Company, a 
name which had been suggested previously by George W. 
Perkins. The Company was incorporated in New Jersey on 
September 16, 1908, with a capitalization of $7,000,000 of 
preferred and $5,500,000 of common stock. The entire stock 
of the Buick Motor Car Company was immediately acquired 
by exchange of securities. On October 5, 1909, Durant ob- 
tained an option, on behalf of the Combination, to purchase 
the entire capital stock of the Ford Motor Company at a 
price of $8,000,000, $2,000,000 of which was to be paid in 
cash and the remainder in one- and two-year notes.? Be- 
cause the funds of the Combination had been largely ab- 
sorbed by other acquisitions and by plant expansion, and 
because it was impossible to procure an adequate loan, this 
option was allowed to lapse. A similar project for the 
acquisition of the Maxwell-Briscoe Motor Company was 
abandoned for much the same reason. By 1910, however, 
partly by exchange of stock, partly by means of cash realized 
from the sale of securities, and partly by use of the current 
large profits of its constituents, the General Motors Company 
had acquired an entire or controlling interest in some twenty 
automobile and parts-producing enterprises; and it was

marketing ten different brands of motor vehicles, the com- 
bined output of which constituted about 21 per cent by 
volume and 22 per cent by value of the total American 
automobile output.? 

The primary object of the Combination, that of a 
the risks of automobile production by offering a variety of 
automobile products, appeared to be on the high road to 
realization. But this object required the active operation of 
many plants; and, because most of the constituents were 
poorly equipped, the liquid resources of the Combination 
were soon exhausted by fixed capital expansion, and large 
obligations were incurred to banks and suppliers. In 
September, 1910, through a voting trust agreement made in 
this connection, control of the Company passed into the 
hands of a syndicate of investment bankers, who, in return 
for something less than $12,500,000 in cash, received $15,- 
000,000 of the Combination’s five-year sinking-fund notes 
(secured by deed of trust) and a bonus of a large block of 
stock. Under the conservative management of the bankers, 
the financial troubles of the General Motors Company 
quickly disappeared, but its original purpose was largely 
forgotten: the number of motor-vehicle brands was sharply 
reduced, and, by 1915, the Combination’s share of the aggre- 
gate American automobile output had fallen to 8.5 per cent 
by volume and 13.6 per cent by value.? 


10. THE UNITED STATES Motor FIASCO 


While Durant was organizing the General Motors Com- 
pany, Briscoe had proceeded with a separate attempt at 
combination. In September, 1910, the United States Motor 
Company, which had been quietly incorporated with a 
nominal capital in New Jersey two years before, increased 
its authorized capitalization to $30,000,000, divided equally 
between preferred and common stock; and, in the following

38 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


month, largely by exchange of securities, obtained control 
of nine automobile and parts-manufacturing enterprises.* 
The new combination, aided by the financial support of 
Anthony N. Brady and other Eastern financiers, immedi- 
ately embarked upon a large program of expansion. Within 
a few months, its working capital was depleted and it was 
forced to issue $6,000,000 of debenture bonds, which, ac- 
companied by a common stock bonus, were sold to Eugene 
Meyer & Co. and largely retained by this firm. With the 
funds thus realized, the United States Motor Company 
renewed its expansion policy. But the failure of virtually all 
of its twenty-eight distinct models of automobiles, produced 
under seven trade names, to meet with popular approval, 
and the large needs of scores of sales and parts-producing 
companies that it had purchased or organized, forced a 
receivership in 1912. More than a hundred and fifty distinct 
corporations possessing diverse and complicated interrela- 
tions were found to be controlled by the United States 
Motor Company. The bulk of these were liquidated; many 
of the plants, distributed over five states, were sold; and all 
of the automobile products were discontinued. 

A reorganization committee composed of a large number 
of prominent financiers — George W. Davison, Charles H. 
Sabin, Benjamin Strong, Jr., Albert H. Wiggin, James C. 
Brady, and Eugene Meyer, Jr., among others — effected the 
organization of a new company, the Maxwell Motor Com- 
pany, which, with $3,000,000 of fresh capital, took over the 
remains of the United States Motor Company. In order to 
procure the services of Walter E. Flanders, then head of the
