Chrysler Corporation. 


I1. THE SELDEN PATENT 


The several attempts at combination just described re- 
sulted in no perceptible diminution of competition in the 
automobile industry. More than a decade previously, how- 
ever, the industry had faced the imminent threat of monopo- 
listic control; and the prolonged legal litigation that fol- 
lowed had important effects upon the competitive relations 
of American automobile manufacturers. 

Reference has been made to the filing of the first automo- 
bile patent application in the United States by George B. 
Selden, of Rochester, New York, on May 8, 1879. Selden, 
an attorney, dubious of the immediate prospects of exploit- 
ing his invention, had managed to keep the application 
‘alive’ in the Patent Office by various legal means for more 
than sixteen years before causing the patent to be issued. 
When, on November 5, 1895, the patent was finally issued, 
apparently embodying Selden’s wide claims to the original 
application of the internal combustion hydro-carbon motor 
to the road vehicle, the inventor was unable to enlist finan- 
cial support for its exploitation. In 1897, the wave of in- 
dustrial combination then sweeping the United States was 
carried to the nascent automobile industry by William C. 
Whitney, the well-known traction financier, who organized 
a combination of numerous electric vehicle enterprises under 

* 172 Federal 923. . 


40 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


the holding company ownership of the Columbia & Electric 
Vehicle Company.t On November 4, 1899, this company, 
which was succeeded a few months later by the Electric 
Vehicle Company, acquired exclusive rights to the Selden 
patent under an arrangement whereby, eventually, the in- 
ventor received a total of about $200,000 in royalties.? 

The Electric Vehicle Company, coming into possession 
of what appeared to be the basic gasoline automobile patent 
at precisely the time when the gasoline vehicle was supersed- 
ing both steam and electric automobiles, set about immedi- 
ately to enforce its claims. A test suit was started against 
the Winton Motor Carriage Company on November 5, 1900, 
and similar suits were soon brought against others. On 
March 5, 1903, the Winton company and nine other 
automobile producers formed the Association of Licensed 
Automobile Manufacturers, the members of which agreed to 
recognize the validity of the patent and to pay a royalty of 
1.5 per cent of the retail price of all automobiles sold by 
them, two-fifths of the royalties to be returned to the As- 
sociation. This arrangement cleared the air for members of 
the Association, but it threatened to create a partial mono- 
poly of the gasoline automobile manufacturing business of 
the United States, for licensing under the patent was the 
exclusive privilege of members of the Association, the doors 
of which might be closed at any time to new manufacturers. 

The Ford Motor Company, organized a few months pre- 
viously, refused an invitation to join the Association; and on 
October 21, 1903, at the behest of the Association, the 
Electric Vehicle Company brought suit for infringement 
against C. A. Duerr & Co., the New York agent of the Ford 
Company, to which the latter was joined as defendant. 
In the stress of the long legal battle that followed, the 
doors of the Association were thrown open to nearly ninety 
per cent of American automobile manufacturers; the royalty 

* Doolittle, op. cit., p. 60 ff. 

2 Ibid.

DEVELOPMENT OF THE AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 41 


rate was reduced first to one per cent, then to four-fifths of 
one per cent, and finally, toa maximum of $150,000 annually, 
the remainder of the four-fifths of one per cent still collected 
being retained by the Association for its general purposes.’ 
After nearly six years of legal proceedings, the record of 
which filled thirty-six large volumes, Judge Charles M. 
Hough, in September, 1909, rendered a decision upholding 
the patent in every particular.2 The case was immediately 
appealed to the United States Circuit Court of Appeals, 
which, in January, 1911, rendered an opinion formally 
sustaining the Selden patent, but holding that it did not 
apply to the Ford automobile: the latter, the Court decided, 
was based upon the Otto type of gasoline engine, whereas 
Selden’s claims were held to describe an engine known as 
the Brayton type. This decision effectively vitiated the 
Selden patent and destroyed the original basis of the As- 
sociation of Licensed Automobile Manufacturers, for vir- 
tually all American automobiles employed the Otto type of 
engine. 

It is interesting to note that this decision was rendered ata 
time of pronounced public hostility to all forms of combina- 
tions and monopolies. The Association had come in for its 
share of criticism on this ground, despite its liberal conces- 
sions. 

The extent to which the Selden patent litigation actually 
retarded the development of American automobile manu- 
facture is difficult to appraise. The patent claims probably 
discouraged a measure of financial support for some pro- 
ducers; on the other hand, licensing under the patent had 
soon become open to all; the numerous licensees developed a 
large business; the royalty fees cannot be judged to have 
been excessive; and the Ford Motor Company, against which 
the claims were most strenuously pressed, became, during 
the pendency of the suit, the most important automobile 
manufacturer in the world. An important consequence of 

* The usual activities of trade associations.

the litigation, moreover, was the acceleration of a movement 
toward intercorporate standardization and codperation 
among American automobile producers. 


12. STANDARDIZATION AND ‘COOPERATIVE COMPETITION’ 


The traditions and technique of American manufacture 
had virtually enforced some measure of standardization in 
product by each automobile producer from the very begin- 
ning. Most of the components of his products were ordered 
in quantity from independent parts-makers; they could be 
efficiently assembled into finished vehicles only if the latter 
were confined to a few standardized models. The enormous 
economies of mass production of parts and of mass assem- 
bling were progressively accentuated as the demand for auto- 
mobiles increased. The motor vehicle was an intricate piece 
of machinery, moreover, and had to be adjusted and repaired 
by divers local establishments; hence approximate identity 
in construction in the products of each manufacturer was 
a great advantage. Finally, the generic demand for auto- 
mobiles was soon so great that problems of production, 
simplified by standardized practice, rather than those of 
sales, were long uppermost in the minds of automobile pro- 
ducers. 

The great diversity in design of components that at first 
obtained among different automobile producers, was com- 
bated by the influence of the parts-makers, many of whom 
contributed similar components to a number of different 
producers. When the first trade association in the industry 
was formed in 1900, parts- and tire-producers were ad- 
mitted as associate members; and a number of recommen- 
dations for standardized practice were soon adopted. With 
the beginning of the Selden patent controversy, the move- 
ment for intercorporate standardization of minor parts was 
greatly accelerated, the community of interest existing with 
respect to the patent, among the unlicensed, as well as among 

* The National Association of Automobile Manufacturers adopted a 


standard guarantee on automobiles, standard spacing of tire lugs, and 
standard lamp brackets and rim sections, in 1902. 


DEVELOPMENT OF THE AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 43 


the licensed, producers being made the basis for wider codper- 
ation. The charter of the American Motor Car Manufac- 
turers’ Association, formed to combat the patent, provided 
for the interchange of technical information by employees 
of the different producers with a view toward standardiza- 
tion of various components; and the Association of Licensed 
Automobile Manufacturers, in 1905, established a Mechan- 
ical Branch, under the leadership of which intercorporate 
standardization of parts and materials was actively and suc- 
cessfully prosecuted. 

When the Selden patent was finally adjudged inapplica- 
ble to existing automobiles, the National Automobile Cham- 
ber of Commerce was formed to succeed the previous trade 
associations; and the field of technical research and the 
_Teadership in the movement for standardization were taken 
over by the Society of Automotive Engineers. By virtue of 
its representative membership, its effective research achieve- 
-ments, its bulletins and conferences, and the codperation of 
parts- and tire-makers (who had organized as the Motor and 
Accessory Manufacturers’ Association), the Society of Auto- 
motive Engineers succeeded in standardizing many scores of 
parts and many kinds of materials. Whereas, for example, a 
single parts-maker was at one time supplying 800 different 
lockwashers to be used with bolts of only slightly varying 
diameter, requiring, therefore, 800 different producing dies, 
the number of lockwashers was reduced to 16; more than 
1600 sizes of steel tubing gave way to less than 20; the num- 
ber of alloy steels was reduced from more than 200 to less 

than 50;and soon.? No attempt has been made to standard- 
ize the design of the larger components of the various 
‘makes’ of automobiles; and the elimination of needless 
variation in hundreds of small hidden parts and in many 
kinds of materials has not detracted substantially from the 
individuality of the different brands of motor vehicles. Such 
standardization, on the other hand, has effected large econo- 


* See John K. Barnes, ‘The Men Who “Standardized ” Automobile 
Parts,’ in the World’s Work, vol. 42, p. 204 ff. 


44 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


mies in manufacture, and has greatly facilitated expansion 
in output. 


13. POOLING OF PATENT RIGHTS 


Striking as was the progress in standardization of parts 
and materials, a more unusual achievement of intercorporate 
codperation among American automobile producers was the 
pooling of patent rights. Patent litigation in the industry 
had not been confined to Selden’s basic claims. As in the 
sewing machine, agricultural implement, electrical, and 
other industries in which machine-made parts are of great 
importance, numerous patented improvements appeared 
constantly. A competitive scramble for these diverse ad- 
vances was likely to give no single producer a pronounced or 
prolonged advantage: exclusive rights to a preponderant 
proportion of the improvements were not likely to come into 
the possession of any single manufacturer; each advance? 
might be quickly superseded by another; and a wide dis-} 
tribution of the patents would leave each producer at a dis- 
advantage with respect to some improvements. The indus- 
try as a whole, moreover, would be prevented from uni- 
formly adopting progressive advances in product. 

As against the natural difficulties of effecting a free dis- 
tribution of patent privileges among active competitors, 
American automobile producers possessed a keen apprecia- 
tion of the costs, delays, and uncertainties of patent contro- 
versy. Even before the Selden suit had been finally adjudi- 
cated, various proposals for cross-licensing of patents, there- 
fore, had been made.' When, by 1915, the volume of patent 
litigation in the industry was threatening to increase i 
definitely, the sentiment for minimizing this source of ex: 
pense and uncertainty overcame all obstacles. Through the 
agency of the National Automobile Chamber of Commerc 
approximately 100 automobile producers signed a cross- 
licensing agreement whereby, with few exceptions, licenses 
under all patents owned by any party to the agreement w 

* Doolittle, op. ctt., p. 186, 


DEVELOPMENT OF THE AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 45 


to be extended to all other signatories without payment of 
royalties. Patents on large aspects of design were excepted, 
and provision was made for excepting possible radical de- 
partures from existing practice.? In addition, the Associa- 
tion was empowered to acquire patent rights in its own 
name, as trustee, for the common use of its members. The 
- pooling of patents thus achieved was a remarkable extension 
of the principle of standardization through intercorporate 
codperation. It reduced patent litigation in the industry to 
a minimum, and, by means of the provision for joint negoti- 
ation with outside patent-holders, through the Association, 
it minimized the competition for, and hence the costs of, 
additional patent privileges. 


14. EXPANSION OF AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE MANUFACTURE 


For a dozen years prior to 1909, American automobile 
manufacture had been steadily emerging from a novel, spec- 
tacular, and uncertain development into an organized in- 
dustry of considerable promise. The technical character of 
the product had been progressively improved and was com- 
manding increasing public confidence; a number of success- 
ful producers had appeared; and the automobile was steadily 
outgrowing exclusive use as an expensive toy and as an agent 
of sport. Less than 230,000 motor vehicles had been pro- 
duced by the end of 1908, but twenty-five per cent of these 
had been manufactured in the latter year; and, as respects 
both demand and productive capacity, the ground had been 
prepared for great and rapid growth. 

In 1909, the industry entered upon a long period of re- 
markable expansion. The low-priced automobile, which had 
previously made only brief appearances, due perhaps to the 
narrow range and sportive character of the early demand, 
now found a ready market. Led by the Ford Motor Com- 
pany, there was a marked diversion of resources to low- and 
medium-priced vehicles, and a corresponding increase in the 
total volume of sales. The output of 1909 exceeded that of 

* No exceptions have yet been made on this score. 


46 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


1908 by more than 100 per cent, and during the four years 
following, 1,260,000 motor vehicles valued at more than 
$1,292,000,000 were produced.t By 1912, automobiles re- 
tailing at prices under $1000 had climbed to 43.8 per cent of 
the total output, and, by 1915, to 72.3 per cent.? 

Many influences helped to speed the growth in demand. 


Each increase in output was accompanied by a multiplica- | 


tion of sales and service establishments. Municipal, county, 


state, and federal agencies undertook extensive construction 
and improvement of roads. As the automobile became 


more reliable in operation, there was a growing appreciation . 


of its services in the ordinary conduct of business. The Ford 
car, selling at extremely low and falling prices, appealed to 
an enormous market, and served as a powerful educative 
force for potential purchasers of higher-priced cars. And as 
the use of automobiles became more and more common, 
motives of emulation and display reénforced the immediate 
services of the vehicle as important elements in the demand. 
Until 1918, when American participation in the World War 
caused the diversion of much of the industry’s productive 
resources to the manufacture of military products, there was 
a sharp, though varying, increase year by year in the output 
of motor vehicles. At the close of the War, the industry 
quickly renewed its expansion. When, during the depression 
of 1921, the crest of the automobile demand appeared now 
to have been passed, the industry again confounded all pre- 
dictions. Between 1921 and 1926, according to the prelim- 
inary estimates of the National Bureau of Economic Re- 
search, the average income per person gainfully employed in 
the United States increased by more than one-third in pur- 
chasing power; * the automobile claimed a goodly proportion 
of this increase. Through an extensive development of the 
installment-payment plan for the retail sale of automobiles, 
and through sharp reductions in prices, hundreds of thou- 
sands of new purchasers were brought into the market.

48 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


by the motor vehicle, the ability of American producers to 
uncover the enormous lower layers of demand and to re- 
spond so rapidly to their appearance must be explained 
in terms of the industrial organization of the industry, its 
technical processes, and its finance — factors to which we 
have previously alluded. 

The industry inherited, we have noted, a technique of 
manufacture built upon the machine-tool, interchangeabil- 
ity of parts, and codperation of independent enterprises 


supplying convergent sub-products. This technique it pro- — 


ceeded to employ and to extend to an unprecedented degree. 


The number and complexity of the components of the auto- — 
mobile and the great precision necessary in their manufac- — 
ture encouraged the widest distribution of manufacturing ~ 


burdens among specialized suppliers of parts and materials. © 
This distribution, in turn, permitted the early introduction — 
of machines and processes more specialized or elaborate - 
than was justified by the output, at first, of any single pro- — 
ducer; it stimulated a nice attention to detail; and it early | 


resulted in the manufacturing and marketing economies of © 
large-scale operation. As demand increased, the pressure 
upon the parts-makers became ever greater, forcing the 


adoption of every kind of labor-saving and time-saving de- 
vice; and the inventors and producers of machine-tools were 
in turn stimulated to extraordinary ingenuity. 

The factories of automobile producers had at first con- 
sisted of unspecialized buildings equipped with relatively 
little machinery and devoted chiefly to final assembling. 
As output expanded, the assembling operations alone took 
on huge proportions; and as reinvested profits swelled their 
capital resources, a number of producers undertook the di- 
rect production of various components. The Ford Motor 
Company had begun in 1908 to concentrate its efforts upon 
a single standardized model; due to this policy, to the manu- 
facturing genius of Henry Ford and others, and to the re- 
markable expansion of its business (effect as well as cause), 
it was able to introduce radical improvements in factory 


i a ae 


DEVELOPMENT OF THE AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 49 


design and operation;* improvements later copied by other 
producers. ‘Henry Ford taught the rest of us much of what 
we know of automobile production methods,’ said Mr. Roy 
D. Chapin in an interview with the writer.2 The achieve- 
ments of Frederick W. Taylor in ‘scientific management,’ 
the Ford conveyor system, and specialized processes origi- 
nated by various parts-makers, were incorporated in modern 
automobile factory buildings and in the plants of suppliers. 
Internally, the factories became, almost literally, huge re- 
volving machines. Materials entered in the original freight 
cars; reloaded on to divers belts, rollers, monorails, and 
factory trains, they were carried progressively to machine 
after machine, each of which, supervised by one or more 
workers, performed in a few strokes a simple or complex 
operation. The components thus struck off and progres- 
sively refined were subjected to rapid inspection by workers 
armed with instruments of the most remarkable precision. 
Leaving the testers and still carried on moving apparatus, 
the components passed down the assembling lines to be 
joined successively in some major unit or in the finished ve- 
hicle. So certain and accurate became the technique of 
automobile manufacture that the need for final testing of the 
completed vehicles was largely eliminated; and a number of 
producers came to save enormous sums in freight charges by 
shipping parts rather than assembled automobiles to various 
distributing centers at home and abroad. 

To expedite production as well as to meet the deficiency 
of skilled workers, labor tasks were progressively subdivided 
and redivided until hundreds of operations previously re- 
quiring skilled mechanics were taken over by untrained work- 
ers, including a growing number of women in recent years, 
each of whom, aided by minutely specialized machine- 
tools, performed but a simple routine task. The clock-like

movement of materials and components, the scientific ar- 
rangement of machines, and the enforcement of experi- 
mentally determined standards of output, minimized time, 
space, and motion, and rigidly limited the vagaries of in- 
dividual workers, hence speeding volume and reducing costs. 
To counteract the aversion of factory operatives to the 
monotony and strain of routine work performed under con- 
ditions of mechanically enforced regularity and speed, and 
to stimulate individual and group effort, a variety of 
bonus systems based upon output, attendance, and length 
of service were introduced, and relatively high wages, on a 
piece-work basis wherever possible, were paid.t In recent 
years, due to continued improvement in the organization of 
operations, and to the unremitting substitution of mechan- 
ical power for human workers (there was an increase of 44 
per cent in the volume of primary horsepower employed per 
worker in the combined vehicle- and parts-producing 
branches between 1923 and 1925 alone’), the effectiveness 
of labor has markedly increased. While statistical data in 
this field are often subject to misinterpretation,’ it may be 
noted that an index of physical productivity based upon the 
output of 1914 as 100 shows the average productivity per 
worker to have increased to 249 in 1922, 270 in 1923, 262 in 
1924, and 272 in 1925.4 The average annual earnings of 
wage-earners in the combined industries increased from 
$802 in 1914 to $1630 in 1923; between 1922 and 1925, the

average full-time weekly earnings increased from $32.92 to 
$36.37. Because of the great variety of trades involved, 
the rapidity with which the necessary skill for most of them 
can be acquired, and the relatively high wages prevailing, 
there has been little attempt to unionize the employees of 
automobile factories. 

The technical efficiency of American automobile manu- 
facture resulted in falling costs of production, falling prices, 
improved products, and an expanding market; it placed the 
industry well in the forefront of American manufactures; 
and, in its own field, gave it an overwhelming international 
superiority. The average unit value of the American pas- 
senger automobile at the factory in 1914 was $762; in 1925 
it was $692.2 The volume of low-priced vehicles was vastly 
greater in the latter year than in the former, of course; on 
the other hand, the decline in average wholesale value took 
place in the face of a much higher price level and despite 
costly improvements in the product (the proportion of 
closed cars, for example, rose from less than five per cent to 
more than sixty per cent between 1914 and 1925). ‘The 
automobile is one of the few important manufactured com- 
modities that can be bought to-day at less than pre-war 
prices,’ said the Bureau of Foreign and Domestic Commerce 
in its 1926 Yearbook. 

The manner in which the great and rapid expansion of the 
industry was financed has been roughly indicated and will 
}receive more detailed consideration in subsequent pages. 
The collateral growth of the various branches of the indus- 
try gave great flexibility to the capital requirements of in- 
lividual automobile producers and permitted most of them 
-o rely chiefly upon reinvested earnings to increase their own 
nvested capital. There was no lack of distributors and 
lealers willing to contract in advance for a year’s output

and to pay cash upon delivery of the vehicles. The parts- 
makers increased in number and in scale of operations, many 
of them, like the producers proper, building up huge enter- 
prises almost solely out of reinvested profits. Between 1914 
and 1925, the number of establishments engaged in the man- 
ufacture of ‘motor vehicle bodies and parts’? increased 
from 971 to 1358; the wholesale value of their products, 
from $129.6 millions to $1523 millions; the average number 
of their wage-earners, from 47,785 to 228,382.? 

Individual financiers made sporadic investments in auto- 
mobile enterprises from time to time, but the organized 
fixed-capital markets, until very recent years, played a rel- 
atively small rdle in financing the expansion. The five-year 
note issue of the General Motors Company in 1910 was the 
first important public offering of automobile company secu- 
rities. In the same year, investment bankers aided in putting 
together the unfortunate United States Motor Company, pre- 
decessor of the Maxwell and Chrysler companies. In 1911, 
the Studebaker Brothers Manufacturing Company, in ab- 
sorbing the Everitt-Metzger-Flanders Company as a means 
of entering the gasoline vehicle field, reincorporated as the 
Studebaker Corporation, which realized $8,328,802 in cash 
from the sale of $13,500,000 par value of seven-per-cent pre- 
ferred stock; 3 a few months later, this company sold $8,000,- 
ooo of ten-year five-per-cent bonds.4 The securities of the 
General Motors, Maxwell, and Studebaker enterprises were 
the first of the industry to be admitted to the list of the New 
York Stock Exchange. In 1915, the Willys-Overland Com- 
pany sold issues of common and preferred stocks in con- 
nection with its acquisition of a number of small companies; 
and the Chevrolet Motor Company (Delaware), which soon 
after obtained control of the General Motors Company, 
marketed nearly $7,000,000 par value of common stock.

(DEVELOPMENT OF THE AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 53 


These issues represent the chief public offerings of automo- 
bile company securities up to the end of 1915. In subsequent 
years, partly to raise new capital, but much more largely for 
the purpose of liquidating and distributing the holdings of 
original owners, the capital structures of most of the lead- 
ing producers were readjusted; their securities were mar- 
keted in substantial quantities through investment bankers 
‘and became active in speculative and investment circles; 
and banker-representatives became attached to their boards 
of directors. The greatest part of their existing invested 
capital, however, it will be shown, has been derived from the 
reinvestment of profits. ! 

The capital, risk, and technical burdens of large-scale 
automobile manufacture, diffused in varying measure among 
hosts of independent enterprises, each of which possessed its 
own sources for augmenting capital and credit and its own 
specialized organization, offered singularly small resistance 
to the growth of the industry. To the elasticity of this type 
of industrial practice must be attributed much of the ease 
and rapidity with which the expansion of American auto- 
mobile manufacture took place. 


15. THE AUTOMOBILE FINANCE COMPANY 


We have observed that the ability of automobile manu- 
facturers to produce and to market a large and increasing 
quantity of product has been due in important measure to 
the shifting of capital burdens to dealers and distributors as 
well as to suppliers of parts and materials. The producers 
possessed neither the physical facilities nor the capital to 
store and ‘carry’ large inventories of finished product 
through periods of seasonal fluctuations in retail sales; but 
they were able to contract with wholesalers and retailers for 
orearranged schedules of shipments, irrespective of current 
sales to consumers, and to exact immediate cash payment 
ipon delivery. Distributors (virtually wholesale agents), 
ind, to a lesser degree, retail dealers, were chosen largely 
iccording to financial responsibility, the producers expect- 


54 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


ing to be relieved by them from all burdens incident to th 
final sale of the products. 

In the earlier years of the industry, the selling agents were 
able to finance themselves from their own capital and by 
ordinary banking accommodation. With the tremendous 
expansion of output, the number of dealers and distributors 
was increased, but their average capital resources declined, 
and adequate financing of their costly inventories could no 
longer be undertaken by commercial banks: the possible 
banking collateral arising from their operations consisted of 
chattel mortgages and warehouse receipts that were not read- 
ily adaptable to the requirements of routine banking prac- 
tice, while the amount of credit required exceeded that which 
the banks would lend on unsecured promissory notes. On 
the other hand, the needs of dealers and distributors were 
essentially legitimate commercial credit needs. A special 
credit institution to serve as a financial intermediary be- 
tween sales agents and commercial banks was a natural re- 
sponse to these conditions. 

More important, however, in stimulating the growth of 
specialized automobile finance companies was the extensive 
development, particularly after the World War, of the 
practice of selling automobiles at retail on installment-pay- 
ment plans. The automobile was one of the most expensive 
of consumers’ goods; to encourage sales, dealers were forced 
to extend an increasing amount of credit to their customers. 
In some cases, the promissory notes accepted in payment of 
vehicles could be discounted at commercial banks; but credit 
accommodation was demanded by (and, as a result of com- 
petition, offered to) large numbers of individuals who lacked 
conventional credit standing. This situation, even more 
than the one alluded to above, called for a specialized type 
of credit institution: (1) the dealers’ capital was better em- 
ployed in their own business — namely, the purchase and 
sale of automobiles, rather than in ‘carrying’ the notes of 
customers; (2) such notes could not be readily discounted at 
commercial banks because of the unconventional form of the 


DEVELOPMENT OF THE AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 55 


security — that is, chattel mortgages or conditional-sale 
agreements on Cars in active use and subject to rapid depre- 
ciation — and because of the difficulty and expense of provi- 
sions for the investigation of risks and the ‘repossessing’ 
and sale of cars in cases of default; (3) the substitution of the 
notes of an intermediary finance company for those of in- 
dividual customers not only provided commercial banks 
)with paper better adapted to their operations, but it also 
permitted a wider distribution of such paper among banks 
and a wider distribution of risks (by pooling the notes of 
‘many customers purchasing different ‘makes’ of cars in 
idifferent localities). 

The automobile finance company, therefore, does two 
kinds of business: it finances the purchases of distributors 
and dealers from manufacturers, and it finances purchases at 
retail. Its funds are derived from invested capital, issues of 
collateral trust notes, and direct borrowing from commer- 
tial banks.t The number of automobile finance companies 
yperating in the United States was recently estimated at 
six hundred;? some of them, such as the General Motors 
Acceptance Corporation, are subsidiaries of automobile pro- 
lucers; others have exclusive contracts with various manu- 
Jacturers for the business of the latter’s distributors and 
lealers; most of them operate independently. More than 
eventy-five per cent of motor vehicle sales in the United 
jtates in 1925 were made on the installment-payment plan; 
he volume of installment paper issued in that year exceeded 
12,500,000,000; the average contract on new vehicles called 
or a cash payment of one-third of the list price, with the 
valance, including financing charges, payable in twelve 
qual monthly installments; the nominal interest and serv- 
se charges averaged ten per cent of the full amount of the 
npaid balance (the actual rate, allowing for monthly repay- 


See Estey, ‘Financing the Sale of Automobiles,’ and Hodges, ‘Fi- 
ancing the Automobile,’ in the Annals of the American Academy of 
olitical and Social Science, vol. CXVI, no. 205. 

* Ibid. 


56 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY { 
ment of fractions of the loans, was measurably greater). 
The statement that the capital turnover of an automobile 

finance company may easily exceed twenty to thirty times a 

year, made by the treasurer of one of these companies,’ re- 
flects the extent to which the ultimate financing is done by 

commercial banks. The legitimacy of the wide use of com- 

mercial banking credit in this connection may be open to 

question; but there can be little doubt that the extensive 

application of the installment-payment system has been 

partly responsible for the enormous growth in the sales of 

automobiles in the United States in very recent years. 


16. CONCENTRATION, INTEGRATION, AND COMPETITION 


Fairly early in the development of the industry, the 
‘greater part of the business of finished vehicle production 
began to be concentrated into relatively few hands. The de- 
mand for the more successful ‘makes’ of cars grew cumula- 
tively by force of reputation and example. The successful 
producers, in expanding output, increased the number and 
distribution of their sales agencies, thus accentuating their 
marketing advantage. Greater output brought economies 
in the purchase and manufacture of components and mate- 
rials, and in assembling operations, permitting lower prices. 
The competitive advantage of the larger producers was pro- 
gressively augmented, moreover, by the important réle of 
reinvested profits in financing expansion of output. By 1912, 
when at least fifty-seven different enterprises were produc- 
ing motor vehicles on a truly commercial scale, more than 
half the American output was produced by seven companies. — 
In 1917, ten out of seventy-six active producers were re- 
sponsible for seventy-five per cent of the physical output of 
the American industry; in 1923, the same ten produced 
more than ninety per cent of the output; and in 1926, these

An increasing measure of industrial integration has ac- 
companied the concentration of production in recent years. 
The Ford Motor Company, which has adopted the slogan, 
‘From Mine to Finished Car, One Organization,’ has been 
most conspicuous in this respect: iron, coal, and lead mines; 
extensive timber properties; blast furnaces, glass and textile 
factories; and even rail and water transportation have been 
added to the scope of its operations.t ,The General Motors 
Corporation, through its purchase of a large number of

parts-making enterprises in 1918, and through its subsequen 
acquisition of the Fisher Body Corporation, has likewis 
achieved a large measure of integration. Other leading pro 
ducers, in varying degree, have similarly undertaken the} 
direct manufacture of an increasing proportion of the com-} 
ponents of their products. This tendency has been facili- 
tated by the curtailment of the number of models that fol- 
lowed the widespread appreciation of the enormous econo- 
mies of standardized mass manufacture, and it has been en- 
couraged by the availability of funds derived from reinvested 
profits for financing such extension of operations. The es- 
tablishment of factory-owned wholesale organizations and 
the direct operation of retail stores in the larger cities, by a 
number of producers, are developments in the same direc- 
tion. Integrated production entails larger requirements for 
fixed and working capital, and, to the extent that it effects 
substantial economies, competitive superiority becomes a 
function of size. The rapid growth of industrial integra- 
tion in the automobile industry in very recent years is 
partly reflected by Table 4, in which the total wholesale 
value of the American motor-vehicle output is compared 
with the wholesale value of parts, tires, and accessories 
purchased by automobile producers from others for the 
production and original equipment of their vehicles, 1922 to 
1926, inclusive. 

Despite the concentration and integration of manufacture, 
competition among automobile producers has increased, 
rather than diminished, in recent years. As a result of the 
tremendous expansion of manufacturing facilities and of the 
marked improvements in the utilization of plant equipment, 
since the World War, the productive capacity of the indus- 
try has been increased to a point far beyond any demand

17. SUCCESS AND FAILURE 


The history of the American automobile industry has been 
marked by such conspicuous financial successes that the 
large number of unsuccessful entrants is sometimes ignored. 
As in a lottery, only the great prizes catch the eye, though 
the winnings are confined to a few. There were many 
would-be competitors for the large rewards of successful 
automobile manufacture: nearly one thousand distinct 
enterprises are on record as having been organized at one 
time or another for entrance into the field.t Four-fifths of 
these, however, passed little beyond the promotion stage, 
falling early victims to one or more of the numerous hazards 
that attended all initial efforts in the industry. Between 
1902 and the beginning of 1927, some 181 enterprises reached 
the stage of production in commercial volume; of these, only 
44 were still in existence in the latter year. Excluding short- 
lived promotions, and disregarding changes in name or in 
corporate ownership, the record of entrances into and exits 
from the industry by years for this period is presented in 
Table 5, the data for which were drawn from a recent study 
of failures in the industry.3 

A high rate of mortality among automobile producers was 
not unnatural. The industry was new; its product, complex. 
Technological and business factors encouraged the entrance 
of numerous small enterprises. Successful operation re-

‘quired frequent prevision of and prompt adjustment to cur-

Most of the large number of retirements in I910 were 
orecipitated by the radical changes in automobile design 
chat were then occurring, and by a retrenchment in credit 


66 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


caused by the grave and widely known difficulties of the 
General Motors Company. In addition, an absolute as well 
as relative decline in the demand for high-priced vehicles” 
had begun to manifest itself just previously: this change in 
the character of the market caught many producers una- 
wares, and was responsible for a large number of further 
retirements in the years following. Twenty-two new enter- 
prises were started during the post-war boom of 1919-20; 
most of these joined victims of accumulated difficulties in 
the many exits of 1922-26. 

Numerous as the failures have been (and not all the exits 
from the industry were precipitated by technical insol- 
vency), relatively few of them resulted in large losses. We 
have noted that most of the producers started with small — 
resources; and that the amount of capital ‘staked’ or risked — 
in automobile production proper was minimized by the wide ~ 
diffusion of capital burdens that obtained. Of the total num- | 
ber of 181 active producers, approximately thirty per cent re- 
tired within three years; more than half dropped out within 
six years; and two-thirds, within nine years. Many of those 
who subsequently retired, earned and distributed or rein- 
vested large profits for some years. Bond issues were rare. 
Few large enterprises, moreover, failed. The present Chrys- 
ler Corporation, now highly successful, is the only important 
producing enterprise that is the product of a receivership. 
Of the ten largest producers in 1909 (measured by physical 
volume of output), five were still among the ten largest in 
1926; and of the ten leading producers in 1918, eight held 
places among the first ten in 1926. 

However great the danger of rationalization, and how- 
ever large the rdle of accident, it is almost impossible to 
escape the conclusion that, transcending and underlying 
sundry advantages of location, design, finance, or good 
fortune, the elusive and imponderable qualities of personal-— 
ity have been the most effective causes of conspicuous suc- 
cesses in the automobile industry. Outstanding talent for 
manufacturing proper was possessed by Henry Ford, Ran-

som E. Olds, Henry M. Leland, Walter E. Flanders, Howard 
E. Coffin, Walter P. Chrysler, Charles W. Nash, the Dodge 
brothers, and others. These men paid meticulous attention 
to design, materials, and factory operation. Ford and Olds 
still dominate enterprises initiated more than twenty years 
ago. Leland, after some years of parts-making, developed 
the Cadillac company. Flanders, likewise previously a 
parts-maker, was called in by Henry Ford in 1908 to effec- 
tuate the latter’s newly launched program for greatly ex- 
panded production. Subsequently, Flanders performed 
similar services for a number of other producers, notably 
Briscoe, Everitt-Metzger-Flanders, Studebaker, and Max- 
well. Coffin contributed unusual engineering talent to the 
Thomas-Detroit, Chalmers, and Hudson enterprises, as well 
as to the movement for intercorporate standardization of 
parts and materials in the automobile industry. The Dodge 
brothers, after many years of parts manufacture for the 
Ford Motor Company, brought out the Dodge automobile 
in 1914; within eleven years, their original investment of 
$5,000,000, augmented only by reinvested earnings, became 
an enterprise that was sold in 1925 for $170,000,000.? 
Chrysler and Nash, previously Buick factory executives, as 
well as Flanders and several other men, took over new or 
moribund enterprises and energized them with striking 
rapidity into leading producers. This was the case with 
William C. Durant, who, after a long business experience in 
the carriage and wagon and other industries, became in- 
terested in the failing Buick company, and, within three 
years, made it the largest automobile producer in the United 
States:? his subsequent exploits with the General Motors, 
Chevrolet, and United Motors enterprises, were likewise 
evidence of unusual promotion and organizing talent. John 
N. Willys performed a feat similar to Durant’s, in connection 
with the Willys-Overland Company. Roy D. Chapin, 
Howard E. Coffin, and R. B. Jackson developed the Hudson 
Motor Car Company into one of the world’s largest pro- 
* See Chapter V, below. 2 See Chapter IV, below. 


68 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


ducers from an original investment of less than $15,000. 
Henry P. Joy, one of the few men of wealth who entered the 
industry from unrelated fields, brought to the Packard a 
passion for excellency which the product has traditionally 
reflected. Erskine of the Studebaker; Hastings of the Hupp; 
Jewett of the Paige; Metzger, Briscoe, and others, were men 
of unusual business ability. 

It seems hardly too much to say that individuals capable 
of large and direct accomplishments constituted the primary 
condition of outstanding success in the automobile industry. 
The Ford Motor Company, dominated though it has always 
been by Henry Ford, owes much to a number of such men;" 
and the presence of unusual personal capacity has been 
conspicuous in the history of every other prominent pro- 
ducer. Ample rewards went to those, often chosen from the 
ranks, who demonstrated ability to achieve quick and signi- 
ficant results: salaries ranging as high as or exceeding $100,- 
000 a year; bonuses proportioned to corporate profits; and 
large stock interests — sometimes enough for effective con- 
trol — were available to such men.? The late James J. 
Storrow, of Lee, Higginson & Co., formerly chairman of 
the board of directors of the General Motors Company, il- 
lustrated the transcendent importance of personality in the 
automobile, as in other industries, in explaining to the 
writer the origin of the Nash Motors Company. Mr. Storrow 
said: 3 

Charlie Nash had been a good wagon manufacturer, and then a

other example, then sales manager of the National Cash Register Com- 
pany, was given effective control of what became the Chalmers Motor 
Car Company in order to enlist his services. The General Motors Cor- 
poration, it may be noted, now regularly provides for substantial profit- 
tare for stock-ownership, by its executive employees. (Cf. Chap-

fine Buick factory man. I picked him to be head of General Motors. 
In five years, he turned a wreck into a concern having $25,000,000 in 
the bank. When Durant took control of General Motors away from 
us, I wired Nash to come here, and I said: ‘Charlie, you did a fine 
job of G.M.; if you could do that once, you can do it again; look 
around for another wreck; I’ll back you.’ He picked the Jeffery out- 
fit, which we bought for less than $5,000,000. Nash made a hundred 
million dollars out of it for us in seven years. There was nothing to 
the Jeffery outfit. Nash was everything. He could do the same with 
a railroad. 


18. INTERNATIONAL SUPERIORITY OF THE UNITED STATES 


Despite the earlier start of the industry in Europe, notably 
in France, the United States has become preéminently the 
world’s greatest producer, consumer, and exporter of motor 
vehicles. American producers accounted for more than 
eighty-five per cent of the aggregate motor-vehicle produc- 
tion of the world in 1926, the average monthly output of the 
United States being far in excess of the entire year’s output 
of its nearest competitor.t Of a total world registration of 
more than 27,500,000 motor vehicles at the beginning of 
1927, nearly eighty per cent were registered in this country.? 
Exports of motor vehicles from the United States and Can- 
ada (the latter’s output is produced by subsidiaries of United 
States enterprises almost exclusively) totaled 478,928 in 
1926, or more than twice the entire output of any other 
country. Automotive exports, we have observed, rank first 
in value among all the manufactures exported from the 
United States, and are exceeded in value only by raw cotton 
and mineral oils among all American commodity exports. 
The comparative importance of the leading automobile-pro-

It seems reasonable to conclude that the greater per capita 
income of the United States and the presumably broader 
distribution of income made the American market a superior 
one for the sale of automobiles. That this market was 
obtained by domestic rather than foreign producers is to be 
explained by various considerations of economic and cultural 
import. 

Throughout the developmental period of American auto- 
mobile manufacture, the domestic industry was ‘protected’ 
by anad-valorem duty of 45 per cent levied against imported 
motor vehicles.2, Superimposed upon shipping costs, this 
duty offered almost insuperable obstacles to the importa- 
tion of any but relatively high-priced vehicles. It is tempt- 
ing, therefore, to point to the American automobile industry 
as a glowing example of the force of the ‘infant industries’ 
argument for tariff protection, alleging that, in the absence 
of such protection, the early efforts of the American pro- 
ducers would have been smothered by the competition of the 
more experienced French manufacturers.3 While tariff pro- 
tection was not without some influence, such a claim, in the 
opinion of the present writer, would be of extremely doubt- 
ful validity. Imports spur and educate the home producer, 
as well as compete with him: much was learned by American 
inventors and manufacturers from imported French ma- 
chines, and a number journeyed abroad at various times to 
acquaint themselves with the development of the French 
art. ‘There was no lack of inventive genius in the United 
States; there was an abundance of the required natural re- 

* From Income in the United States, p. 144. 


* Under the heading of ‘manufactured metal’ in the Dingley tariff. 
3 Cf. Rolls, Encyclopedia Britannica, vol. xvi, p. 914 ff. 


DEVELOPMENT OF THE AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 73 


sources; and, most of all, this country possessed a manufac- 
turing technique peculiarly well adapted to automobile 
manufacture, that, even now, is largely lacking abroad. The 
ease and rapidity with which technical knowledge and ma- 
terial means of production were mobilized for the American 
automobile industry indicate that, even in the absence of 
tariff protection, foreign producers could have hoped for 
but a temporary advantage in the American market. 
European automobiles, moreover, were better adapted to 
the excellent roads of France and England than to those of 
the United States, Canada, Australia, and other newer, less 
populous, or less developed countries — a fact which still 
operates to give the sturdier American vehicle a distinct 
advantage in such markets. The European tendency to- 
ward light-car construction, which might have resulted in 
the early appearance of low-priced vehicles, was discouraged, 
moreover, by European governments, which, with an eye to 
the military possibilities of the motor vehicle, inaugurated 
various subvention schemes that encouraged the manufac- 
ture of heavier, though not more powerful, vehicles. Thus, 
for some years prior to 1914, the French Government of- 
fered a subsidy of about $1400, payable over a period of 
four years, for each motor vehicle earmarked for potential 
military use; in Germany, the subsidy amounted to more 
than $2000 per vehicle; and in England, to about $584." 
These subsidies, shared in varying proportion by producers 
and purchasers, coupled with the narrow upper-class mar- 
kets abroad, served to divert European efforts to the manu- 
facture of relatively costly vehicles. In the United States, in 
contrast, less expensive automobiles of rugged construction 
and great power dominated the industry. Even so far as 
international trade is concerned, the American automobile 
industry grew up quickly indeed: by 1907, we have observed, 
the value of American exports of motor vehicles was already 
exceeding that of imports; and in the years since, the export 


t Wyatt, ‘The Motor Industry and the War,’ in the Quarterly Review, 
July, 1915, p. 179; and The Motor Industry (Pitman), p. 13 ff. 


74 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


leadership of the United States has become ever more 
pronounced. 

Certain less tangible considerations of tradition and 
culture must be credited with an important réle in the supe- 
rior development of the American industry. The sparsity 
of population over great areas in this country for many 
years, and the rapid development of the means of trans- 
portation and communication, had early fostered geograph- 
ical centralization of American manufactures and the meth- 
ods of large-scale production. The widespread purchase 
of standardized commodities, produced in large volume in 
relatively few centers, became a familiar characteristic of 
our economic life. This modern development proceeded 
more slowly abroad, where smaller national areas, made 
economically distinct by tariff barriers, and where the 
greater density of population and an older tradition of 
manufacture to order, had long encouraged local manufac- 
tures. Thus, in the United States, with its traditions of 
large-scale, standardized production, and its greater per 
capita income, the automobile came naturally to be pro- 
duced in standardized patterns and in large quantities, with 
consequent great economies; whereas in Europe, with its 
traditions of individualized products, and its lower per 
capita incomes, the automobile was produced on an in- 
comparably smaller scale of operations and in a great variety 
of patterns. 

The continuing technical superiority of American manu- 
facturing methods is clearly evidenced by the extent to 
which American producers are able to hurdle European 
tariff walls, and by their success in operating branch plants 
abroad in competition with European producers. In July, 
1927, there were twenty-five American-owned assembling 
and manufacturing establishments in foreign countries. The 
benefits derived from the latter practice are obvious: saving 
in ocean freight by shipping unassembled parts; saving in 
duty by reason of the fact that some countries assess a lower 
rate on parts for assembly than on complete vehicles; the 


DEVELOPMENT OF THE AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 75 


ease with which distribution may be made to foreign dealers 
through local plants; the ability to adhere more closely to 
local preferences in design and equipment; and, in some 
markets, the advantage accruing from lower costs of labor 
and materials.

* The totals of unit vehicle exports do not include a large, though uncertain, num- 
ber of vehicles that have been assembled abroad from exported parts, but that have 
not been so reported. It is the practice of all Canadian producers and of most Ameri-— 
can producers to declare exports of parts, whenever possible, as the equivalent of so 
many complete vehicles. A varying proportion of foreign assemblies, however, is the 
product of exported parts that have not been so declared; such assemblies are not 
commonly included in either export or home production totals, whereas the former 
are. On the other hand, to add all foreign assemblies to the home production and 
unit export totals involves duplication of vehicle units already counted, though ex- 
ported in the form of parts. A similar distinction must be observed in computing the 
value of American automotive exports. In the first six months of 1927, the Bureau 
of Commerce found that between 43 and 55 per cent of foreign assemblies of American 
automobiles were made up of exported parts that had not been declared as the equiva- 
lent of so many complete vehicles. (Cf. Commerce Reports, September 12, 1927, p. 
657 ff.) Such assemblies should be added to the home production and export totals 
(United States and Canada). The total unit volume of foreign assemblies of Ameri- 
can vehicles for the years 1922 to 1926, as compiled by the Automotive Division of the 
Bureau of Foreign and Domestic Commerce, was as follows:

CHAPTER III 
THE FORD MOTOR COMPANY 


1. VOLUME AND IMPORTANCE OF BUSINESS 


THE Ford Motor Company, judged by physical volume of 
output and by amount of invested capital, is the largest 
producer of automobiles in the world. The first unit of its 
famous ‘Model T’ was produced on October 1, 1908, some 
five years after the Company’s incorporation; the millionth 
unit of this model left the factory on December 10, 1915; 
between this date and June 4, 1924, 9,000,000 more were 
produced; and on May 26, 1927, the fifteen-millionth 
“Model T’ rolled off the assembly lines.? During the four- 
teen years ended in 1926, the output of the Ford Motor 
Company averaged well above forty per cent of the aggre- 
gate volume of American motor-vehicle production. A 
comparison of the Company’s annual output during this 
period with that of the American industry as a whole is 
presented in Table 18. 

While the Ford Motor Company is primarily a producer 
of finished automobiles, motor trucks, and automotive farm 
tractors, a vast program of industrial integration, initiated 
some years ago, has carried its operations into many fields; 
and to-day it is a business with far-flung properties and 
diverse, though related, interests. The aim and result of 
this program was expressed in a pamphlet entitled ‘Ford 
Products,’ issued by the Company in August, 1924: 

A self-contained industry produces Ford cars, trucks, and Ford- 
son tractors. It is the Ford industry, whose resources go back to the 
standing timber and unmined ore. They include Ford-owned coal 


mines, blast furnaces, glass factories, huge manufacturing units, as- 
sembly plants, and an organization of trained men whose combined

activities comprehend every phase of human endeavor. From widely 
scattered Ford-owned sources, raw materials are brought by Ford 
transportation units to Ford factories where they are refined, 
moulded, machined and assembled under the most favorable of in- 
dustrial conditions. ... 


The chief manufacturing units of the Company are located 
in Highland Park and Fordson, Michigan, on the outskirts of 
Detroit, and employ approximately 100,000 workers. In 
addition, the Company operates thirty-six branch assembly 
and manufacturing plants in the United States, and, through 
foreign associated companies, it conducts manufacturing 
and assembling operations in the principal countries of 
Europe and South America.t_ Legal ownership of the great

workers, and the foreign branches, 11,028, according to a Company an-

THE FORD MOTOR COMPANY 85 


bulk of the Ford properties is vested directly in the Ford 
Motor Company, a Delaware corporation. The Ford Motor 
Company of Canada, Ltd., is a separate corporate entity, 
as are the other foreign Ford enterprises, control of which is 
maintained through the stock-ownership of Henry and 
Edsel Ford. Control of the Detroit, Toledo & Ironton Rail- 
road, acquired in 1920, is maintained in the same way. The 
Michigan Iron, Land & Lumber Company, incorporated in 
September, 1920, and the Fordson Coal Company, incor- 
porated in February, 1923, are direct subsidiaries of the 
Ford Motor Company, which owns all of their capital stock. 
This is likewise the case with the Lincoln Motor Company, 
acquired at a receiver’s sale on February 4, 1922, and now 
operated as a ‘division’ of the Ford Motor Company. 

The Ford organization may be said to include more than 
the Company’s immediate interests and those of its sub- 
sidiaries. More than 52,000 sales agencies and service sta- 
tions, individually owned by their operators, are directly 
supervised by the Company’s executives. The Company 
determines the retail prices of its cars, trucks, and parts; 
suggests the design of sales rooms and service quarters; fixes 
the charges for repair work; and supervises the accounting 
system and advertising policy of its dealers.? In its financial 
crisis of 1920-21, the Company felt free to call upon its 
dealers to absorb shipments of cars in excess of contracted 
allotments, exacting immediate cash payment, and thereby 
shifting to its dealers much of the burden of the emergency 
financing.? The Ford organization may also be said to include 
a number of independent parts-makers whose chief or ex- 
clusive customer is the Ford Motor Company. In many 
cases, the latter purchases the necessary materials, supplies 
the patterns, investigates the accounts to determine upon 
prices, and sometimes helps to finance the business of such 


Cf. The Ford Industries, published by the Company, p. 125; and 
Henry Ford, My Life and Work, p. 60. 

2 Cf. the Detroit News of July 22, 1921, in which James Swineheart 
‘reports an interview with Henry Ford. 


86 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


suppliers.! ‘There are hundreds of concerns from which we 
take the major part of their output...’ Henry Ford was 
quoted as saying in July, 1921.’ 

The chief commercial products of the Ford Motor Com- 
pany are the Ford automobile, produced in various body 
styles; the Ford truck; the Fordson farm tractor; the 


Lincoln automobile; and, very recently, the Ford (Stout © 


Metal) airplane. The most important of these by far, need- 
less to say, is the Ford passenger automobile, which has 
been produced and sold in incomparably greater quantities 
than the product of any other automonile manufacturer. 
The distinctive characteristic of the Ford cars, from an eco- 
nomic standpoint, is that they have long been the lowest- 
priced automobiles produced in the United States, and, 
therefore, in the world. For many years, all other automo- 
biles retailed at prices exceeding those of the Ford cars by 
$200 or more. The productive technique that made possible 
its low prices, and the enormous volume of demand that 
resulted from them, made the Ford Motor Company the 
largest producer of automobiles in the world. More than 
15,000,000 Ford cars and trucks, we have noted, have been 
produced and sold; the tenth million required only 132 
working days to produce. 


2. ORIGIN OF Forp Motor COMPANY 


Unlike the United States Steel Corporation, the General 
Motors Corporation, the great railroad enterprises, and 
other very large business organizations, the Ford Motor 
Company acquired its present enormous assets and achieved 
its present position in industry neither by combinations and 
consolidations nor by the aid of a large body of investors. 
Competitive superiority in the largest field of automobile 


* Dodge v. Ford Motor Company, 1070 Mich. Sup. Ct. Briefs and 
Records. 

* Detroit News, July 22, 1921. 

3 Announcement of the Ford Motor Company, quoted in the Detroit 
News of June 5, 1924. 


THE FORD MOTOR COMPANY 87 


manufacture — the field of low-priced automobiles — and 
the continuous reinvestment of much of the large profits of 
the enterprise, are the principal factors that explain the 
present position of the Ford Motor Company. 

Henry Ford, the founder of the Company, first com- 
pleted the construction of a gasoline automobile in 1893, 
when only one other gasoline vehicle had been built in the 
United States. After devoting five years to the improve- 
ment of his model, Ford resigned from his position as chief 
engineer of the Detroit Edison Company, and set out to in- 
terest capital in the manufacture of his vehicle. By entering 
his model in a number of automobile races, he attracted the 
attention of a small group of men, and, with their aid, or- 
ganized the Detroit Automobile Company, in 1898. This 
company was capitalized at $50,000, of which P1O,000) was 
contributed in cash.” 

At the end of three years, during which time the Coane 

had produced two cars, Ford, dissatisfied with his one-sixth 
interest and consequent lack of control, sold his stock and 
resigned from his position as the Company’s chief engineer. 
The Detroit Automobile Company later became in turn the 
Cadillac Automobile Company and the Cadillac Motor Car 
Company. 
- A second Ford company, the Henry Ford Automobile 
Company, was incorporated in Michigan on November 23, 
1901, with a paid-in capital of $38,000, of which Henry Ford 
was credited with $10,000.2, This company was dissolved 
in less than a year, the victim of dissension among stock- 
holders and officers, most of whom, it is said, insisted upon 
the manufacture of large and expensive automobiles as 
against Ford’s desire to produce low- priced cars in large 
quantities.3 

Ford now entered into an arrangement with Alexander 


t Arnold and Faurote, Ford Methods and the Ford Shops, p. 13. 


2 Annual report of the Henry Ford Automobile Company for 1901; 
Michigan Secretary of State. 


3 Henry Ford’s Own Story as Told to Rose Wilder Lane, p. 112 ff.

Y. Malcomson, a prosperous Detroit coal dealer, whom he 
had met during his employment with the Detroit Edison — 
Company, whereby, in return for a 25.5 per cent interest in 
the projected Ford Motor Company, Malcomson was to — 
guarantee the financing of the production of the Ford auto- 
mobile up to the amount of $3000." Malcomson was com- 
mitted for $7000 before the Ford Motor Company was 
finally organized. 

The Ford Motor Company was incorporated in Michigan 
on June 16, 1903, with an authorized capitalization of 
$150,000, all common stock, of which $100,000 was issued. 
Of the latter, $49,000 was listed as issued for cash; $40,000 
for ‘letters patent issued and applied for’; $10,000 for ma- 
chinery and stock; and $1000 for ‘contracts for supplies.’ ? 

Ford and Malcomson each received $25,500 of stock; 
John S. Gray, president of the German-American Savings — 
Bank of Detroit, subscribed for $10,500, with the presidency 
of the Company, and with Malcomson’s personal guarantee 
against loss, offered as inducements; 3 John W. Anderson and 
Horace H. Rackham, Malcomson’s attorneys, each sub- 
scribed for $5000; John F. Dodge and Horace E. Dodge, 
proprietors of a machine shop, subscribed for $5000 each, 
paying for their subscriptions with promissory notes which 
they later met from their profits on orders filled for the new 
company;4 James Couzens, then a clerk in Malcomson’s 
coal office, paid $1000 in cash and $1500 in promissory 
notes for $2500 of stock; Albert Strelow, Vernon E. Fry, and 
C. H. Bennett each subscribed for $5000; and C. J. Woodall, 
Malcomson’s bookkeeper, subscribed for $1000. Only 
$28,000 in cash was actually received by the Company, and, 
according to a Ford publication issued in September, 1920, 
this was all the cash ever paid in on its capital stock.s

THE FORD MOTOR COMPANY | 89 ' 


Operations were begun in June, 1903, in rented quarters in 
the rear of a carpenter’s shop. A small amount of space 
was sufficient because engines, chassis, bodies, and most of 
the assembling operations were to be completed by inde- 
pendent establishments, the work of the Ford Motor Com- 
pany being confined to a small amount of final assembling 
and to sales. The Company’s plan of operations is de- 
scribed in some detail in the following quotation, taken from 
a letter written just prior to the incorporation by Anderson, 
one of the stockholders mentioned above, in which letter 
Anderson asked his father to procure a loan of $5000 to en- 
able the son to make good his subscription to the Company’s 
stock: ? 


... After canvassing the matter thoroughly, ... they determined 
to enter into contracts with various concerns to supply the different 
parts and simply do the assembling themselves. 

So they entered into a contract with the Dodge brothers, who own 

a machine shop here, to manufacture the automobile complete — less 
the wheels and bodies — for $250 apiece, or $162,500 for the 650 
_ machines which are to be delivered at the rate of 10 a day, com- 
mencing July 1, if possible, and all by October 1. I drew the con- 
tracts, so know all about it. 
_ Now, to comply with this contract, which was made last October, 
' Dodge Brothers had to decline all outside orders and devote the 
entire resources of their plant to the turning out of these automobiles. 
_ They were paid only $10,000 on account, and had to take all the rest 
of the risk themselves. They had to borrow $40,000, place orders for 
castings all over the country, pay their men from last October (they 
have a large force), and do everything necessary to manufacture all 
the machines before they could hope to get a cent back. ... 

In addition to this, contracts for the remaining parts of the auto- 
mobile — the bodies, seat cushions, wheels and tires — were made so 
they were to be supplied as wanted. The bodies and cushions by the 
C. R. Wilson Carriage Co. at $52 apiece and $16 apiece, respectively. 
The wheels by a Lansing, Mich., firm at $26 a set (4 wheels). The 
tires by the Hartford Rubber Co. at $40 a set (4 wheels). 

They found a man from whom Mr. Malcomson rents a coal yard 
on the Beltline Railroad with a spur track running into it. He agreed 
to erect a building, designed by Mr. Ford for their special use, for 
assembling purposes (which will cost between $3000 and $4000), and 


* The letter is quoted in full in the Detroit News of January 14, 1927. | 


90 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


rent it for three years to Mr. Ford and Mr. Malcomson at $75 a 
month.... 

To this assembling plant are shipped the bodies, wheels and tires 
and the machines from Dodge Brothers, and here the workmen, ten 
or a dozen boys, at $1.50 each and a foreman, fit the bodies on the 
machines, put the cushions in piace, put the tires on the wheels, the 
wheels on the machines and paint them and test them to see they 
run ‘O.K.,’ and it is all ready for delivery. Now this is all there is to 
the whole proposition. | 

Now, as to the investment feature. You will see there is absolutely 
no money to speak of tied up in a big factory. There is the $75 a 
month rent for three years, and a few machines necessary in the as- 
sembling factory. All the rest is done outside and supplied as or- 
dered, and this, of course, is a big saving in capital outlay to start 
with. 

The machines sell for $750, without a tonneau. With a tonneau 
the price is $850. This is the price of all medium-priced machines and 
is standard. It is what the Cadillac and Great Northern sell for here, 
and what other machines elsewhere sell for. Now the cost, figured in 
the most liberal possible estimate, is as follows:

THE FORD MOTOR COMPANY OI 


$97,500, without a tonneau, and more in proportion to those sold with 
tonneaus.... ; 

Now, the demand for automobiles is a perfect craze. Every 
factory here (there are three including the Olds — the largest in 
the country — and you know Detroit is the largest automobile 
center in the United States), has its entire output sold and can- 
not begin to fill orders. Mr. Malcomson has already begun to be 
deluged with orders, although not a machine has been put on the 
market and will not be until July 1.... And it is all spot cash on de- 
livery, and no guarantee or string attached of any kind. 


The Ford Motor Company was a business success from 
the start. Sales for the three-and-one-half-month period be- 
tween June 16 and September 30, 1903, totaled $132,482, 
and resulted in a net profit of $36,958. On October 1, 1903, 
a dividend of 2 per cent was paid; a month later, 10 per cent; 
in January, 1904, 20 per cent; and six months later, 68 per 
cent.? Ina little more than fifteen months from the date of 
incorporation, the Ford Motor Company had paid dividends 
totaling 100 per cent of the issued capital stock. 
~ As soon as commercial production had been started, the 
Company began the construction of special racing cars, for 
the widespread popular interest in automobile racing caused 
great publicity to be given to enterprises producing the win- 
ning cars, and ‘practically every noteworthy automobile 
company entered its cars in the races.’3 Henry Ford per- 
sonally piloted the Ford racers in a number of national con- 
tests, and later delegated this task to Barney Oldfield, one 
of the most successful of the professional racing pilots. 

During its first year of operation, the Company produced 
a single model, selling at $950 f.o.b. Detroit. During the 
second year, influenced partly by the enthusiasm for racing, 

‘perhaps, and partly by the importunities of some of the 
‘stockholders,4 the Company produced three models, one of 
which was a large six-cylinder motor car selling at $2000;

3. HENRY Forp’s ACQUISITION OF CONTROL 


During this year internal dissension among the stock- — 
holders resulted in the purchase by Henry Ford of the 255 
shares of stock held by Alexander Y. Malcomson. A short 
time later, Ford purchased the 10 shares of C. J. Woodall; 
and in 1907, he purchased 65 of the 150 shares sold by three 
other small stockholders, bringing his holdings up to 57 per 
cent of the Company’s issued stock. James Couzens pur- 
chased the other 85 shares sold at this time. In the mean 
time, the death of John S. Gray, president of the Company, — 
resulted in the transfer of his stock to the John S. Gray 
Estate and in the election of Henry Ford to the presidency 
of the Company. 

During its fourth fiscal year, ended September 30, 1907, the 
Company discontinued its expensive models and produced 
two types of low-priced vehicles, retailing at $600 and $700, 
respectively. Striking gains in production, receipts, and 
profits accompanied the change: output jumped from 1599 
to 8759; receipts, from $1,491,626 to $5,773,851; and profits, 
from $102,398 to $1,124,675. The following year, however, 
the Company reintroduced its $2000 model, and sold its 
smaller cars at $700 and $750. Its output suffered by the 
change, but its profits increased. The results of the Com- 
pany’s operations during its first five years are summarized 
in Table 19. 

The successful character of the Company’s operations is 
readily reflected by the table. In addition to the profits re- 
invested in the business, which caused the Company’s net 
worth to grow from less than $100,000 to more than $2,000,- 
000 in five years, the Company had paid dividends approxi-

4. EXPANSION 


With the year 1909, the Ford Motor Company began a 
remarkable series of strides in production, receipts from 
sales, and in profits, which placed it far in the forefront of all 
motor-car manufacturers. 

In the latter part of 1908, the Company contracted for 
the services of Walter E. Flanders, then an outstanding ex- 
pert in machine production, with the view of greatly aug- 
menting its output. Henry Ford had designed a new auto- 
mobile model — the famous ‘ Model T,’ and was prepared to 
initiate a radical program of quantity production. Flanders 
was to receive a bonus of $20,000 in addition to his salary if 
the Company’s output in the year ending September 30, 
1909, reached 10,000 cars.? The actual output was 10,660, 
the Ford Motor Company being the first automobile manu- 
facturer to achieve such a volume of output. 

During the next five years, the business of the Company 
increased by leaps and bounds. Its annual output was 
multiplied by seventeen; its net profits increased by nearly 
goo per cent; and, despite the payment of special dividends 
aggregating $15,000,000 in addition to regular dividends of 
60 per cent annually,3 the net worth of the Company in- 
creased by more than 1300 per cent. The results of its opera- 
tions during these five years are summarized in Table 20. 


5. THE SELDEN PATENT LITIGATION 


The growth of the Ford Motor Company up to the year 
1911 took place during the pendency of the bitterly contested 
patent suit brought against the Company by the owners of 
the Selden patent. The Ford Motor Company, then just be-

ginning its career, had refused to join the Association of 
Licensed Automobile Manufacturers, and suit for infringe- 
ment of the Selden patent had been started on October 21, 
1903. After six years of strenuous litigation, as we have 
noted, the Selden patent was sustained in every particular 
by the United States Circuit Court of the Southern District 
of New York.t The Association of Licensed Automobile 
Manufacturers immediately instituted a campaign of news- 
paper advertising, warning the public against the purchase 
of Ford cars, and asserting that every owner of a Ford car 
was liable for damages. The Ford Motor Company coun- 
tered this attack by advertisements announcing that it had 
deposited a bond with a New York trust company to protect 
its customers; and it appealed from the decision to the Cir- 
cuit Court of Appeals. A reversal of the decision appeared to 
be unlikely, for the opinion of Judge Hough had been ren- 
dered only after one of the lengthiest patent suits in Ameri- 
can history. 

The Ford Motor Company, during the year previous to 
Judge Hough’s decision, had entered into preliminary ne- 
gotiations with Messrs. William C. Durant, of the Buick 
Motor Car Company, Benjamin Briscoe, of the Maxwell- 
Briscoe Motor Company, and Ransom E. Olds, of the Reo 
Motor Car Company, for the formation of a combination of 
motor-car manufacturers; but had dropped the negotiations

THE FORD MOTOR COMPANY 97 


when its demand for a cash payment of $3,000,000 had been 
refused.t Shortly after the Company’s reverse in the patent 
litigation, however, Durant, on behalf of the newly organized 
General Motors Company, as we have observed,” succeeded 
in obtaining an option, dated October 25, 1909, to purchase 
all of the capital stock of the Ford Motor Company for 
$8,000,000, of which $2,000,000 was to be paid in cash, and 
the remainder, in one- and two-year notes. Durant allowed 
the option to lapse because he could not obtain the funds for 
the initial payment. The possible subsequent history of the 
Ford enterprise under the auspices of the General Motors 
Company is a tempting subject for speculation. The de- 
cision of Judge Hough, however, was reversed by the Cir- 
cuit Court of Appeals in January, 1911;3 the chief obstacle 
in the path of the meteoric rise of the Company was thus 
eliminated. 

The Selden patent suit strained the resources of the Ford 
Motor Company at times,‘ and hence may have retarded its 
growth. Nevertheless, the records disclose no sign of this: 
while the litigation was at its height, the Company was 
emerging as the leading producer of automobiles in the 
world. Even an adverse decision might not have had serious 
consequences for the Company. In the opinion of James 
Couzens, then and for many years vice-president of the 


Company, a license under the patent would have been 
easily obtainable;5 the royalty rate had never exceeded 1.5,

at the time of the decision, the rate was only four-fifths of 
one per cent; the bulk of the royalties, moreover, were pay-
