6. SALES AND OPERATING POLICIES 

The great spurts in the business of the Ford Motor Com- 
pany during the five years beginning with 1909 were stimu- 
lated by successive reductions in the price of the product, by 
a more aggressive sales policy, and by improvements in 
manufacturing organization. 

The initial price of the ‘Model T’ in 1909 was $950. In 
1910, the price was reduced to $780, and sales jumped from 
19,051 to 34,979 cars. In 1911, the price was reduced to 
$690, and sales nearly doubled, reaching 76,150 vehicles. 
In the following year, with the price cut to $600, sales 
totaled 181,951; and in 1913, with the price reduced to $550, 
sales increased to 264,972 cars. 

Soon after its organization, the Company had established 
branch retail stores in the larger cities. These were now 
eliminated, and the number of independent sales dealers 
was greatly increased. By March 1, 1913, the Company 
had one or more sales agents in every city or town of 1000 
population or more in the United States. Branch sales 
offices were established by the Company in various parts 
of the country to supervise and to stimulate the efforts of 
the dealers. Among the general instructions issued to the 
latter, was the following: ? 

A dealer or salesman ought to have the name of every possible 
automobile buyer in his territory, including all those who have never 
given the matter a thought. He should then personally solicit by 
visitation if possible — by correspondence at the least — every man 
on that list and then making necessary memoranda, know the auto- 


mobile situation as related to every man so solicited. If your terri- 
tory is too large to permit this, you have too much territory. 


* Testimony of Norval A. Hawkins, sales manager of the Company 
in 1913, before the United States Board of Tax Appeals, February 1, 
1927. 

* From Henry Ford, My Life and Work, p. 60. 


THE FORD MOTOR COMPANY 99 


Every dealer was required to maintain a stock of Ford 
parts sufficient to make prompt replacements, and to keep 
every Ford car in his territory in active service; to maintain 
a thoroughly equipped repair shop operated by skilled 
mechanics; a comprehensive bookkeeping system; a ‘follow- 
up’ sales system; and suitable signs, quarters, and publicity.* 
When the seasonal character of the demand for automobiles 
began to create problems of production, warehousing, and 
credit, for the Company, a campaign of ‘education,’ by 
newspaper advertisement and letters, was prosecuted to 
convert both dealers and consumers to the policy of dis- 
tributing purchases throughout the year.? Dealers were re- 
quired to submit orders by months for a year in advance, the 
unfilled orders being subject to cancellation upon a month’s 
written notice; and the strong influence of the Company 
was exerted upon the dealers to anticipate their spring and 
summer requirements by stocking up during the winter 
months. 7 

Improvements in manufacturing organization; as well as 
price reductions and an improved selling organization, con- 
tributed to the great growth of the Company’s business from 
1909 to 1914. In 1906, the original quarters of the Company 
had been deserted for a three-story factory building, the 
construction of which had been financed by funds taken from 
the Company’s working capital. Here, the Company had 
begun to manufacture a few of the parts of its products, and 
to do more of the assembling. In 1909, the Company pur- 
chased sixty acres of land in Highland Park, a suburb of 
Detroit, and constructed one of the largest manufacturing 
plants in the world. ‘To pay for the new lands and buildings, 
I slightly raised prices,’ wrote Mr. Henry Ford in his au- 
tobiography. 

In the new plant, the Company introduced many and 
radical improvements in factory and shop practice, innova- 


*From Henry Ford, My Life and Work, p. 60. 
2 Ibid., p. 166 ff. 3 Ibid., p. 73. 


100 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


tions which have since been widely copied.t In contrast 
with the prevailing practice in the automobile industry, the 
Company concentrated its already large and growing re- 
sources upon the manufacture of a single model (motor and 
chassis, on which were mounted several types of bodies). 
And, while other producers continued to make large financial 
outlays for annual changes in their numerous models, the 
Ford Motor Company standardized its product (making 
even small changes in design only infrequently), and em- 
ployed its resources for improvements in production tech- 
nique. 

The large volume of its output and its standardized pro- 
duct permitted the employment of the most specialized 
tools, machines, and processes. This specialization it ex- 
tended to its suppliers, most of whom came to make but 
a single component of the product. Whole independent 
organizations were moved to Detroit from other cities to 
take care of Ford orders. The Ford Motor Company pur- 
chased materials for its components-makers, reorganized 
their manufacturing processes, supervised their larger poli- 
cies, and, in some cases, aided them in financing production.? 
The Company became so dependent upon the production 
of its specialized suppliers that its own operations were fre- 
quently within thirty minutes of suspension because of 
tardy deliveries of parts or materials.3 

In its new plant, the Company gradually took over the 
direct production of more of the components. This limited 
integration proceeded slowly, however, for the Company 
needed the resources of its suppliers to sustain the volume of 
its output; and, in contrast with its more recent policy, the 
Company was not then averse to purchasing virtually all of 
its materials and parts from independent producers.4 Never- 


* For a full and technical discussion of the radical innovations in 
factory organization and operation initiated by the Ford Motor Com- 
pany, see Arnold and Faurote, Ford Methods and the Ford Shops. 

* Dodge v. Ford Motor Company, 1070 Mich. Sup. Ct. Briefs and 
Records. 


3 Ibid. 4Ford, My Life and Work, p. 52 ff. 


THE FORD MOTOR COMPANY 101 


theless, it found the business of parts-manufacture extremely 
profitable. ‘We were very careful to make the parts our- 
selves which wore out the quickest. .. . In the case of frames, 
we did not begin to make them ourselves for some years 
because they lasted so long,’ said Mr. Norval A. Hawkins, 
formerly sales manager of the Company.' Because parts- 
manufacture was so lucrative, and because supervision of 
independent concerns under the purchase system proved 
difficult, the Company gradually extended the range of its 
own manufacturing operations. 

And, whenever the Company took over the manufacture 
of any parts, ‘it always made them cheaper than the former 
makers.’? The Company had special machines and tools 
designed and constructed ‘to do just that one job, whereas 
the outside manufacturer had to consider other products 
with the same machine.’3 Thus, a bolt previously purchased 

from the Western Automatic Screw Machine Company at 
“$50 a thousand was produced directly by the Ford Motor 
Company at a cost of $8.70 a thousand, according to the 
Company’s books, during the first year of its manufacture of 
this bolt; a saving of $582,635 for the year resulted.4 Ana- 
logous savings were effected by the substitution of direct 
manufacture for purchase of transmissions, springs, push- 
rods, and other parts.s 

It is pertinent at this point to note briefly some of the 
operating policies introduced by the Company between 1909 
and 1914 that have been recognized as contributing greatly 
to its manufacturing efficiency.® 


t Testimony of Norval A. Hawkins, before the United States Board of 
Tax Appeals, in Washington, February 1, 1927. 

2 Dodge v. Ford Motor Company, 1070. Mich. Sup. Ct. Briefs and 
Records. 

3 Ibid. 4 Ibid. 

5’ Ibid. The direct manufacture of push-rods, previously purchased 
from the Michigan Bolt & Nut Works, effected a saving of $153,258 the 
first year that the Company undertook such manufacture; transmissions 
formerly purchased from Dodge Brothers were made directly at a saving 
of from $9 to $10 a car; the direct manufacture of front springs effected 
a saving of $86,171 the first year; etc. 

6 Cf. Arnold and Faurote, The Ford Methods and the Ford Shops. 


102 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


1. The minute subdivision of labor tasks and production 
processes, making possible the wide use of the machine- 
tool and of unskilled labor. Tasks previously requiring 
skilled workmen were broken up into many small operations, 
skill in any one of which could be acquired in a few hours; 
and tools and machines were substituted for virtually all 
hand operations. The importance of this practice is ap- 
preciated when it is recognized that the existence of a large 
class of skilled machinists and wood-workers in various 
localities early influenced the location of automobile fac- 
tories; yet the present scale of the Ford operations would be 
impossible did it depend upon skilled labor.t The specialized 
tool and machine; the subdivision and specialization of 
labor tasks, making possible the efficient use of all grades of 
labor; even the stimulus to invention that results from 
specialization ? — these applications of long-known theoret- 
ical advantages of large-scale production and specialization 
contributed much to the manufacturing efficiency of the 
Ford Motor Company. 

2. The economy of factory space and of working time by 
the use of internal factory trains, monorails, moving belts, 
and the ‘endless chain production line.’ The use of belts 
carrying materials above the floor released floor space. The 
use of trains and monorails throughout the factory for carry- 
ing materials permitted the placing of machines with re- 
ference solely to their use in the progressive assembling of 
the product, and eliminated walking and the carrying of 
materials by the workers. The ‘endless chain production 
line’ eliminated further carrying of materials because it 
permitted the product in process to be carried along from 
worker to worker and from machine to machine by power- 


* ‘Sufficiently skilled men to the number needed could not have been 
trained in a hundred years. A million men working by hand could not 
even approximate our present daily output. No one could manage a 
million men.’ (Henry Ford, My Life and Work, p. 78.) 

* Hundreds of suggestions emanating from the workmen, most of 
whom are foreign-born, as to changes in machines and as to new devices, 
have been incorporated by the Company. (Jbid., p. 100 ff.) 


THE FORD MOTOR COMPANY 103 


driven chains. The Ford Conveyor System — ‘which is 
responsible for a great part in the success of Ford quantity 
production’ *— has now been adopted by nearly all large 
automobile factories. 

3. The shifting of the final assembling of the product from 
the main manufacturing unit in Highland Park to a large 
number of branch assembling plants distributed throughout 
the United States and in foreign countries. This practice 
greatly facilitated the physical distribution of the enormous 
volume of product; economized factory space in the chief 
producing center; and effected tremendous savings in freight 
costs. 


During this period of rapid growth, the Ford Motor Com- 
pany was virtually free from effective competition in the 
field of low-priced automobiles. Potential competitors did 
appear; thus, the Olds Motor Works produced an automo- 
bile selling at $650 as early as 1900 and for three years there- 
after; and, in 1909, the Hupp Motor Car Corporation pro- 
duced a car selling at $750 as compared with the Ford at 
$950; but in each of these as in other cases the potential com- 
petitor deserted the field for that of higher-priced cars, or 
went out of business. The widespread conception of the 
automobile as a luxury, and the large profits per car in the 
higher-priced field served to divert the Company’s potential 
competitors. In addition, the restraint imposed by the As- 
sociation of Licensed Automobile Manufacturers, operating 
under the Selden patent (which Ford, alone, among the im- 
portant producers, refused to recognize), undoubtedly re- 
tarded the appearance of competitors. Before the influence 
of these factors had greatly diminished, the Ford Motor 
Company had become firmly established, and had already 
begun its policy of distancing potential competitors by pro- 
gressive reductions in the price of its product and by high 
technical efficiency. 

The expansion of the Company during this period ap- 

* Facts from Ford, 4th ed., p. 12, Ford Motor Company. 


10646 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


peared to be conditioned only by the physical limits of pro- 
duction. In addition to the favorable circumstances noted 
in the foregoing, the Company encountered none of the usual 
obstacles in matters of finance. The large profits of the en- 
terprise easily yielded the increasing sums required for fixed 
capital; and, because of the prevailing practices in the auto- 
mobile industry, whereby the Company received payment in 
cash for its product immediately upon delivery, while its 
suppliers of parts and materials extended the customary 
commercial credit of thirty or sixty days, its working capital 
requirements were remarkably small. 

On September 30, 1914, the Ford Motor Company pos- 
sessed aggregate assets of $59,801,836, of which $27,092,713 
consisted of cash, and was subject to total liabilities of only 
$5,926,610. During the year ended on that date it had paid 
out $11,000,000 in special dividends, in addition to the regu- | 
lar quarterly dividends totaling $1,200,000;? it had inau- 
gurated on January 1, of the year, an employees’ ‘profit- 
sharing’ plan providing for a minimum wage of $5 a day for 
adult employees, and entailing an increased annual expendi- 
ture of $10,000,000;3 and in July of that year it had an- 
nounced a ‘buyers’ profit-sharing agreement’ whereby ap- 
proximately $15,000,000 was to be returned to purchasers of 
its cars at the end of the ensuing year if a Company’s sales 
attained a stipulated volume.‘ 


7. THE DODGE ay 


During the same year the Company began plans for a vast 
program of expansion, involving the duplication of its High- 
land Park plant on a large site in River Rouge, Michigan, 
and the construction of blast furnaces, foundries, glass 
factories, and othér plants, with a view toward the integra- 

* Annual report of the Ford Motor Company, 1914, to the Michigan 
Secretary of State. 

* Dodge v. Ford Motor Company, 204 Mich., 464. 

3 Facts from Ford, p. 53, Ford Motor Company. 


‘ Dodge v. Ford Motor Company, 1070 Mich. Sup. Ct. Briefs and 
Records. 


THE FORD MOTOR COMPANY 105 


tion under its ownership of many of the industries necessary 
to its final product. In order to accumulate the funds re- 
quired for this program, the Company postponed a reduc- 
tion in the prices of its products during the year 1915-16; 
and, in consequence, its profits that year totaled $59,994,- 
918.* On August I, 1916, the Company announced a reduc- 
tion of $80 in the price of its touring car — from $440 to 
$360 — and proportionate reductions in the prices of its 
other models. The new prices represented a decrease of 
$40,000,000 in anticipated receipts if the volume of produc- 
tion and sales remained the same as in the year preceding.? 
The expectation was, of course, that the volume of output 
and sales would be greatly increased as a result of the lower 
prices. 

This sharp reduction in prices and the large expansion pro- 
jects of the Company, coupled with an announcement by 
Mr. Henry Ford on August 31, 1916, that the bulk of the 
profits of the Ford Motor Company would thenceforth be 
reinvested in the business of the Company, led to the filing 
of a suit by John F. Dodge and Horace E. Dodge, owners of 
ten per cent of its capital stock, to enjoin the Company from 
all further expansion, and to compel the distribution of ac- 
cumulated earnings held in cash and all future earnings.4 

The Dodge brothers had resigned from directorships in 
the Ford Motor Company and had ceased to manufacture 
parts for the Company in 1914, when they had organized a 
separate enterprise of their own for the manufacture of auto- 
mobiles. This company was now operating successfully as 
Dodge Brothers, Inc.; and the demand of the Dodges for 
a distribution of the profits of the Ford Motor Company may 
be supposed to have been motivated by the desire to expand 
their own business and to restrict the expansion of a com- 
petitor. 

The bill of complaint, filed on November 2, 1916, and 


t Ibid. 2 Ibid. 3 Detroit News, August 31, 1916. 
4 Bill of Complaint, Dodge v. Ford Motor Company, 1070 Mich. Sup. 
Ct. Briefs and Records. 


106 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


amended on April 26, 1917, charged the Ford Motor Com- 
pany with violation of the Michigan statute limiting the 
capital stock of a corporation to $25,000,000 (the legislature 
increased this limit to $50,000,000 during the hearing of the 
suit); with ultra-vires expansion projects, such as those for 
blast furnaces and foundries; with reckless price reductions 
and reckless plans for expansion; with domination of the 
Company by Henry Ford; with the sacrifice of the stock- 
holders’ interests for philanthropic purposes; and with an 
intent to monopolize the automobile business. 

The answer of the Ford Motor Company and of Henry 
Ford, James Couzens, and others who were made co-defend- 
ants, admitted the allegations of facts with respect to the 
Company’s profits and expansion projects, but denied all 
the equities in the bill of complaint. The answer cited the 
record of special dividends; referred to the unanimous vote 
of the stockholders on various decisions with respect to the 
Company’s expansion; declared that the extensions were 
made necessary by the inadequacy of the existing equipment 
to satisfy the demand for the Company’s products; as- 
serted that the reductions in the prices of its models were 
only in keeping with its traditional price policies; and de- 
clared that the maintenance of large cash balances was es- 
sential to the safety and well-being of the Company. 

The plaintiffs, on December 9, 1916, procured an injunc- 
tion from the Wayne County Circuit Court (Michigan), 
enjoining the Ford Motor Company from further expendi- 
tures on its expansion program. On appeal to the Supreme 
Court of Michigan the Ford Motor Company was granted 
an alternative order of mandamus against the Circuit Court 
judges, ordering the dissolution of the injunction and the 
substitution of a bond for $10,000,000 to protect the plain- 
tiffs against loss. 

Hearing of the suit began before Judge George S. Hosmer 
of the Circuit Court on May 21, 1917. The chief legal issues 
argued concerned the power of the Company to engage in 
the smelting of ore and other alleged wlira-vires activities, 


THE FORD MOTOR COMPANY Bey iy 


and the question whether the Michigan statutory limitation 
on the capital stock of a’corporation was synonymous with 
a limitation on the total value of property to be owned by 
a corporation. : 

The judge’s decision, delivered on December 5, 1917, was 
based exclusively on the second issue, and held that the 
statutory limitation on capital stock applied equally to 
property. The continued construction of the Ford blast 
furnaces was therefore enjoined as ‘an excessive investment 
beyond the legal limit,’ and, since the Ford Motor Com- 
pany’s investments already exceeded the legal limit, the 
Court held that the excess should be distributed as requested 
by the plaintiffs. The Court ordered that one-half of the 
earnings of the fiscal year ended July 31, 1916 — $29,500,000 
— less all dividends declared during the pendency of the 
suit, leaving $19,275,385.96, should be distributed as a 
special dividend. 

An appeal from this decision to the Michigan Supreme 
Court was filed on April 9, 1918; and additional briefs and 
testimony were presented. The Supreme Court, in a decision 
delivered on February 9, 1919," held that the statutory limi- 
tation on the capital stock of a corporation could not be con- 
strued as limiting subsequent additions to the property of 
a corporation through reinvestment of profits; and hence the 
decree of the lower court restraining the Ford Motor Com- 
pany from proceeding with its expansion program was re- 
versed. The Court held that the expansion projects of the 
Ford Motor Company were not ultra vires; and to the charge 
that the Company was intent upon a monopoly of the 
automobile manufacturing business, the Court replied: 

Where no monopoly will accrue to a corporation by reinvesting 
its profits in expanding its plant other than such as accrues to a con- 


cern which makes what the public demands, there is no violation of 
any anti-trust laws in such policy of expansion. 


Sustaining the contention of the plaintiffs, however, that 
profits were being retained by the Company for philan- 
* Dodge v. Ford Motor Company, 204 Mich., 402 ff. 


108 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


thropic uses at the expense of the stockholders, the Supreme 
Court upheld that part of the lower court’s decision which 
directed the payment of a special dividend. Two newspaper 
pronouncements of Mr. Henry Ford, the authenticity of 
which was admitted, and which provided the basis for the 
Court’s decision on this point, are here reproduced: 


My ambition is to employ still more men, to spread the benefits of 
this industrial system to the greatest possible number, to help them 
build up their lives and their homes. To do this we are putting the 
greatest share of our profits back into the business. .. . With regard 
to dividends, the company paid 60 per cent on its capitalization of 
$2,000,000, or $1,200,000, leaving $68,500,000 to reinvest for the 
growth of the company.? 

I have been fighting to hold down our income right along. ... We 
made an enormous profit last year because we needed the money for 
expansion and the benefit will eventually go to the buyer and the 
laborer. We could easily have maintained our prices for this year 
and cleaned up from 60 to 75 millions; but I don’t think it would 
have been right to do so, so we cut our prices and are now clearing 
$1,500,000 to $2,500,000 a month, which is all any firm ought to 
make — maybe more — unless, as I said, the money is to be used for 
expansion.? 


On this point, the Supreme Court declared: 


It is not within the lawful powers of a board of directors to shape 
and conduct the affairs of a corporation for the merely incidental 
benefit of the shareholders and for the primary purpose of benefiting 
others. ... Where a corporation would have a surplus of approxi- 
mately $30,000,000 after paying for approved expansion and im- 
provement of its plant, and could reasonably expect a profit of 
$60,000,000 a year, refusal by the board of directors to distribute 
extra dividends was arbitrary, and the decree of the court below de- 
termining that $19,275,385.96 (one-half of the cash surplus, less 
special dividends declared after the filing of the bill) should be dis- 
tributed as an extra dividend is affirmed.

During 1917 and 1918, a large part of the facilities of the

pany’s series of price reductions. The price of its touring car 
in 1913 was $550; in 1914, $490; in 1915, $440; in 1916, 
$360; in 1917, $450; and in 1918, $525. 


8. REORGANIZATION OF THE Forp Motor CoMPANY 


The decision of the Michigan Supreme Court in the Dodge 
‘case was widely construed as greatly strengthening the legal 
position of minority stockholders; and it caused Henry Ford 
to consider relinquishing active participation in the adminis- 
‘tration of the Ford Motor Company and to plan a new 
automobile enterprise in which he should possess entire 
control. Even before the suit had been finally adjudicated, 
Ford had organized a separate corporation, owned entirely 


* Annual reports to the Michigan Secretary of State. 


110 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


by himself, his wife, and his son, for the manufacture of an 
automotive farm tractor which he had designed. On Decem- 
ber 31, 1918, he resigned from the presidency of the Ford 
Motor Company, declaring that he desired to devote his 
time ‘to other organizations,’ and that he wished to be 
‘relieved of all responsibilities and obligations arising from 
holding a salaried office in the Company.’? A short time 
later, wide currency was given to a newspaper interview 
with Henry Ford in which he was reported to have an- 
nounced his intention to build a new and lower-priced 
automobile in a factory of his own.’ Instead of organizing 
this competing enterprise, however, Ford set out to acquire 
the minority stockholdings in the Ford Motor Company; 
and, as they allege, the minority stockholders were coerced 
into selling their stock to the Fords at unduly low prices be- 
cause of the threatened formation of a competing Ford 
company.‘ 

For the purpose of financing the purchase of the minority 
stock, the Fords organized two holding companies, Henry 
Ford & Son, Inc. (New York), and the Eastern Holding 
Company (Delaware). To the first. of these, they trans- 
ferred their stock in the Ford Motor Company (Michigan), 
save for directors’ qualifying shares. The Delaware corpora- 
tion, which had been incorporated on July 9, 1919, to deal 


* Henry Ford & Son, which was incorporated in Michigan in August, 
1917, with a capitalization of $1,000,000. By resolution of February 2,

name ‘Ford’ for the latter, with the proviso that the name ‘Henry’ pre- 
cede ‘Ford’ in the name of the tractor enterprise, and, further, that the 
Ford Motor Company be free to enter upon tractor manufacture upon 
payment of $46,810. (Dodge v. Ford Motor Company, 1070 Mich. Sup. 
Ct. Briefs and Records.) The tractor enterprise was subsequently taken 
over by the Ford Motor Company.

In the consolidation of the Ford interests into a family 
corporation, no attempt was made to purchase the mi- 
nority stock ownership in the Ford Motor Company of 
Canada, Ltd., or in other of the associated Ford companies 
incorporated in foreign countries. The American company 
controls these by means of working agreements and by 
means of stock ownership held in the names of the American 
company’s stockholders; and it aids in financing them by 
deferring claims for parts and materials supplied them.! 
The Canadian company, the largest of the associated Ford 
companies, had a net worth on July 31, 1926, of $35,552,- 
579, all of which, except for the original investment of $125,- 
000 (half of which was contributed by the American com- 
pany in the form of franchise rights) had been built up by the 
reinvestment of profits.? The officers of the Canadian com- 
pany include (1927) Henry Ford, president, and Edsel B. 
Ford, vice-president. The stock of the Company is listed 
on the Detroit Stock Exchange and occasionally enjoys an 
active market.

organization was followed by a vigorous renewal of the 
policy of expansion. The blast furnaces, foundries, paper 
and glass factories, and other plants constructed on the 
River Rouge site were rapidly put into full operation, and 
the Company began to acquire holdings of timber and ore 
lands, coal mines, water-power sites, and other primary 
sources of power and materials. Its investment in real estate, 
plant, machinery and equipment, in 1918 was $66,453,345; 
in 1919 (July 31), $79,188,320; and on April 30, 1920, $127,- 
210,862. During the calendar year of 1919, the Company’s 
net profits total $69,924,411;? and during the calendar year 
1920, the production of cars and trucks (a light commercial 
truck had been added to the Company’s line of products 
early in 1918) broke all previous records, totaling 1,074,336.5 
An output of 1,250,000 cars and trucks was planned for the 
year 1921. 


9. THE CRISIS OF 1920-21 


But it was during this year that the Ford Motor Company 
was faced with the greatest, and, so far as the records dis- 
close, the only, financial crisis in its history. 

The post-war boom in general business had shown signs 
of imminent collapse early in 1920, and, as summer came, 
the automobile business experienced a marked slackening 
in sales. The business of the Ford Motor Company slumped 
with that of others. On September 21, 1920, President Edsel 
B. Ford announced a reduction in the prices of the Ford 
products, that of the touring car being cut from $575 to 
$440, and other models being similarly reduced. The new 
prices meant a loss on the immediate orders of the Com- 
pany, according to the announcement, because of the large 
volume of inventories purchased at the high prices pre- 
viously prevailing; but the reductions were made to stimu- 


* Reports of Ford Motor Company; balance sheets reproduced in 
Moody’s Industrials. 


* Benson, The New Henry Ford, p. 144. 
§ Facts from Ford, p. 29, issued by the Ford Motor Company.

late sales. A short spurt in the business of the Company fol- 
lowed the price cuts, but sales soon fell off again. 

In view of the period of liquidation in general business 
which was then setting in throughout the country, the 
vulnerable financial position of the Ford Motor Company 
at this time may be seen from a survey of its balance sheet 
of December 31, 1920:?

In spite of the new insertion of a good-will item of $21,- 
262,833 in the list of assets, the net worth of the Company 
as exhibited by the balance sheet above showed a decrease 
of more than $60,000,000 as compared with the net worth 
on July 31, 1919. Liability on unsecured indebtedness had 
increased from $96,271,959 to $143,025,301; credits had 
decreased from $96,182,704 to $54,438,634; cash had de- 
creased from $59,829,279 to $13,557,245; and tangible goods 
other than real estate had increased from $88,506,436 to 
$124,350,295. 

In this situation, the Company took radical steps, open 
only to a concern of its size, to force down the prices of raw 
materials and of manufactured parts, and thus to permit it 
to stimulate its sales by further price reductions. Cognizant 
of the effect of its action on many firms of which it was the 
sole or chief customer, the Company suddenly ceased pur- 
chases of raw materials and parts; and, while maintaining 
its production at highest pitch, it made preparations for 
a sudden shut-down of the great Highland Park plant so 
soon as the accumulated inventories of materials had been 


t Annual report to the Michigan Secretary of State. 


116 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


turned into finished products. In justifying the action of 
his Company, Henry Ford is quoted as having said:* 


I knew something drastic had to be done, and was determined to 
do it. When the war ended, the excuse for war prices ended, but 
those who sold us raw materials did not seem to know it. 


Late in December, 1920, after its inventories had been 
made up into finished parts and shipped to its thirty-five 
branch assembling units throughout the country, the Com- 
pany suspended operations at its Highland Park plant — 
first, for two weeks, and then, ‘indefinitely.’ 

During January, all the assembled cars were shipped from 
the Company’s branches to the more than 10,000 Ford 
dealers, draft bills of lading attached. About 125,000 cars 
were thus marketed, and much of the burden of emergency 
financing was thus shifted to the dealers, who were re- 
quested to take up the cars under their annual quota-ar- 
rangement with the Company. At the same time, foreign 
agents were called upon to remit $3,000,000 on outstanding 
accounts; $3,700,000 was realized from the sale of by- 
products of the Ford operations; and $7,900,000 from the 
sale of Liberty bonds.? After six weeks of shutdown, the 
Highland Park plant was reopened for operation. 

Striking economies were now effected in the productive 
operations. The office force was cut in half; all order blanks 
and statistical forms that did not aid directly in production 
were abolished; sixty per cent of the-telephone extensions 
were removed; the number of foremen was cut to one-fourth 
of the previous number (one foreman being now employed 
for every twenty men instead of one for every five men); 
the number of employees per car per day was reduced from 
fifteen to nines ‘We cut the overhead charge from $146 
to $93 a car, and when you realize what this means on more

than four thousand cars a day you will have an idea how... 
it is possible to make an impossible price,’ wrote Mr. Henry 
Ford, in discussing these changes, in his autobiography.* 
Continuing, he declared: 


Most important of all, we found out how to use less money in our 
business by speeding up the turnover. And in increasing the turn- 
over rate, one of the most important factors was the Detroit, Toledo, 
& Ironton Railroad — which we purchased. ... We discovered... 
that freight service could be improved sufficiently to reduce the cycle 
of manufacture from twenty-two to fourteen days. That is, raw 
material could be bought, manufactured and the finished product 
put into the hands of the distributor in (roughly) 33 per cent less time 
than before. We had been carrying an inventory of around $60,000,- 
000 to insure uninterrupted production. Cutting down the time one- 
third released $20,000,000. .. . Counting the finished inventory, we 
saved approximately $8,000,000 more — that is, we were able to re- 
lease $28,000,000 in capital....Qn January 1 (1921), we had 
$20,000,000. On April 1, we had $87,300,000, or $27,300,000 more 
than we needed to wipe out all our indebtedness.? 


The success of the Ford Motor Company in extricating 
itself from financial embarrassment without the customary 
expedients of long- or short-term borrowing provoked wide- 
spread comment. While operating economies played a large 
role in this success, an equally important one was that in- 
volved in the shifting of the burden of credit to the Com- 
pany’s sales dealers and suppliers. When other firms were 
facing acute embarrassment by reason of the sudden cancel- 


t Page 173. 

2 Control of the Detroit, Toledo & Ironton Railroad was acquired by 
Henry Ford on July 9, 1920, by the purchase of approximately 97 per 
cent of the outstanding securities of the road, according to an announce- 
ment by Fosburgh, Pratt & Osborn, of New York, who negotiated the 
sale. (Cf. Commercial and Financial Chronicle, vol. 111, p. 294.) The 
total price was approximately $5,000,000 and was paid at the rate of 
$600 per $1000 bond; $5 per share of preferred stock; and $1 per share of 
common stock. The road was acquired ‘because its right of way inter- 
fered with some of our improvements on the River Rouge. We did not 
buy it as an investment, or as an adjunct to our industries, or because of 
its strategic position.... We bought the railway because it interfered 
with our plans. Then we had to do something with it.’ (Henry Ford, My 
Life and Work, p. 224.) | 


118 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


lation of orders by their customers, the Ford Motor Com- 
pany prevailed upon its dealers to accept shipments (sight 
drafts attached) in advance of orders; and, at the same time, 
persuaded its suppliers of parts and materials to extend 
credit. The power of the Company to employ such expe- 
dients in a period of acute distress in general business can be 
explained only by the extraordinary profitableness to its 
dealers and suppliers of their relations with the Ford 
enterprise. 


IO. RECOVERY AND INTEGRATION 


The Ford Motor Company emerged from its embarrass- 
ment with a greatly improved manufacturing organization 
and in a liquid financial condition. In the six months be- 
tween December 31, 1920, and June 30, 1921, its cash and 
bank balances rose from $13,557,245 to $54,844,538; its 
current liabilities (including reserves and accruals) declined 
from $143,025,301 to $89,417,027.2 The fruits of its new 
manufacturing economies were strikingly displayed in the 
years immediately following. The price of the Ford touring 
car was reduced from $440 to $355 in 1921; the Company’s 
net profits in this year of depression totaled $75,890,836.3 
In 1922, the Company’s net worth increased by more than 
$133,000,000; 4 and in 1923, with the price of its touring car 
reduced to $295, the Company’s output and sales exceeded 
2,000,000 vehicles — the largest volume it has yet achieved.

Looking forward to the future with undiminished con- 
fidence, the Company made great strides in its policy of 
industrial integration and in further expanding its produc- 
tive capacity. On February 4, 1922, at a receiver’s sale, it 
paid $8,000,000 for the plants and other assets of the Lincoln 
Motor Company, producer of high-priced automobiles; in 
December, the Michigan Land & Lumber Company, a sub- 
sidiary, purchased 30,000 acres of timber property, including 
sawmill and logging railroad; in February, 1923, the Com- 
pany acquired the Allegheny Plate Glass Company, of 
Glassmere, Pennsylvania; in the same month, the Fordson 
Coal Company was incorporated in Delaware with a capital 
stock of $15,000,000 to take over coal properties in West. 


120 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


Virginia and Kentucky; in June, the Company obtained per- 
mission from the Federal Power Commission to develop 
electric power at the high dam between St. Paul and Min- 
neapolis, for a new $10,000,000 manufacturing unit; in 
September, 400,000 acres were added to the holdings of its 
timber-owning subsidiary; in October, the factories of the 
Johannson Gauge Company, of Sweden and of Poughkeepsie, 
New York, were purchased; etc.? In 1924, 1925, and 1926, 
large additions were made to the blast furnaces, rolling 
mills, and other units of its River Rouge plants; new as- 
sembling factories were opened in Dallas, Texas; Somerville, 
Massachusetts; Copenhagen, Denmark; Plozensee, Ger- 
many; Yokohama, Japan; and in several other places. Im- 
portant additions were made to a number of foreign and 
domestic manufacturing branches; tire, battery, and textile 
manufacture were introduced into the Company’s Highland 
Park plant; and, among various other things, the Company 
began manufacture of airplanes, through the Stout Metal 
Airplane Company, a subsidiary.’ 


11. THE NEw Forp CAR 


Its vast program of expansion was not accompanied, how- 
ever, by a corresponding growth in output and sales. In 
each of the three years after 1923, declines rather than gains 
in output were recorded:

In 1924 and 1925, the Company introduced a number of 
changes in its models: the body-lines were redesigned for 
greater grace; the traditional black finish gave way to lac- 
quer paints in red, green, and gray; a few internal improve- 
ments were made; and new accessories were added. These 

* Standard Statistics Company, Inc., Miscellaneous Companies, 1923 
to 1927. 

* Ibid. | 
3 Figures for 1923, from The Ford Industries, published by the Com- 
pany; the others, from Moody's Manual of Industrials, 1927. 


THE FORD MOTOR COMPANY 121 


small innovations, tardily incorporating only a few of the 
refinements previously introduced by most other manu- 
facturers, were made only with great difficulty, expense, and 
delay; and they did not suffice to regain for the Company its 
lost momentum of ever-growing sales. As demand continued 
to remain stationary or to decline, the Company adopted the 
five-day week for the bulk of its employees, in order to 
maintain the size of its working force against an expected 
renewal of growth;' a little later, most of its factory workers 
were given only four, three, or even two days of employment 
per week. Hourly and daily wage-rates were raised some- 
what to compensate the workers for diminished employment, 
but most of such wage increases were conditioned by, and 
proportioned to, increased productivity per worker.? The 
sales of other producers, on the other hand, continued to 
expand: in 1925 and in 1926, new high records of output were 
made by the American automobile industry. 

The enormous material and technical resources of the 
Ford Motor Company had, perhaps, too long been directed 
at the single task of augmenting and perfecting its manu- 
facturing organization. Too little attention had been paid 
to the changing character of the automobile market in 
recent years. During the long period when the bulk of 
automobile demand had come from first-time purchasers, 
the combination of cheapness, reliability, and practical 
utility had given the Ford car an unrivaled supremacy; now, 
a more discriminating market demanded that these elements 
be supplemented by minor technical refinements, and by 
various concessions in matters of color, fittings, body-design, 
accessories, etc. Extensive new construction and improve- 
ment of roads had greatly increased the pleasure use of the 
automobile; the relative importance of speed had grown. 

t Henry Ford, in various published interviews, explained the five-day 
week as a measure designed to promote efficiency and to give workmen 
greater leisure. 

2 See announcement of the Company, November 13, 1926, paraphrased 


by the Standard Statistics Company, Inc., in the latter’s Miscellaneous 
Companies, 1927. 


122 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


In a ‘pleasure car,’ moreover, elements of ‘style’ are mat- 
ters of great marketing importance. The products of other 
manufacturers, less secure in their ‘price-classes,’ had un- 
dergone progressive and conspicuous refinement, and much 
effort had been made to enhance their marketing appeal 
by external embellishment of various kinds. The Ford 
Motor Company had lagged conspicuously behind other 
producers in accommodating its product to the changing 
tastes of the public. Its essential philosophy of manufac- 
ture, to which all its vast resources had long been devoted, 
and to which it owed much of its remarkable success, 
rested securely upon the quantity production of a stand- 
ardized, substantially unchanging, automobile. Only in 
the technique of production did its massive, centralized 
organization exhibit ready flexibility and elasticity. 

Even in the field of price-competition, moreover, the Com- 
pany was losing some of the pronounced advantage that it 
had long enjoyed. A number of other important producers 
had introduced attractive automobiles retailing at various 
prices ranging downward from $1000 to little more than 
$500, and these cars had found an expanding market. The 
unit sales of three of such low-priced vehicles (one of which, 
the Chevrolet, retails for very little more than the Ford car) 
in the five years ended in 1926 were as follows:*

The competitive advantage of the lower prices of the Ford 
models was diminished also by the great development of the 
installment-payment practice. The initial cash payment 
required for the purchase of other cars became relatively

little more than that required for the Ford; and the small 
differences between the operating costs of the lowest- and 
those of somewhat higher-priced automobiles further re- 
dounded to the advantage of other producers. 

Tardily appreciative of considerations such as these, the 
Ford Motor Company, in the latter part of 1926, finally de- 
cided to discontinue the production of its famous ‘Model 
T,’ and to replace it with a brand new Ford car designed 
to possess the speed, beauty, and appointments that have 
latterly become important to automobile purchasers. 

The new car, according to President Edsel B. Ford, will 
be produced ‘in greater quantities than ever before.’! If this 
expectation is to be realized, however, it may be ventured, 
the Company must be prepared to change its models with 
far greater frequency than in the past. The ‘style’ element 
in motor cars appears to be increasing rather than diminish- 
ing in importance; and the competition of other producers, 
no longer merely potential even so far as price is concerned, 
promises to grow. The domestic automobile market, at 
least, is rapidly approaching stabilization; and in a market 
dominated by the replacement demand, a standardized ve- 
hicle, however attractive, which is produced in enormous 
quantities, must encounter great marketing difficulties.? 
Grave technical obstacles, moreover, confront an attempt 
to introduce frequent changes in a uniform product that is 
produced in quantities of 2,000,000 or more a year. The 
relatively slight changes in the Ford models in 1924 and 
1925 occasioned much difficulty and delay; and the vast 
Ford plants were nearly at a standstill throughout the 
greater part of 1927, pending completion of the design of the 


« Cf. various newspaper announcements; among others, those quoted 
in the New York Times of May 26, 1927, and the Detroit Free Press of 
June 1, 1927. 

2 Since this was written, the new Ford car has appeared, embodying 
numerous refinements in construction, appearance, and equipment. 
Making further concessions to the new character of the automobile mar- 
ket, the Company is offering each of the six body styles in four different 
color schemes.

new car and factory readjustments for its production. A 
considerable degree of inelasticity appears to be an inexor- 
able penalty of great and highly centralized organization.* 

A realization of the impending stabilization of the auto- 
mobile market may have been partly responsible for the 
program of industrial integration carried on by the Ford 
Motor Company: such integration, alone, perhaps, could 
provide adequate outlet for its enormous resources. And it 
is easily possible that, in the near future, the Company will 
find that a single standardized automobile will no longer 
suffice to absorb its productive facilities: like the General 
Motors, Studebaker, Nash, Dodge, Willys-Overland, Reo, 
Hudson, and other producers, it may yet be forced by mar- 
keting considerations to introduce a wide variety of auto- 
mobile products.? 


12. SUMMARY OF THE FINANCING OF THE ForpD Motor 
COMPANY 


The foregoing account of the corporate history of the 
Ford Motor Company has necessarily reflected the Com- 
pany’s financial history and financial policies; hence, little 
more than a summary and analysis of these aspects remains 
to be presented. 

The Ford Motor Company has always been a close corpo- 
ration, and its policies, operating and financial, have con- 
spicuously reflected the desires and judgments of its founder, 
Henry Ford, and, toa lesser degree, those of James Couzens.3 
On the operating side, this has been responsible for the Com- 
pany’s specialization in the field of low-priced vehicles, pro- 

t The producing units of the General Motors Corporation, it may be 


noted, possess separate plants and technical staffs, and possess a large 
measure of autonomous control over their operations. 

2 The Lincoln enterprise, though not acquired specifically for this pur- 
pose, may be regarded as a step in this direction. 

3 Horace Rackham, one of the original Ford stockholders, and a co- 
defendant in the Dodge suit, declared in a deposition, ‘This defendant 
states that the policy of the Ford Motor Company during its entire 
history has been suggested and directed by Henry Ford and James 
Couzens, up to the resignation of said Couzens’ (1919). 


THE FORD MOTOR COMPANY 125 


duced in a standardized pattern and in tremendous quan- 
tities; and, on the financial side, it has caused the policies of 
the Company to reflect its founder’s repugnance to all kinds 
of direct borrowing.? Partly in consequence of this attitude 
toward borrowing, partly because of factors common to the 
automobile industry as a whole, and partly asa result of the 
extraordinary profitableness of the Company’s operations, 
the Ford Motor Company presents the striking case of a 
great and far-flung business enterprise that has consist- 
ently financed its operations and growth out of its own earn- 
ings, without resort to long- or short-term borrowing. 

Turning first to the provision of fixed capital, we may 
repeat, what has several times been observed in the fore- 
- going, that at the outset of the Company’s history no great 
amount of capital was necessary to engage in the automo- 
bile manufacturing business. The automobile was a product 
_of several thousand parts, each of which could be and was 
manufactured by a host of metal-working and wood-working 
shops. The assembling of the automobile was a relatively 
simple process requiring no elaborate machinery and no 
special plant, and virtually all of the assembling, also, could 
be and was completed by independent shops working under 
contracts made in advance. The bulk of the fixed capital 
required for the manufacture of the automobile could be 
obtained by diverting to this employment plant and ma- 
chinery already in existence and owned by numerous con- 
cerns engaged in the business of making wood and metal 
products. 

Thus the Ford Motor Company at its beginning was faced 
by no large problems of fixed capital. It rented small 
quarters in the rear of a carpenter’s shop, and ordered from 
others the manufacture of the parts and most of the assemb- 
ling of its product. Three years elapsed before the Company 
found it necessary to move to a new plant. Then it con- 


« ‘Mr. Ford’s policy was to keep the company in such condition that 
it would never be necessary to borrow any money whatsoever.’ (Rack- 
ham, tbid.) 


126 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


structed a three-story building and devoted it almost en- 
tirely to assembling operations. On October 1, 1905, the 
Company valued its total machinery and equipment at 
$28,671; and two years later, on September 30, 1907, when 
its net worth had increased to $1,038,822, its real estate 
employed in the business was valued at $21,733, and its 
buildings, machinery and equipment at $238,778. 

The fixed investment of the Company expanded rapidly 
in the years following, but this expansion was financed 
solely by the reinvestment of profits. The unusual notion 
that investments made by the stockholders through the 
corporate reinvestment of profits differed radically from in- 
vestments made with borrowed money, in that the former 
did not subject the business to a continuing charge whereas 
the latter did, is thus expressed by Henry Ford in discussing 
the purchase of the Highland Park site and the construction 
of the new plants:! 


In order to pay for the new land and buildings, I slightly raised 
prices. I did exactly the same thing a few years ago — or rather, in 
that case I did not lower the prices as is my annual custom — in 
order to build the River Rouge plant. The extra money might in 
each case have been had by borrowing, but then we should have had 
a continuing charge upon the business and all subsequent cars would 
have had to bear this charge. 


The confidence with which the Ford Motor Company faced 
the project of expending approximately $40,000,000 for new 
plants and equipment at River Rouge, Michigan, was re- 
flected in the testimony of C. Harold Wills, then its chief 
engineer, who, when the wisdom of the expenditures was 
called into question in the Dodge suit, declared that the 
economies resulting from the new plant would be sufficient 
to pay for the entire cost of construction in little more than 
one year. In the mean time, the cost was to be defrayed 
from profits retained in the form of cash. 

The remarkable liquidity of the operations of the Ford 
Motor Company throughout its history may be seen from 

* Ford, My Life and Work, pp. 73-74. 


THE FORD MOTOR COMPANY 127° 


Tables 23 and 24. Table 23 presents the net worth of the 
Ford Motor Company at the beginning of each fiscal year; 
its receipts from sales during the year; and its profits for 
the year. Table 24 shows the ratio of receipts to net worth 
and the ratio of profits to net worth for each year. 

Two facts which bear directly upon the financing of 
the Company are strikingly reflected by these tables. In 
the first place, the high ratios of receipts to net worth 
during the earlier years of the Company’s existence reveal 
the fact that the amount of invested capital required was 
relatively small as compared with the volume of business. 
Measuring the turnover of its invested capital by the ratio 
of receipts to net worth, we find that the average turnover 
of the net invested capital of the Company during the first 
ten years of its history was eight times a year.? In the second 
place, the annual profits of the Company were more than 


adequate to finance its. great expansion directly out of earn- 


ings. The absolute size of these profits is shown in Table 
23, and their potency when reinvested in the business should 
be considered in the light of the rapid turnover of the Com- 
pany’s capital. Solely through such reinvestment of earn- 
ings, the Company’s net worth rose from a nominal $100,000 
to nearly $17,000,000 during its first ten years of operations. 
During this period, moreover, greater sums were disbursed 
to its stockholders than were reinvested in the business. The 
dividend record of the Ford Motor Company, so far as the 
data are available, is presented in Table 25. 

The working-capital requirements of the Ford Motor 
Company were minimized by factors common, as we have 
observed, to all American automobile producers. The great 
demand for automobiles and the large profits available for 
sales agents, in the early years of‘the industry and of the 
Ford enterprise, enabled the producers to exact substantial

creased during the year; like other similar tables, those just presented 
are somewhat inaccurate on this ground.

advance deposits of cash for all orders and to require cash 
payment upon delivery of the vehicles (sight draft being 
attached, usually, to the bill of lading). The suppliers of 
parts and materials, on the other hand, extended book-ac- 
count credit of thirty to ninety days. Thus the automobile 
producer had a month or more in which to assemble and 
sell his vehicles before bills from suppliers became due; and 
much of his labor costs could be paid from dealers’ deposits. 
Since the assembling process was short, and sales, imme- 
diate, the manufacturer could maintain a liquid condition 
without possessing a great amount of working capital and 
without excessive dependence upon banking accommoda- 
tion. Senator James Couzens, in a letter to the present 
writer (previously quoted in part), declared:! 

As I have stated I do not recall that we asked any credit from the 
banks in the early years, because in addition to the advance deposits 
made by the dealers, the industry was on a strictly cash basis which 


enabled the company, in many cases, to receive payment for the cars 
before it was necessary to pay the parts manufacturers. 


The expansion of the Ford Motor Company nevertheless 
* Under date of February 2, 1924. 


THE FORD MOTOR COMPANY. 131 


necessitated great increases in its working capital: overhead 
expenses rose; operating policies had to be initiated far in 
advance of current business, requiring advance purchases of 
large quantities of parts and materials; and the automobile 
industry as a whole faced an uncertain future in which sud- 
den declines in sales might be experienced. Against such 
requirements and contingencies, the Company early de- 
veloped a policy of maintaining unusually large balances in 
a number of widely distributed banks. The Company delib- 
erately avoided the usual policy of arranging for possible 
banking accommodation against periods of unusual stress 
(though for no obvious good reason), and sought to make it- 
self independent of banking support. Table 26, taken from 
a compilation made by the Detroit Trust Company for sub- 
mission in the Dodge suit, compares the Ford Motor Com- 
pany’s average ratio of cash balances to gross sales for the 
years 1914, 1915, and 1916 with those of other large manu- 
facturing companies. :

THE FORD MOTOR COMPANY 133 


years 1914, 1915, and 1916 with those of other large manu- 
facturing enterprises. 

The Ford Motor Company, during the years cited, em- 
ployed a relatively smaller amount of working capital in 
proportion to volume of sales than did certain other pro- 
ducers. It cannot be concluded from this fact, however, that 
its financial condition was less liquid than that of others. 
By contract with its sales dealers, the Company was assured 
of a cash market for its product immediately upon produc- 
tion, irrespective of current retail sales. The Company was 
thus relieved of the burden of carrying the accounts of its 
dealers and of carrying a large inventory of finished product. 
Its inventories of raw materials, moreover, were converted 
into finished goods at a very rapid rate: its cycle of manu- 
facture, from the receipt of raw materials and parts to ship- 
ment of finished vehicles, was only twenty-two days before 
1921; and it was cut to fourteen days by the radical econ- 
omies effected during that year. In recent years, the integra- 
tion of the Company’s operations has diminished the rate of 
its capital turnover; and the difficulty of employing great 
amounts of capital effectively has diminished its rate of 
profit. 

The book value of the Company’s net assets on December 
31, 1926, was $714,902,288; the market value of the Com- 
pany was at least $1,000,000,000 (the price offered by Horn- 
blower & Weeks, who expected to reseli it to the public, by 
the issue of securities, for perhaps twenty-five per cent more 
than this price).1_ With the exception of $28,000 invested in 
cash, and patents, contracts, and other intangible items 
valued at $72,000 more, the entire gigantic investment of the 
enterprise has been built up out of reinvested profits. 

t Testimony of John W. Prentiss, partner in Hornblower & Weeks, 


before the United States Board of Tax Appeals, in Washington, D.C., 
February 2, 1927. 


CHAPTER IV 
THE GENERAL MOTORS CORPORATION 


1. NATURE AND IMPORTANCE OF BUSINESS 


THE General Motors Corporation, because of its great size, 
the wide range of its automobile products, its high degree 
of industrial integration, the volume and activity of its se- 
curities in the investment and speculative markets, and its 
important financial connections, is widely regarded as the 
dominant organization in the automobile industry. In 
many quarters its position is held to be analogous to that of 
the United States Steel Corporation: the current volume of 
its operations is regarded as reflecting the state of the entire 
automobile industry and as indicative of the trend of general 
business. 

It has been observed that, as respects amount of invested 
capital and volume of motor-vehicle output, the General 
Motors Corporation is exceeded in importance in the auto- 
mobile industry only by the Ford Motor Company. The 
difference between the amounts of invested capital of these 
two companies, however, is relatively small, and may be 
more than offset by differences in their valuation practices; 
and the General Motors Corporation has recently out- 
stripped the Ford Motor Company in financial volume of 
sales, as well as approached it in volume of physical product. 
The motor-vehicle output and financial volume of sales of 
each of the two enterprises and the aggregate American auto- 
mobile output for the seven years ended in 1926 are pre- 
sented in Table 28. 

The net profits of the General Motors Corporation in 
1925 were exceeded only by those of the American Tele- 
phone and Telegraph Company and the United States 
Steel Corporation among American corporations; in 1926 
its net profits surpassed those of any other American business

enterprise.t The securities of the General Motors Corpora- 
tion are among the most active on the New York Stock 
Exchange; their market values fluctuate greatly; at their 
high points in 1926, the aggregate value of the Corpora- 
tion’s securities was more than $2,000,000,000. 

The General Motors Corporation owns directly most of 
the physical properties employed by its manufacturing 
units; primarily, therefore, it is an operating company. 
Some of its multifarious interests, however, are in the hands 
of separate companies whose capital stock it owns entirely 
or in large part; to this extent it is a holding company. The 
corporate structure of the General Motors Corporation is 
graphically portrayed in Chart V. Most of its subsidiaries, 
it should be noted, are separately incorporated for legal con- 
venience alone, and their nominal capitalizations bear little 
relation to their volume of business. 

The chief products of the Corporation and its subsidiary 
and affiliated companies fall into three groups: motor cars 
and trucks; automobile parts and accessories, a portion of 
the output of which is sold to independent automobile manu- 
facturers; and miscellaneous products and services, some of 


t Standard Statistics Company, Inc., report numbered G 19. 


136 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


which are, and others of which are not, connected with the 
automobile industry. 

The first and most important group, that of finished cars 
and trucks, comprises seven distinct lines of passenger 
vehicles, produced in 72 different models and retailing at 
prices ranging from $525 to $4350, and several lines of 
motor trucks and motor busses. The passenger cars are 
produced by five separate operating organizations, known 
as ‘divisions,’ all of the assets of which are owned directly 
by the Corporation;* and connected with each division is 
a sales unit of similar name (for example, Buick Motor 
Company), the nominal capital stock of which ($10,000 in 
each case) is owned by the Corporation. Two of the pas- 
senger-car divisions also produce commercial vehicles, the 
Chevrolet and Pontiac trucks; the latter, like the Pontiac 
passenger cars, are produced by the Oakland division. One 
other division, the Cadillac, also produces two lines of 
passenger vehicles— the Cadillac and La Salle. Most of the 
truck-producing operations of the Corporation have been 
centered in the Yellow Truck and Coach Manufacturing 
Company, which the Corporation controls by ownership of 
a majority of the voting stock. The Corporation owns all 
of the common stock of Vauxhall Motors, Ltd., an English 
producer of motor vehicles; and all of the capital stock of 
General Motors of Canada, Ltd., Canadian producer of 
General Motors vehicles. Fifteen foreign subsidiaries con- 
duct assembling operations and supervise the distribution of 
the Corporation’s products in Europe, Asia, Africa, Aus- 
tralia, New Zealand, and South America.? 

The Corporation’s announced policy is to ‘establish a 
complete line of motor cars from the lowest to the highest 
price that would justify quantity production.’ 3 This policy

nual reports of the General Motors Corporation.

has been pursued with great success in recent years, and its 
vehicles have become leading competitors in their respective 
price-classes of the products of nearly all other American 
automobile producers. Data bearing on the relative 
financial importance of its various constituent organiza- 
tions are largely withheld by the Corporation, but it is in- 
formally conceded by several of the leading officials that the 
Buick division, up to the end of 1926, has been by far the 
most important and the most profitable.t The Buick leader- 
ship is now being challenged, however, by the Chevrolet 
division. As respects volume of output, the relative im- 
portance of the Corporation’s vehicle-producing divisions

General Motors Corporation. The operations of thirteen of

ship of physical assets or capital stock (cf. Chart V), are 
consolidated in its accounts with the business of its vehicle- 
producing units. Some of them, like the Fisher Body division 
and its subsidiaries, are exceedingly important enterprises 
which transact a large volume of business with independent 
automobile producers. Their products are extensively ad- 
vertised in automobile trade publications, and the Corpora- 
tion maintains, in the main, their operating autonomy.? 
Through the Fisher Body division, the Corporation owns 
standing timber, operates hardwood mills, glass and hard- 
ware factories, etc., and manufactures automobile bodies 
for its own vehicle divisions and for independent pro- 
ducers. 

The Corporation includes among its accessory and parts 
organizations, the Delco-Light Company, producer of water 
pumps, farm lighting and power plants, and electric re- 
frigerators; and the Frigidaire Corporation, a sales subsid- 
iary for the distribution of Frigidaire refrigerators. These 
products, extraneous to the automobile industry, were added 
to the Corporation’s lines as if by accident, as parts of 
sundry acquisitions made in 1919; and for some years their 
réle was unimportant. Recently, however, with the growing 
use of electric refrigerators, this branch of the Corporation’s 
activities has expanded tremendously: the Corporation’s 
investment in refrigerator manufacture has increased to 
approximately $40,000,000, and the Frigidaire earnings in 
1926, according to President Sloan, were more than sufficient 
to pay dividends on all the preferred stock of the General 
Motors Corporation.3

THE GENERAL MOTORS CORPORATION 139 


Included in the Corporation’s balance sheet under the 
heading of ‘Investments’ on December 31, 1926, were the 
following: (1) The General Motors Acceptance Corporation, 
which is engaged in the business of financing the wholesale 
and retail purchase of General Motors products. Organized 
under the banking laws of New York, its entire capital stock, 
$25,000,000, is held by the General Motors Corporation. 
Apart from its paid-in capital and surplus, this subsidiary 
obtains most of its funds by debenture note-issues: it sold 
two note-issues of $50,000,000 each through J. P. Morgan 
& Co. in 1926 and 1927. (2) The General Motors Building 
_ Corporation, which owns and operates the General Motors 
Building in Detroit. (3) The Ethyl Gasoline Corporation, 
owned jointly by the Standard Oil Company of New Jersey 
and the General Motors Corporation (each holding 7500 
shares of $100 par value common stock), distributor of 
Ethyl gasoline, a compound originated by a General Motors 
research agency. (4) The General Exchange Insurance 
Corporation, which writes insurance on vehicles financed by 
the General Motors Acceptance Corporation. (5) The Yel- 
low Truck and Coach Manufacturing Company, which, as 
before noted, has taken over the Corporation’s truck divi- 
sion. The Corporation’s investment in this Company is 
valued on its books at $24,091,000, and includes fifty-seven 
per cent of the voting stock; the Company will probably be 
merged shortly with the General Motors Corporation. (6) 
Vauxhall Motors, Ltd., which, also noted previously, pro- 
duces and sells Vauxhall motor cars in England. The Cor- 
poration owns all of its ordinary stock, valued at $2,575,201. 
(7) Five enterprises engaged in providing housing facilities 
for the Corporation’s employees. The Corporation’s in- 
vestment in these enterprises was $8,461,334 on December 
31, 1926, the capital stock of each being owned by the 
Corporation or one of its subsidiaries. 

In addition to the interests already cited, the Corporation 
and its subsidiaries own all of the capital stock of more than 
a score of sales companies connected with one or another of 
its various products. 


140 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


The administration of the host of interests which comprise 
the General Motors Corporation — its factories, alone, are 
located in some forty different cities — offers a fruitful field 
for extended exposition; the limits of this study, however, 
permit only a brief description. The underlying principle of 
the Corporation’s administrative policy is that of a federa- 
tion of codrdinated but semi-autonomous organizations. 
Each organization possesses its own physical facilities, its 
own operating personnel, and a large measure of control 
over its operating policies. ‘For the sake of efficiency, we 
deliberately preferred to be a collection of small or moderate- 
sized units, well codrdinated, rather than one large consol- 
idated business,’ declared the Corporation’s president in a 
recent published article.t ‘It is a General Motors policy to 
decentralize control, at the same time taking advantage of 
the strength, resources, and experience of the organization 
asa whole... .’ declared one of the operating vice-presidents 
in a similar article? The existing scheme of administration, 
while it permits a large measure of operating autonomy for 
each of the constituent organizations, provides for cen- 
tralized supervision of capital expenditures, inventories, 
purchases, and cash resources. Maximum budget allow- 
ances for each unit limit the capital and inventory ex- 
penditures. Interplant transactions are settled chiefly by 
book entries. The cash reserves of the Corporation are 
pooled in a dozen New York and Detroit banks. The daily 
receipts of each unit are deposited in a local bank, which, 
through the telegraph service of the Federal Reserve Sys- 
tem, transmits all but a stated maximum local deposit, to 
the Corporation’s chief banking depositories. The latter 
transmit funds by telegraph, whenever needed, to any of the 
banks in thirty-three cities that carry disbursing accounts 
of the Corporation. The deposits of the Corporation are

2C. S. Mott, ‘Organizing a Great Industrial,’ in Management and —

THE GENERAL MOTORS CORPORATION 141 


everywhere made to draw interest, and each account serves 
as the basis of a possible line of bank credit averaging five 
times the size of the average deposit balance.t A num- 
ber of advisory bodies and ‘interdivision relations com- 
mittees’ maintain constant contact with every unit and 
seek to establish codrdinated policies with respect to 
sales, purchasing, advertising, operating, and engineering 
problems. 

The management of the Corporation proper is in the 
hands of a board of directors of thirty-two men, evenly 
divided between representatives of important stockholding 
interests and active officers of the Corporation; a finance 
committee of thirteen, among whom nationally known 

financiers are conspicuous; and an executive committee of 
nine. In addition to the chairman of the board of directors 
and the president of the Corporation, there are sixteen vice- 
presidents, most of whom are the chief executives of the 
Corporation’s larger constituents. | 

“ The General Motors Corporation stands ninth among 
American corporations in volume of securities outstanding, 
and among the first three or four in total market value of 
securities. Its capital structure, however, is extremely sim- 
ple. When certain changes in its capitalization, authorized 
between 1924 and 1927, are completed, the Corporation will 
have outstanding only two classes of securities: common 
stock of $25 par value (17,400,000 shares issued, and 
30,000,000 shares authorized); and seven per cent preferred 
stock. The capitalization on December 31, 1926, prior to the

for each share of no par value common stock (authorized 
September 12, 1927), and to the completed conversion of 
older issues of preferred and debenture stocks, was as fol-

The total number of stockholders during 1926 ranged be- 
tween 47,805 and 72,665; on March 12, 1927, the number of 
common stockholders was 36,536. 

Control of the General Motors Corporation is possessed 
by E. I. Du Pont de Nemours & Co. and affiliated interests. 
The Du Ponts first acquired stock in the Corporation in 1915; 
by the end of 1917, E. I. Du Pont de Nemours & Co. had 
acquired 27.6 per cent of the Corporation’s outstanding 
common stock.? In November, 1920, through a subsidiary 
organized for the purpose, the Du Pont interests purchased 
the holdings of William C. Durant, then president of the 
General Motors Corporation, and Pierre S. Du Pont replaced 
Durant as president of the Corporation.? In 1923, when the 
Du Ponts, by previous arrangement, were called upon to sell 
Durant’s holdings, approximately 2,250,000 shares, to the 
Managers Securities Company for resale to executive em- 
ployees of the General Motors Corporation, they sold, in- 
stead, a thirty per cent interest in the General Motors Se- 
curities Company, a Du Pont subsidiary which owned about 
7,500,000 shares of General Motors common stock — thus 
retaining undiminished control of their holdings. On Sep- 
tember 9, 1927, following receipt of notice from the Federal 
Trade Commission that relations between the Du Pont 
company, General Motors, and the United States Steel 
Corporation were under investigation, Mr. Irénée Du 
Pont made public a statement declaring that the Du Pont 
company now owned the equivalent of 1,966,244 shares of

General Motors common stock, or 22.6 per cent of the total 
outstanding.’ 

Control of the General Motors Corporation by the Du 
Pont interests is furthered not only by the wide distribution 
of General Motors common stock, which makes effective 
minority control possible, but also by the large stockhold- 
ings of groups affiliated with the Du Pont company. J. P. 
Morgan & Co. acquired a substantial interest in the com- 
mon stock of the Corporation in June, 1920, when it under- 
wrote an issue of 3,219,856 shares at $20 a share,? and six 
Morgan representatives were shortly thereafter elected to 
the board of directors. The Morgan firm increased its 
General Motors holdings in November, 1920, when it ob- 
_ tained a one-fifth interest in the Du Pont Securities Com- 
pany for its services in underwriting $20,000,000 of one-year 
collateral trust notes issued by the latter to finance the pur- 
chase of approximately 2,250,000 shares of General Motors 
common stock from Durant.4 Nobel Industries, Ltd., a 
British firm, subscribed jointly with the Canadian Ex- 
plosives, Ltd., for 1,800,000 shares of the Morgan under- 
writing of General Motors common stock in June, 1920. 
E. I. Du Pont de Nemours & Co. is a stockholder in the 
Canadian enterprise; ’ and, because of their similar indus- 
trial interests (explosives and chemicals), may be presumed 
to be allied with the British concern. 

The control of the Corporation by the powerful Du Pont 
and Morgan industrial and financial interests undoubtedly
