144 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


promotes a more generous accommodation of the Corpora- 
tion’s financial requirements at the hands of commercial 
banks. Like other American motor-vehicle manufacturers, 
its credit needs are extremely small in ordinary times. 
Nevertheless, the integrated character of its operations re- 
quires the maintenance of large inventories of materials, 
and any slackening in the rate of production quickly results 
in enormous increases in these inventories, commitments 
for which are made far in advance; hence, at times, the 
Corporation may require very substantial banking accom- 
modation. The chief issue of preferred stock, it may be 
noted, is scarcely calculated to jeopardize the control of the 
common stockholders: the preferred stock has no direct 
voice in the management, except that it may veto mortgag- 
ing of the Corporation’s properties; and its contingent con- 
trol, effective after two successive defaults in its quarterly 
dividend claims, is limited to the election of twenty-five per 
cent of the board of directors. Its dividend rights, on the 
other hand, are exceedingly well protected: additional 
issues of preferred must wait upon equal increases in cor- 
porate surplus or in issued common stock; no cash dividends 
are payable on common stock unless the excess of quick 
assets over current liabilities remains at seventy-five per 
cent or more of the par value of the outstanding preferred, 
and unless the common stock and surplus equity behind the 
preferred remains at least as great as it was in 1924, when 
the present preferred stock was issued to consolidate previ- 
ous varieties; and the preferred dividends are cumulative. 
The General Motors Corporation, under the name of the 
General Motors Company (New Jersey), was the first large 
combination in the automobile industry. Since its forma- 
tion in 1908, several other important combinations have been 
projected or actually organized; but it alone has proved 
conspicuously successful. The General Motors enterprise 
was likewise the first automobile manufacturing organiza- 
tion to enlist the services of investment bankers in any large 
measure; and the volume of its corporate financing through 


— ———————s 


THE GENERAL MOTORS CORPORATION 145 


these agencies has exceeded that of any other automobile 
company. The association of the enterprise with Eastern 
banking houses and financiers soon after its birth, and its 
status then as a holding company, early gave it a financial 
rather than industrial character; and in diminished degree 
this character has persisted to the present day. 

The present General Motors Corporation was incorpo- 
rated under the laws of Delaware on October 13, 1916. By 
exchange of securities it acquired all of the capital stock of 
the General Motors Company, which had been incorporated 
in New Jersey on September 16, 1908. The latter company 
was dissolved on August I, 1917; its principal constituent 
companies were likewise dissolved at this time, and the 
General Motors Corporation took over direct ownership and 
operation of the bulk of the physical properties previously 
controlled by the holding company and its subsidiaries. 
The history of the General Motors Corporation, to which we 
now turn, begins, therefore, with that of the original New 
Jersey company and its constituents. We begin with the 
Buick Motor Company, the nucleus of the original combina- 
tion, and its most important single constituent since. 


2. THE Buick NUCLEUS 

David Buick, in 1900, entered the business of manufactur- 
ing stationary motors for farm use under the name, the 
Buick Manufacturing Company. He became indebted for 
materials to Benjamin Briscoe, then a producer of sheet- 
metal stampings and later an important figure in the auto- 
mobile industry. To secure this indebtedness of about 
$2000, and a loan of $1500 more that Buick needed for his 
experiments with an automobile motor, Briscoe arranged 
with Buick to form the Buick Motor Company, $99,700 of 
the $100,000 capital stock of which was pledged to Briscoe to 
secure the latter’s claims.! This company was incorporated 
in Michigan on May 19, 1903. 

« Cf. Benjamin Briscoe, ‘The Inside Story of General Motors,’ pub- 


lished as a series of four articles in the Detroit Saturday Night of January 
15, 22, 29, and February 5, 1921. 


146 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


Briscoe planned to take over ownership and operation 
of the Buick Motor Company in September, 1903, if Buick 
was not then able to meet his obligations, for Briscoe’s 
business of metal stampings had already developed into an 
enterprise principally devoted to the manufacture of auto- 
mobile radiators and parts. Becoming interested in a motor 
designed by J. D. Maxwell, who had collaborated with the 
Apperson brothers, Elwood Haynes, R. E. Olds, and other 
pioneer automobile designers, Briscoe decided to dispose of 
his interest in the Buick company and to ally himself with 
Maxwell in what became the Maxwell-Briscoe Motor 
Company.?, An arrangement was made with the Flint 
Wagon Works, of Flint, Michigan, which had begun to ex- 
periment with automobile manufacture, whereby that com- 
pany took over the operation of the Buick enterprise. The 
Buick Motor Company was reincorporated on January 30, 
1904, with a capital stock of $75,000, half of which was listed 
as paid for in cash, and $15,000 of which was given to David 
Buick.s The new company quickly became involved in 
difficulties: a total of sixteen vehicles was produced in 1903 
and only thirty-six in 1904; the Company was obliged to 
give liens on its property to secure its creditors; finally, its 
obligations to Flint banks late in 1904, said to have been 
large enough to threaten the safety of three of the town’s 
financial institutions, forced a reorganization.4 It was in 
connection with this reorganization that William Crapo 
Durant, later promoter of the General Motors Combina- 
tion and one of the outstanding figures in the automobile 
industry, first entered the automobile business. 

Durant, a grandson of a former governor of Michigan, 
had been millhand, drug clerk, patent-medicine salesman,

real estate and insurance agent, traveling salesman for a 
cigar company, and secretary of the Flint Water Works, all 
before he had attained the age of twenty-five. In 1886, with 
the aid of J. Dallas Dort, then a clerk in a Flint hardware 
store, Durant acquired rights to a patented road cart; 
and, with a total capital of $2000, the two men organized 
the Durant-Dort Carriage Company. In a few years, with 
Durant supervising sales and Dort in charge of factory 
operations, this enterprise became one of the largest in the 
wagon industry, operating fourteen plants in the United 
States and Canada, and producing 150,000 vehicles a year.? 

In 1904, the Durant-Dort Carriage Company, capitalized 
at $2,000,000, was one of the largest and most successful 
business enterprises in Flint; its large earnings had already 
' previously tempted its principal owners, who were forty- 
three and forty-two years of age, respectively (in 1904), to 
engage (albeit unsuccessfully) in bicycle manufacturing and 
other ventures; Durant, by virtue of his business success, his 
wide acquaintance, and his personal popularity, possessed a 
very high reputation in the Flint community; and the wagon 
and carriage business in 1904 was commonly considered as 
closely related to the growing automobile business. In view 
of these factors, it is not surprising that, when the prospec- 
tive failure of the Buick Motor Company threatened to in- 
volve important Flint creditors, Durant was called upon, 
and was willing, to undertake the reorganization of the 
Company.? 

Durant immediately had the authorized capitalization of 
the Company increased from $75,000 to $500,000, and pro- 
cured subscriptions to $250,000 of the stock. It does not 
appear that he was able to obtain new cash capital for the 
Company immediately, but he did succeed in obtaining new 
credit and extensions of time from old creditors.3 The next

In the following year the Buick Motor Company defi- 
nitely began its career of expansion and profit-making. Du- 
_ rant’s selling experience and country-wide acquaintance, 
acquired in connection with his carriage business, enabled 
him to build up quickly an effective dealer-organization; 
the Company’s new product, a four-cylinder automobile 
selling at about $1000, proved to be very popular; ma- 
chine shops and more formal parts-making establish- 
ments were already available in and around Detroit and 
Flint, enabling the Company to expand its business without 
greatly increasing its fixed capital requirements; and the 
practice of advance deposits on dealers’ orders and of sales 
for cash, which had early become established in the auto- 
mobile industry, simplified the working-capital problem. 
In 1906, the Company sold 2295 cars as compared with 673 
the year before, and was, next to the Cadillac Motor Car 
Company, the largest American producer of automobiles."

and, with an increase of only $30,830 in paid-in capital

and $15,000 of common stock issued for ‘property’), the

In 1907, despite the disturbed financial condition pre-

3848 cars, as against 8759 cars sold by the Ford Motor Com- 
pany, and 2867 by the Cadillac Motor Car Company. On

of common stock from $900,000 to $2,000,000, and a stock 
dividend of $250,000 par value of common stock was de- 
clared. By October 1, 1907, all intangible assets had been 
written off the books of the Company, and its net assets, 
consisting of real estate, plant and equipment, cash, and 
receivables, less its outside liabilities, had risen to $2,880,- 
478.7 

In 1908, the year of the organization of the General Mo- 
tors Company, the Buick Motor Company was the largest 
producer of automobiles in the United States, producing 
a total of 8487 cars, as compared with 6181 produced by the 
Ford Motor Company, and 2380 by the Cadillac Motor 
Car Company. Its large earnings in this year enabled it to 
more than double its investment in real estate, plant and 
equipment, to retire $100,000 of its preferred stock, and to 
make investments in various parts-making enterprises.4 On 
October 1, 1908, the date of its acquisition by the General 
Motors Company, its net worth totaled $3,417,142, almost 
all of which had been built up by the reinvestment of earn- 
ings.

3. BEGINNINGS OF THE GENERAL Motors CoMPANY | 


Such had been the brief history and such was the status of 
the Buick Motor Company when, in the middle of 1908, 
plans were first broached for a combination of the larger 
motor-car manufacturers. The first efforts in this direction 
and some of the factors that provided the impulse for the 
movement have already been noted.? We have observed that 
the automobile industry during this period was an extremely 
speculative one: large numbers of small companies, meagerly 
financed, absorbed the greater part of the market;? the ten- 
tative technical character of the automobile, real and fan- 
cied, made the future of any particular ‘make’ very uncer- 
tain; and even the largest producers possessed so small a 
margin of liquid capital that a single unpopular model 

threatened their existence. Combination offered the op- 
portunity of diversifying risks among a variety of automobile 
products, and of obtaining readier access to investment 
capital. Thus, as we have seen, four of the largest producers 
— the Ford, Buick, Reo, and Maxwell-Briscoe enterprises — 
came within an ace of combining their interests. 

Undaunted by the failure of several combination projects 
which he and Benjamin Briscoe had initiated, Durant, on 
September 16, 1908, effected the incorporation in New 
Jersey of the General Motors Company, which obtained a 
perpetual charter containing a very broad grant of powers. 
The initial capitalization of $2000 was increased two weeks 
later to $12,500,000, of which $7,000,000 consisted of seven 
per cent preferred stock and $5,500,000, of common stock, 
both classes being of $100 par value. On October 1, 1908, 
in exchange for 18,870 shares of the common stock of the 
Buick Motor Company, $500,000 in cash, and an under- 
writing agreement assuring the sale of $2,000,000 par value

panies in 1908 was 18,411 (figures from records of General Motors 
Corporation and from The Ford Industries); the total American output 
was 65,000.

of its preferred stock, the General Motors Company issued 
to Durant $2,387,000 par value of its preferred and $2,193,- 
500 of its common stock; $1,000,000 of the latter was im- 
mediately donated back to the Company for reissue as a 
fifty per cent bonus with the sale of its preferred stock. The 
nature of this transaction may be seen from the following 
itemization: 
Issued for 18,870 shares of Buick common: 
$1,887,000 General Motors preferred and 
$943,500 General Motors common; 
Issued for $500,000 cash: 
$500,000 General Motors preferred and 
$250,000 General Motors common; 
Issued for underwriting agreement, and donated 
back to the Company for reissue as bonus 
with sale of preferred stock: 
$1,000,000 General Motors common. 


The remaining capital stock of the Buick Motor Company 
was acquired on the same day on substantially the same 
terms (one share of preferred and one-half share of common 
of General Motors for each share, common or preferred, of 
the Buick Company). In the months following, partly for 
cash and partly by exchange of securities, one enterprise 
after another was brought into the Combination. Within 
two years after its incorporation, the General Motors Com- 
pany had acquired a stock interest in more than two dozen 
different enterprises: these included eleven automobile-pro- 
ducing companies and a variety of parts, accessory, and 
lamp companies. Most of these interests were acquired 
piecemeal: Durant’s procedure was to come to an arrange- 
ment with one or more of the principal stockholders of a 
company, and to offer similar, though not always identical,

Of the car-producing enterprises thus acquired, only 
three warrant special consideration: the Buick Motor Com- 
pany, the history of which has been detailed above; the 
Cadillac Motor Car Company, and the Olds Motor Works. 

Next to the Buick, the Cadillac Motor Car Company 
was the most important acquisition made by the Combina- 
tion. This company had been incorporated on October 27, 
1905, to succeed the Cadillac Automobile Company and 
the Leland & Faulconer Manufacturing Company, a parts- 
making enterprise; its entire capital stock, $1,500,000 of 
common, had been issued for the assets of its predecessors.3 
Its principal stockholders, Clarence A. Black, Lem W. 
Bowen, William H. Murphy, and W. W. White, had pre- 
viously supported Henry Ford in the Detroit Automobile 
Company, and, after Ford’s withdrawal, had organized the 
Cadillac Automobile Company. 

The Cadillac automobile had early become popular: its 
sales in 1904 totaled 2534; in 1905, 3920; in 1906, 4045; in 
1907, 2367; in 1908, 2380. On November 12, 1908, Ben- 
jamin Briscoe obtained an option on sixty per cent of the 
Company’s stock at $150 a share.4 This option expired dur- 
ing the progress of Briscoe’s negotiations with J. P. Morgan 
& Co. for the financing of the purchase. Durant, in the mean-:

The Cadillac purchase, in the light of its then-current 
earnings and of its subsequent history, was exceedingly well 
advised. For the year ended August 31, 1909, the Company 
reported earnings of $1,969,382 ? — the equivalent of a forty- 
two per cent return on the amount of General Motors’ 
investment; its net worth, after the payment of $675,000 in 
dividends, was $2,862,709.3 The willingness of its stock- 
holders to sell out can be explained only by the speculative 
character of the automobile industry at the time, coupled 
with the relatively high price offered for the stock as com- 
pared with its book value. 

The acquisition of the Olds Motor Works, which pre- 
ceded that of the Cadillac Company, appears, in contrast, 
to have been a great extravagance. This enterprise had been 
organized originally as the Olds Motor Vehicle Company in 
1896 by Ransom E. Olds, one of the pioneer automobile 
inventors. In 1899, Olds had procured the financial support 
of a Detroit capitalist, S. L. Smith; the Company was re- 
moved from Lansing to Detroit, and reincorporated as the 
Olds Motor Works, with a paid-in capital of $200,000. In 
1900, its ‘Curved-Dash Oldsmobile,’ retailing at $650, gave 
it great prominence, and it promised for a time to become the 
leading American automobile producer: its output in 1900 
Was 1400 Cars; in I9OI, 2100; in 1902, 2500; in 1903, 3000; 
in 1904, 5508. Its orders for parts initiated into the industry 
many later-important producers, and its operating organiza- 
tion included so many individuals subsequently prominent 
in the industry that Olds has been called ‘the schoolmaster 
of the automobile industry.’ 4 In 1905, however, as a result 
of a controversy over the Company’s product — Smith’s

sons desiring to substitute an expensive automobile for the

which had been specially organized for him by citizens of 
Lansing, Michigan. With Olds’s departure, the Olds Motor 
Works went into a decline: in 1905, its output dropped to 
2381; in 1906, to 1372; in 1907, to 1045; in 1908, it was 1146.

liabilities of $1,472,693, gross tangible assets of $2,763,838, 
and net tangible assets of $1,291,145. On November 12, 
1908, the General Motors Company purchased the entire

Company’s president) for $1,827,694 of General Motors pre- 
ferred stock, $1,195,880 of common, and $17,279 in cash. 
Durant purchased the Company chiefly for its reputation, 
according to Mr. A. B. C. Hardy, later its president.* 

The remaining eight lines of automobiles acquired by the 
General Motors Company during its first two years were 
untried or unimportant products, all but one of which,

the Reliance had been incorporated only a year earlier; 4 
the Cartercar had been purchased because it boasted a 
‘friction-drive,’ and ‘maybe friction-drive would be the 
thing’; the Elmore had been acquired for a similar reason 
— it produced a two-cycle motor, and ‘maybe two-cycles 
was going to be the thing for automobiles’; the Randolph 
had only just been incorporated; 5 the Welch Motor Car 
Company had produced less than a dozen cars; * and another

The philosophy that motivated the acquisition of this 

large assortment of motor-car products has already been 
recited: while the generic demand for automobiles was large, 
that for any particular ‘make’ was uncertain; risks could be 
pooled and minimized by producing a variety of automobile 
products. Durant’s policy was summarized by Mr. A. B. C. 
Hardy, who had been intimately associated with him, as 
follows: ? 
_ Durant bought a lot of different companies, most of which were 
not much good; but he paid for them largely in stock. He didn’t 
want the actual assets of these companies; most of them were head 
over heels in debt, anyway. He wanted to have a lot of ‘makes,’ so 
that he would always be sure to have some popular cars. I heard 
him explain this one day in this way: 

“They say I shouldn’t have bought Cartercar,’ he said. ‘Well, 
how was any one to know that Cartercar wasn’t to be the thing? It 
had the friction drive and no other car had it. How could I tell what 
these engineers would say next? Maybe friction drive would be the 
thing. And then there’s Elmore, with its two-cycle engine. That’s 
the kind they were using on motor-boats; maybe two-cycles was 
going to be the thing for automobiles. I was for getting every kind 

of car in sight, playing safe all along the line.’ 


In addition to the car-producers actually acquired and to 
the various parts-making enterprises purchased, the General 
_ Motors Company, as we have noted, made a serious attempt 
to acquire the Ford Motor Company and the Maxwell- 
Briscoe Motor Company. Mention has been made of the 
option obtained by Durant from the Ford Motor Company 
following the latter’s reverse in the Selden patent decision of 

* Incorporated in Michigan on July 10, 1909; annual report as of

2 In an interview with the writer in Lansing, Michigan, December 24,

1909 — an option that Durant failed to exercise only be- 
cause he could not procure the funds necessary for the ini-

Briscoe Company failed for the same reason. A memoran-

exchange of securities of the Maxwell-Briscoe Company at 
a valuation of $3,500,000; and of the Brush Runabout Com-

$100,000 and $460,000, respectively. ‘But, through the 
taking in of the Heany companies at about that time’ (to

so that Maxwell-Briscoe would have gotten about $5,000,- 
ooo of stock in the combination.’? Briscoe tried to obtain 
the consent of the Morgan interests, who had financed his 
operations, to this transaction, but was told that a substan- 
tial cash payment, approximating $2,000,000, would be nec- 
essary to obtain their consent. 


4. STOCK-WATERING: THE HEANY LAMP COMPANIES 


The acquisitions of the General Motors Company recited 
in the foregoing were frequently consummated informally 
and antedated formal authorization by the board of direc- 
tors. This fact, coupled with the relatively large amounts of 
stock issued for several of the smaller companies, opens the 
door to a suspicion that several of the transactions were 
made occasions for the unofficial distribution of ‘promotion 
stock’ to individuals associated with the management of the 
parent company. It is difficult, of course, to obtain author- 
itative comment or official data in corroboration of this in- 
ference. In the case of the Heany Lamp companies, how- 
ever, the facts themselves, and the remark of Mr. Briscoe, 
quoted above, that the Heany transaction made it permis- 
sible to ‘sweeten’ the consideration to be paid for the Max- 
well-Briscoe properties, crystallize such a suspicion. The 
facts were substantially as follows: 


* Briscoe, ‘The Inside Story of General Motors,’ Joc. cit. a Ibid. 


3 From the Official Gazette of the United States Patent Office, p. 934 
fi., and the records of the General Motors Corporation. 


THE GENERAL MOTORS CORPORATION 159 


John Albert Heany, on December 29, 1904, filed applica- 
tion for a patent on an improved electric lamp bulb; and on 
August I, 1906, and November 19, 1906, filed additional ap- 
plications, alleging that these were revisions of his original 
ones and therefore entitled to the original dating. Heany 
was granted the patent in 1907, and shortly after became in- 
volved in litigation with rival claimants who had assigned 
their rights to the General Electric Company. In the fall of 
1907, Heany, his attorney, H. E. Everding, and a patent- 
office clerk, Ned W. Barton, were charged with altering the 
original application to permit the later ones to be classed as 
revisions: Barton and Everding were found guilty in federal 
court and were sentenced to prison; Heany was acquitted. 
‘The latter, in the meantime, had organized the Heany Com- 
pany, the Heany Lamp Company, the Heany Electric Com- 
pany, and the Tipless Lamp Company, to which he had as- 
signed his patent rights. The stocks of these companies were 
acquired by the General Motors Company on January 4, 
1910, in return for $1,111,000 of General Motors preferred 
stock, $5,908,500 of its common stock, and $112,759 in 
cash.t Since the principal assets of the Heany companies 
consisted of cloudy patent rights, which were later voided by 
the United States Commissioner of Patents, the General 
Motors Company received a negligible consideration for 
a volume of securities greater than it had issued for any 
automobile enterprise and greater than its payment for the 
Buick and Olds Companies together. On September 30, 
1910, its holdings in the Heany companies were transferred 
on its books to ‘good-will,’ and, a little later, they were 
written off the books entirely.? 

It is not a little striking to record that on October I, 
1927, the securities issued for the Heany companies (as 
multiplied by stock dividends) had a market value in 
excess of $320,000,000; that, by this date, more than 
$50,000,000 in cash dividends had been paid on them; and 


t See Table 30, above. 
# From the records of the General Motors Corporation. 


160 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


that they now command annual dividends in excess of 
$10,000,v00. 


5. FINANCING ACQUISITIONS AND OPERATIONS 


On September 30, 1910, the General Motors Company 
had outstanding (exclusive of treasury holdings of the par- 
ent and subsidiary companies) $10,042,100 par value of pre- 
ferred stock and $15,819,830 of common stock. The author- 
ized capitalization of the Company had been increased on 
September 15, 1909, to $40,000,000 of common and $20,030,- 
ooo of preferred; and on November 15, 1909, a stock divi- 
dend of 150 per cent, aggregating $6,249,200 par value of 
common stock, had been declared. The origins of the stock 
issues of the Company up to September 30, 1910, and its 
capital receipts and expenditures, may be summarized as 
follows:?

It will be seen at once that, while the Company paid out 
in excess of $6,000,000 in cash for its acquisitions, its receipts 
of cash from the sale of securities only slightly exceeded 
$4,500,000. The current earnings of its constituents, parti- 
cularly of the Buick Motor Company, and the proceeds of 
loans negotiated chiefly in the latter’s name, provided the 
Combination with the remainder of the capital funds re- 
quired. Thus, the Cadillac purchase was originally made by 
the Buick company, the latter procuring the funds by dis- 


* From the records of the General Motors Corporation. 


THE GENERAL MOTORS CORPORATION 161 


counting its short-term notes, secured by the Cadillac stock 
as collateral, with various commercial banks.” 

The capital requirements of the General Motors Company 
were not limited, however, to the provision of funds for the 
direct acquisition of properties. The purpose of the Com- 
bination required the production of the full line of motor 
vehicles it had acquired. Most of the constituents, however, 
were small and untried enterprises, badly in need of equip- 
ment and of inventories; and many of them were in debt. 
In the attempt to develop all of its lines of motor vehicles, 
the Combination, in the year ended September 30, 1910, 
during which few important acquisitions were made, in- 
creased its investment in fixed plant from $6,078,447 to 
$14,094,906; and the book value of its inventories rose from 
$8 520,920 to $39,802,483.? 

Fortunately, the earnings of 1909 and I910 were very 
large: net profits applicable to capital stock of the General 
Motors Company totaled $9,114,498 in 1909 and $10,225,- 
367 in 1910, of which only $1,060,568 had been paid out in 
dividends during the two years. But the operations of the 
Combination had expanded rapidly in its second year, the 
financial volume of sales rising from $29,029,875 in 1909 to 
$49,430,179 in 1910;3 and the increased volume of business 
accentuated the inadequate provision in the financial plan 
for liquid capital. In consequence, despite its large current 
earnings, the General Motors Company and its subsidiaries 
became more and more heavily indebted to suppliers and to 
commercial banks. To further aggravate the situation, the 
sales of the Buick Motor Company, the bulwark of the 
Combination, began to fall below the scheduled volume in 
the summer of 1910, adding the commitments of this com- 
pany to those of the smaller enterprises.‘

The immediate solution of the difficulties of the Combina- 
tion seemed to lie in the sale of securities for cash. The 
times, however, were not propitious for the sale of automo- 
bile company stocks. In 1910, bankers and others, in maga- 
zine articles and in public addresses, were pronouncing fre- 
quent warnings against the continuing growth of the auto- 
mobile industry. A common assertion was that the industry 
was diverting the productive resources of the country into 
unproductive channels. The industry was charged with 
being responsible for the then-prevailing stagnation in the 
bond market by absorbing funds which would otherwise be 
invested in securities; with reducing the average size of 
bank accounts; and with other evil influences. 

Late in August, 1910, with a view, possibly, to facilitate 
the sale of common stock by increasing the number of shares 
and thus reducing the market price per share, William C. 
Durant, chairman of the executive committee, announced a 
plan for increasing the capitalization of the Company and 
distributing a stock dividend of 400 per cent.? Before this 
plan could be put into effect, however, the merchandise and 
bank creditors of the Buick Motor Company (the name and 
credit of which were largely employed by the parent concern 
for the requirements of the entire Combination) met in New 
York to determine upon a common policy.3 The bank in- 
debtedness of the Buick company was found to total ap- 
proximately $2,700,000, and its indebtedness to merchan- 
dise creditors, between $4,000,000 and $5,000,000. A gen- 
eral committee of creditors was appointed for the purpose of 
effecting ‘a reorganization of the management and a restric- 
tion of enthusiasm’; and arrangements were made to extend 


against a scheduled volume of 30,000, most of the decline falling in the

a credit of one year, and to advance $2,500,000 on new notes 
secured by the capital stocks of the Olds and Cadillac 
companies. 


6. FUNDING OF GENERAL Motors’ INDEBTEDNESS 


The General Motors Company had no sooner completed 
arrangements for temporary financial relief than steps were 
taken to fund the Company’s indebtedness and to supply it 
with additional working capital. Late in September, 1910, 
negotiations were concluded with a syndicate headed by Lee, 
Higginson & Co., of Boston, and J. & W. Seligman & Co., 
and the Central Trust Company, of New York, for the 
underwriting of $15,000,000 (of a total authorized issue of 
_ $20,000,000) six per cent sinking fund, five-year notes, 
dated October 1, 1910, and redeemable on any interest 
date at 10214. The plan for the issue of the notes, secured by 
a deed of trust, and protected by the deposit of a majority 
of the outstanding preferred and common stock of the 
General Motors Company under a voting-trust agreement, 
was formally adopted by the Company’s board of directors 
on November 11, 1910. How thoroughly the provisions of 
the deed of trust protected the note-holders may be seen 
from a survey of its principal elements.? 

1. The real estate, plants, patents and other fixed assets of the 
Buick, Cadillac, Olds, Oakland, Elmore, Northway, Marquette, 
Rapid, Reliance, Welch of Detroit, Welch Motor, Champion Ig- 
nition, Jackson-Church-Wilcox, Michigan Motor Castings, and Oak 
Park Power companies were transferred to the General Motors 
Company of Michigan, theretofore the holder merely of some pieces 
of idle real estate in the city of Detroit; and this company issued 
$13,300,000 of First Mortgage, six per cent five-year notes, secured 
by a first mortgage on all the property it then owned or would after- 
wards acquire, to the Central Trust Company, as trustee; these 
notes being pledged as part of the security of the notes issued by the 


parent company. 
2. The General Motors Company (New Jersey) pledged all its 


* Cf. Commercial and Financial Chronicle, vol. 90, p. 719. 
* From the listing application of General Motors Company to the 
New York Stock Exchange, June 22, 1911. 


1644 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


holdings in subsidiary companies directly connected with the manu- 
facture of motor cars and parts (constituting substantially all of 
its assets, except for the stocks of the Heany Lamp companies and 
its small holdings in Brown-Lipe-Chapin Company); and agreed 
that any evidences of indebtedness issued by any subsidiary, a ma- 
jority of whose stock was pledged, other than those issued for cur- 
rent operating expenses, would be acquired by the parent company; 
that none of such subsidiaries would issue stock having preference 
over that pledged; and that if any company, stock of which was 
pledged, increased the amount of its capital stock, the General 
Motors Company would acquire and pledge the amount of the in- 
crease proportionate to its pledged holdings. 

3. The General Motors Company agreed that while any of the 
notes were outstanding, the Company would not distribute in cash 
dividends on its common stock more than one-half of the net profits 
subsequent to October 1, 1910, applicable to such dividends; and 
that it would maintain net quick assets equal to at least 133% per 
cent of the amount of notes outstanding plus an amount equal to the 
total cash dividends, if any, paid on the common stock. 

4. The sinking fund provisions called for the annual cash payment 
to the trustee, on or before October 1 of each year, of the following 
amounts: I9II, $1,500,000; 1912, $1,500,000; 1913, $2,000,000; 
1914, $2,000,000; and the balance at maturity. 


Under the voting-trust agreement, a majority of the pre- 
ferred and common stock of the General Motors Company 
was deposited with a board of five members, three of whom 
represented the underwriting syndicate. The members of 
this board were: James N. Wallace, of the Central Trust 
Company; Frederick Strauss, of J. & W. Seligman & Co.; 
James J. Storrow, of Lee, Higginson & Co.; William C. 
Durant, vice-president of the General Motors Company; and 
Anthony N. Brady, a large stockholder. 

The stringent terms exacted by the banking syndicate for 
the underwriting of this note-issue offer abundant testimony 
of the uncertain, speculative character of the automobile 
industry, in the opinion of bankers and investors, at this 
period. Within the industry, the General Motors Company 
possessed excellent prospects: its constituents, together, con- 
stituted the largest single producer of automobiles in the 
country — their net sales in the year just then ended had 


THE GENERAL MOTORS CORPORATION 165 


exceeded $49,000,000; their business did not require the ex- 
tension of long credits to purchasers or the maintenance of 
large inventories of finished goods; the net profits of the 
Combination had exceeded $19,000,000 in the two years of 
its existence; and the demand for its products appeared to 
be increasing. 

The General Motors Company received about $12,500,- 
000 in cash for its $15,000,000 of notes.t In addition to the 
cash discount, the bankers received a bonus of $5,169,200 
par value of the Corporation’s preferred stock.? By arrange- 
ment with several of the large common stockholders, much 
of this preferred was exchanged for common stock in order 
to permit the bankers to offer a bonus of the latter with the 
notes. Voting-trust certificates for the preferred stock 
ranged in price on the New York Stock Exchange between 
$86.625 and $74.50 a share during 1911. At the lowest mar- 
ket price during 1911, the preferred stock bonus given the 
bankers had a cash value of $3,851,054. 

In view of the speculative character of the automobile 
industry in 1910, the terms imposed by the bankers (the 
voting-trust agreement gave them complete control of the 
board of directors and its principal committees, to which 
they elected themselves and individuals affiliated with them 
shortly after the issue of the notes) are believed in some 
quarters to have been fully warranted, and their assistance 
has been characterized as courageous and liberal. Little 
difficulty appears to have been experienced, nevertheless, in

in advance of the financing, states that the corporation received ‘about 
$12,000,000.’

applications are based on 96 and interest for the notes, with a 20 per 
cent stock bonus. The common stock has been dealt in on the New 
York Curb at 40 to 45 these last two or three weeks. ... 


In November, 1910, the bankers announced that all of the 
notes had been sold in advance of public offering.

Examination of the affairs of the Company by a firm of 
chartered accountants resulted in a report, issued November 
14, 1910, but applicable as of September 30, 1910, declaring 
that the Company’s net equities in its subsidiaries, exclusive 
of allowances for patents, good-will, agreements, etc., and 
exclusive of ‘other investments which are carried at a book 
value of $7,663,940,’ * were valued at $37,770,363; and that, 
after all bank loans and other maturing obligations had 
been met, the Company would have a cash balance of ap- 


* Consisting principally of the stocks of the Heany Lamp companies. 


168 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


proximately $3,000,000 from the proceeds of its note-issue.* 

The immediate direction of the Company was placed in 
the hands of a finance committee chosen from members of 
the board of directors. Of the eleven lines of motor vehi- 
cles only four were continued intact (the Buick, Cadillac, 
Olds, and Oakland); three were consolidated into a single 
unit, the General Motors Truck Company; and the remain- 
der were soon discontinued. Freed from its temporary 
financial embarrassment, the Company soon began to enjoy 
untroubled prosperity. On November 12, 1910, the board 
of directors voted the regular three and one-half per cent 
dividend on preferred stock, due October 1, but payment 
of which had been postponed. On March 3, I9QII, a cir- 
cular letter issued by Lee, Higginson & Co. declared that 
the plants of the Company were in full operation; that the 
earnings of two of the smaller subsidiaries, the Elmore and 
Northway companies, were exceeding those of the preceding 
year, when their earnings alone would have been adequate to 
meet the interest requirements of the outstanding note-issue; 
and that $500,000 had already been paid into the sinking 
fund for the purchase of notes in the open market.? 

An ambitious program of plant expansion and of acquisi- 
tion by purchase, financed and to be financed largely through 
the current earnings of its subsidiaries, had brought the Com- 
pany into the bankers’ hands. With the advent of the vot- 
ing trust, a conservative administrative policy was adopted; 
and the financial affairs of the Company soon reflected the 
unusually favorable conditions, as respects fixed and work- 
ing capital requirements, experienced by virtually all Ameri- 
can automobile producers. During the five years of the 
bankers’ control, dividends on preferred stock were paid 
regularly, though no disbursements were made to the com- 
mon stockholders; and earnings aggregating more than 


* Marwick, Mitchell & Co., quoted in the Commercial and Financial

* Letter quoted in the Commercial and Financial Chronicle, vol. 92, 
Pp. 264. 


THE GENERAL MOTORS CORPORATION 169 


$30,000,000 were reinvested in the business. Substantial 
additions were made to the plants of subsidiaries, and the 
Company had no difficulty in regularly anticipating the 
sinking-fund requirements of its note-issue. In addition to 
current charges for depreciation, the plant and inventory 
valuations of 1910 were reduced by $9,960,000 by charges 
against the growing surplus; and the book value of the Com- 
pany’s intangible assets, which had totaled $16,813,272 in 
1910, was reduced to $8,347,698. In 1915, the Company 
was able to redeem the last of its outstanding notes and to 
carry on a greatly augmented volume of business without 

-any borrowing whatever from commercial banks.? Some of 
the results of the Company’s operations during the five years 
of the bankers’ control and during the two years preceding 
are summarized in Table 31; and the comparative balance- 
sheets of 1910 and 1915, which, however, must be inter-

THE GENERAL MOTORS CORPORATION 171 


The substantial achievements of the voting trust, particu- 
larly as respects financial management, are generally con- 
ceded. As respects industrial expansion, however, the facts 
suggest that the Company might have fared better in the 
hands of an administration less wedded to conservatism. 
The period of the bankers’ control was one of tremendous 
growth in the automobile industry: the aggregate American 
output rose from 187,000 vehicles valued at $225,000,000 in 
1910 to 969,930 vehicles valued at $701,778,000 in 1915. The 
General Motors enterprises had contributed about 21 per 
cent of the total physical output and about 22 per cent of the 


_ wholesale value in 1910; by 1915, their combined share had 


fallen to about 7.8 per cent of the physical output and to 


about 13.3 per cent of the total wholesale value. One of the 


operating vice-presidents of the General Motors Corpora- 
tion, speaking informally in an interview with the present 
writer, declared: ? 


The bankers were too skeptical about the future of the automobile 
industry. They were chiefly interested in trying to realize savings, 
so they closed down some plants, concentrating in others. They 
didn’t take advantage of the opportunities. Under Durant, the 
Company might have had a little financial difficulty now and then, 
but it would have grown much faster and its earnings would have 
been much greater. 


As the final year of the voting trust drew to a close, the 
board of directors, in announcing the first cash dividend to 
be distributed to the common stockholders of the Company 
— a dividend of fifty per cent — declared: 3 


The common stock has never received a cash dividend since the 
company was organized on September 16, 1908. 

Your directors have followed the policy of building up the cash 
assets and credit of the company and have so accumulated and held 
large cash balances pending the maturity of the six per cent notes, the 
original issue of which was $15,000,000, and the last of which will be 
paid October 1, next.... 


* Facts and Figures of the Automobile Industry, 1927. 
* Name withheld by request. 
+ Annual report of General Motors Company for 1915. 


172 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


Your board does not believe in running into debt; hence one 
million dollars has been invested in short-time securities against 
probable extensions. 

The large write-offs amounting to $12,531,013.19, required dur- 
ing the past five years to bring your plants, machinery, merchan- 
dise, and other assets down to a conservative figure are now, we 
believe, at an end.... 

From the balance of cash available your directors have to-day de- 
clared a dividend of 50 per cent on the Common Stock, being $50 per 
share, payable October 15, 1915, to stockholders of record at the close 
of business, September 10, I9I5. 


8. REACCESSION OF WILLIAM C. DURANT: 
THE CHEVROLET MoToR COMPANY 


During the régime of the bankers’ control, William C. 
Durant, erstwhile promoter of the General Motors Com- 
pany and chairman of its executive committee, had played a 
passive réle in the administration of the enterprise. Though 
a member of the board of voting trustees, he had transferred 
his energies, shortly after the accession of the bankers, to the 
organization of a new automobile enterprise, the Chevrolet 
Motor Company. This enterprise, incorporated in Michigan 
on November 6, 1911, acquired a small automobile factory 
operated by William H. Little, and started manufacture of 
an automobile designed principally by Louis Chevrolet, a 
well-known racing pilot. In 1913, soon after production in 
commercial volume was under way, Durant and his associ- 
ates obtained control of the Republic Motor Company, of 
Tarrytown, New York, and, after changing its name to the 
Chevrolet Motor Company of New York, Inc., operated the 
Tarrytown factory as an assembly plant for Chevrolet ve- 
hicles, most of the parts being purchased from parts-makers.* 
The Chevrolet automobile struck the public fancy: output 
in the two years ended August 14, 1915, totaled 15,919 ve- 
hicles; net sales, $11,707,954; and net profits, $1,305,784; ? 


t Cf. listing application of General Motors Corporation to the New 
York Stock Exchange, January 20, 1919. 
* Ibid. 


THE GENERAL MOTORS CORPORATION 173 


and a number of associated Chevrolet enterprises were soon 
organized in various cities. 

Durant’s ulterior object in establishing a New York 
Chevrolet factory was that of interesting Eastern capitalists 
in his automobile projects, and, possibly, of procuring the 
support of some of them in an attempt to regain control of 
the General Motors Company, according to one of his 
associates, who declared :? 

The automobile business was foreign to these Eastern bankers. 
They wouldn’t come all the way out to Michigan to see what the 
_ industry was doing. With a factory at Tarrytown, we thought that 
they could run up and see it; and even if it were only an assembling 
plant, they would be impressed by seeing the actual automobiles 
_being turned out. That was Durant’s object in this Tarrytown 

factory — to get a few of them interested in his operations. 


Durant evidently succeeded fully in this object. As the 
expiration of the voting trust drew near, great activity 
was displayed in the New York stock market by the 
voting-trust certificates for the common and _ preferred 
stocks of the Company. Trading in these securities — 
the first stocks of any automobile enterprise to be listed 
on the Exchange?— had been relatively light for the 
first three or four years, and fluctuations in the price of 
the common stock, while substantial, had not been ex- 
ceptional: between 1911 and the beginning of 1915, the 
‘low’ price had ranged between 25 and 37.37, and the 
‘high,’ between 40 and 99.3 Early in-1915, however, Durant 
and his new supporters — principally, Pierre S. Du Pont, of 
E. I. Du Pont de Nemours & Co., and Louis G. Kaufman, 
president of the Chatham & Phcenix National Bank, New 
York — began to purchase General Motors common stock 
in the open market,‘ and a startling ascent in its price was

soon under way: on January 2, 1915, the closing price of the 
stock was 82; by December 9g, 1915, it reached a ‘high’ 
of 558. On October I, 1915, in the meantime, when the vot- 
ing trust expired, Durant and his associates controlled 
enough stock to elect nearly a clear majority of the board of 
directors; and the chairmanship of the board went to Pierre 
S. Du Pont, Durant’s chief supporter. 

In order to place his tenuous, but effective, control of 
General Motors on a surer basis, Durant proceeded to effect 
a reorganization of his Chevrolet enterprises. The latter had 
been established without public financing, most of their 
securities having been issued for property and services.‘ On 
September 23, 1915, Durant organized the Chevrolet Motor 
Company of Delaware; on October 21, $13,200,000 par 
value of the authorized capital stock ($20,000,000, all com- 
mon, of $100 par value) was exchanged for the stocks of the 
Michigan and New York Chevrolet companies and of several 
small enterprises; and $6,800,000 par value was offered to 
the public through an underwriting syndicate headed by 
Hornblower & Weeks.? This firm announced that the man- 
agement of the Chevrolet Motor Company (Delaware) was 
identified with that of the General Motors Company; that its 
product was to be a new low-priced passenger car; that the 
current earnings of the constituent companies were at the 
rate of $2,000,000 a year; and that the purpose of the public 
offering was to provide funds for increasing the output from 
100 to 300 cars a day.3 The association of Durant’s name 
with the enterprise led numerous motor-car dealers and Gen- 
eral Motors stockholders to subscribe to the new stock; 4 

* The original capitalization of the Chevrolet Motor Company of 
Michigan, the parent and most important of the group, was $100,000; 
this was increased, a month after the incorporation, to $2,500,000 (all 
common stock), of which $316,900 was listed as issued for cash; on June 
18, 1913, $500,000 of the common stock was converted into seven per 
cent cumulative full-voting preferred stock; and on this date, $999,500 of 


the common stock was listed as issued. Cf. articles of incorporation and 
amendments as filed with the Michigan Secretary of State.

and on October 1, before the issue had been formally author- 
ized, the underwriting syndicate announced that the sub- 
scription books had been closed, applications for the stock 
having exceeded ten times the amount offered. The public 
sale was at $85 a share,’ the price to the underwriters, about 
$65 ; ?and, soon after the books were closed, the stock sold on 
the New York Curb market at $97 a share. 

No sooner had this reorganization and financing been 
completed than Durant approached a number of the larger 
stockholders of the General Motors Company, many of whom 
had been associated with him in its original promotion, 
and offered to exchange stock in the Chevrolet Motor Com- 
pany (Delaware) for their holdings of General Motors at the 
rate of five shares of the former for each share of the latter.4 
The response to this offer was evidently adequate, for on 
December 23, 1915, the stockholders of the Chevrolet Motor 
Company voted to increase the authorized capital stock 
from $20,000,000 to $80,000,000 (all common); and author- 
ized the board of directors to issue the additional stock as 
the latter saw fit, specifically empowering them to issue 
Chevrolet stock for General Motors common stock at a 
ratio not greater than five to one.’ Durant and his associates 
exchanged a large portion of their General Motors holdings 
at this rate; and a public offering of Chevrolet stock on a 
similar basis met with sufficient response to warrant a clos- 
ing of the offering on January 25, 1916.° 

The evident plan of the Chevrolet Motor Company to ac- 
quire control of the General Motors Company met with the 
opposition of a number of the directors of the latter company; 
and in January, 1916, a circular letter, signed by eight mem-

bers of the board of directors of the General Motors Com- 
pany, was addressed to the latter’s stockholders, proposing 
a new voting trust of three years, and inviting the codpera- 
tion of the stockholders. The letter read as follows:? 


The undersigned directors have received many inquiries in regard 
to current reports that the control of the General Motors Company 
was about to be acquired by another company, and asking informa- 
tion in the premises. 

Under the circumstances, they would state that no one of the 
undersigned is a party to any arrangement looking to the vesting of 
control of the General Motors Company in any other corporation. 

We believe that the results achieved with President Nash at the 
head of the Company have been satisfactory to the stockholders. 
It has been suggested by some shareholders that it would be well 
to form a three-year voting trust beginning November, 1916, at 
which date the term of the present board of directors expires. The 
majority of the present board of directors favors this plan. Witha 
sufficient number of stockholders wishing to unite in forming this 
voting trust, action to that end will be taken. 

The slip enclosed, on which the shareholders may express their 
wish for a voting trust of five members of the present board acting as 
trustees, to be selected by the present board of directors, should be 
returned at once to Standish Backus, Secretary of the General Mo- 
tors Company, Detroit, Mich. 


The document was signed by Samuel F. Pryer, Albert 
H. Wiggin, Thomas Neal, Charles H. Sabin, J. J. Storrow, 
C. S. Mott, Albert Strauss, and Emory Clark. 

The response to the letter was probably slight, for on 
March 18, 1916, another appeal was addressed to the Gen- 
eral Motors stockholders, this time signed by thirteen 
stockholders ‘unconnected with the management.’ *? Among 
other things, the writers declared: 

Granting that the present effort to vest control of the General 
Motors Company in the Chevrolet Motor Company has not suc- 
ceeded, it is quite possible that a renewed effort in this direction will 
be made or that an attempt may be made to change the control by 


some other method.... 
We do not believe that control by the Chevrolet Motor Company 


* Quoted in the Commercial and Financial Chronicle, vol. 102, p. 157. 
2 Ibid., vol. 102, p. 1063. 


THE GENERAL MOTORS CORPORATION 177 


would result in any improvement in the management. The following 
have consented to act (as trustees of the three-year Voting Trust 
proposed): C. W. Nash, E. W. Clark, C. S. Mott, C. H. Sabin, J. J. 
Storrow, and Albert Strauss. 


This appeal, as the preceding one, bore little fruit; and the 
evident strength of the Durant support is believed to have 
caused a number of the large stockholding interests to dis- 
pose of their holdings, thus facilitating the attainment of 
Durant’s objective. Mr. Pierre S. Du Pont, in answer to 
some queries addressed to him by the present writer, de- 
clared:* | 

The purchase of General Motors stock (and afterward its ex- 
change in part for Chevrolet Motor Company stock) by the E. I. 
Du Pont de Nemours & Company undoubtedly had an influence on 
Mr. Durant’s control of General Motors Corporation, as the rela- 
tions of the Du Pont Company with Mr. Durant were always cordial 
and coéperative. However, I believe that the sale of stock by inter- 
ests that might otherwise have been adverse to Mr. Durant, had a 
greater influence in strengthening the latter’s control. I do not be- 
lieve that the Du Pont Company and Mr. Durant ever had an actual 
majority of shares, but they, together with their following, that is, 
investors who were willing to trust the management of the company 
to them, undoubtedly represented the large majority. 


Ten weeks after the issue of the second appeal for the 
formation of a voting trust, William C. Durant, president of 
the Chevrolet Motor Company, succeeded Charles W. Nash 
as president of the General Motors Company; and a short 
time later, James J. Storrow, Emory W. Clark, and Albert 
Strauss resigned from the board of directors. The Chevrolet 
Motor Company had acquired a clear majority of General 
Motors common stock! ?

With Durant once more in control, the General Motors 
Company again pursued a policy of vigorous expansion. The

After distributing $11,779,123 in dividends, the Company 
ended the fiscal year with a net addition of $17,010,437 to 
its surplus account, and with a cash balance of $22,476,575. 
Its earnings applicable to common stock had exceeded 168 
per cent of the par value of the latter, and, in consequence, 
the market value of the common stock reached new heights.? 
On August 31, 1916, in order, among other things, ‘to afford 
the present stockholders a more liquid and satisfactory in- 
vestment,’ the board of directors voted to readjust the cap- 
italization of the Company.’ On September 16, 1916, it was 
announced that a plan for the formation of a new company 
had received the approval of a majority of the stockholders, 
and would go into effect on November I. 

The General Motors Corporation was incorporated in 
Delaware on October 13, 1916, and acquired the capital 
stock of the General Motors Company (New Jersey) on the 
following terms: one and one-third shares of the six per cent 
preferred stock of the new company were exchanged for each 
share of seven per cent preferred of the old (a total of 199,803 
of the new for 149,582 of the old); and five shares of the new 
company’s common stock were exchanged for each common 
share of the old (a total of 825,590 for 165,118); 4 the new 
was 825,589. Five shares of General Motors Corporation common stock 


had been issued in exchange for each share of General Motors Company 
common stock.

stocks, like the old, were of $100 par value. The General 
Motors Corporation then proceeded to dissolve most of its 
operating subsidiaries, acquiring direct title to their physi- 
cal assets; sales companies of nominal capital ($10,000 in 
each case) replaced the sales organizations of the constitu- 
ents; and, on August 1, 1917, when these changes had been 
completed, the General Motors Corporation formally be- 
came an operating rather than a holding company, prima- 
rily.? 

Durant, in the meantime, continued vigorously to extend 
the operations of both the General Motors and Chevrolet 
enterprises. In the seventeen months between July 31, 1916, 
and December 31, 1917, the fixed-plant investment of Gen- 
eral Motors more than doubled, reaching $38,657,835 on the 
latter date; its inventories rose from $25,100,450 to $46,- 
559,394; and its net quick assets increased from $22,706,207 
to $55,943,929. Reinvested profits in this period totaled 
$27,810,043, after more than $11,000,000 had been distrib- 
uted in dividends.?, The Chevrolet enterprise likewise made 
great strides. In 1915, it had introduced a new low-priced 
car retailing at $550. Previous to this time, the Ford Motor 
Company alone had catered effectively to this largest price- 
class of automobile purchasers. So great was the demand for 
automobiles of such price, moreover, that the Ford com- 
pany’s chief problem was the adequate expansion of its out- 
put; and the Chevrolet company was therefore able to ab- 
sorb part of this waiting market. Branch plants, financed in 
part by local capital, were established in different cities; a 
truck-producing subsidiary was organized; and investments 
were made in various parts-making enterprises. Independ- 
ently of its control of General Motors, therefore, the Chevro- 
let company was developing into an important automobile-

Shortly after the beginning of 1918, negotiations were 
begun for the acquisition by the General Motors Corpora- 
tion of the Chevrolet Motor Company and its subsidiaries. 
Since the latter Company owned a clear majority of the 
common stock of the former (450,000 out of 768,733 shares 
outstanding, exclusive of treasury holdings), and since the 
Durant-Du Pont holdings were adequate for effective control 
of both,? an agreement was speedily concluded, whereby all 
the assets of the Chevrolet Motor Company (Delaware), 
except its holdings of General Motors common stock, were 
to be transferred to the General Motors Corporation in re- 
turn for $28,268,400 par value of the latter’s common stock 
and the assumption by the latter of the outstanding liabil-

Since the net worth of the Chevrolet Motor Company of 
Michigan, two months after its acquisition by the Delaware 
Chevrolet corporation, was only $2,598,156, and that of the 
New York Company much less;? and since the Delaware 
company received only sixty-five cents on the dollar for the 
stock sold for cash, we may conclude that considerably more 
than one-half of the original $20,000,000 of capital stock re- 
presented promotion services and financing charges. The 
stock issued in exchange for General Motors common stock 
had proved to be a profitable investment, apart from the 
control it purchased, for the Chevrolet company received 
dividends aggregating $4,500,000 in 1917 and $2,700,000 in 
the first four months of 1918, from this source. Most of the 
‘water’ in the original capitalization of the Chevrolet Motor 
Company (Delaware) had been eliminated through the rein- 
vestment of earnings by the time the enterprise passed to the 
General Motors Corporation: of the aggregate income of the 
former, up to April 30, 1918, $9,601,210 had been distributed 
to its stockholders, and $13,309,511 had been reinvested in 
the business.2, The General Motors Corporation paid $1.12 
par value of its common stock per dollar of the net tangible 
book assets acquired from the Chevrolet company, and 
seventy-eight cents per dollar of the latter’s total net book 
assets, inclusive of ‘good-will.’ General Motors common 
stock sold on the New York Stock Exchange at prices rang- 
ing between $106.75 and $164 a share during 1918, however. 
The net operating earnings of the Chevrolet Motor Com- 
pany during the fiscal year just preceding the purchase had 
been at the rate of 17.5 per cent on the par value of General 
Motors’ investment in the Company.

10. ACQUISITION OF UNITED Motors CorporATION, ETC. 


The purchase of the Chevrolet Motor Company had not 
been consummated before the General Motors Corporation 
took steps to acquire the assets of a large combination of 
parts- and accessory-manufacturers, known as the United 
Motors Corporation. This combination had been promoted 
by Durant shortly after his reaccession to the control of 
General Motors, with the purpose, in part, of protecting the 
latter’s sources of supplies.t The Company had been incor- 
porated in New York on May 16, 1916, with an authorized 
capital stock of 1,200,000 shares of no par value, 5000 of 
which, denominated Class B stock, possessed sole voting 
power. It had issued 1,101,640 shares in gross for the follow- 
ing: ? , 

Dayton Engineering Laboratories Company — entire issue of 
common stock ($150,000 par value). 

Remy Electric Company — entire issue of outstanding common 
stock ($796,300 par value). 

Hyatt Roller Bearing Company — entire outstanding capital stock 

($399,700 par value, later reduced to $150,000). 

New Departure Manufacturing Company — 23,429 shares ($2,342,- 

900 par value) of the 25,000 shares of common stock outstanding. 
Perlman Rim Corporation — 97,637 shares of no par value of the 

100,000 shares outstanding. 


A large block of the stock thus issued — it is impossible to 
say how much — went to the promoters: Durant is said to 
have received a block valued at $9,000,000, and is known to 
have donated 106,000 shares of the issue to the Chevrolet 
Motor Company (Delaware); 3 Louis G. Kaufman, president 
of the Chatham & Phcenix National Bank (New York), 
who participated in the promotion, likewise is said to have 


1 See announcement by Durant, quoted in the Commercial and Finan- 
cial Chronicle, vol. 102, p. 1816. 

2 From listing application of General Motors Corporation, January 20, 
1919.

received a block valued at $9,000,000.! Some of the stock 
obtained by individuals was sold by them to investment 
bankers, who offered the shares to the public in May, 1916, 
at $62 a share.? 

The United Motors Corporation started purely as a hold- 
ing company; it soon acquired the physical assets of the com- 
panies of which it owned the entire capital stock, however; 
and, with the profits derived from its constituents, it made 
direct investments in other enterprises. One of its divisions, 
the Jaxon Steel Products unit, took over the physical assets 
of the Perlman Rim Corporation, which, like the United 
Motors Combination, had been sponsored by W. C. Durant. 
The Corporation added to its original parts-making enter- 
prises by acquiring a controlling interest in the Harrison 
Radiator Corporation and in the Klaxon Company, pro- 
ducers of automobile radiators and horns, respectively; and 
it established a servicing organization, known as the United 
Motors Service, Inc., to provide repair service for its various 
lines of accessories. In the two years and three months 
ended September 30, 1918, the aggregate profits of this Com- 
bination had totaled $18,487,519, of which $17,944,083 had 
been reinvested in the business.‘ 

On August 27, 1918, the authorized capitalization of the 
General Motors Corporation was increased as follows: pre- 
ferred stock, from $50,000,000 to $100,000,000; common 
stock, from $150,000,000 to $200,000,000. A month later, 
the General Motors Corporation offered to acquire the 
assets of the United Motors Corporation, and to assume the 
latter’s liabilities, paying therefor: $33,049,200 par value of

General Motors preferred stock and $11,016,400 par value

it developed that the General Motors Corporation had 
agreed with the Listing Committee of the New York Stock 
Exchange that it would issue no additional preferred stock 
possessing priority equal to that of the existing issue.? Ata 
special meeting of the stockholders, held December 10, 
1918, it was decided, therefore, to decrease the authorized 
issue of preferred stock to $20,000,000 par value, and to 
authorize the issue of $150,000,000 par value of 6 per cent 
debenture stock, $33,049,200 of which was to be issued in- 
stead of preferred stock as part payment for the assets of 
the United Motors Corporation, and $20,000,000 of which 
was to be reserved for the redemption of the outstanding 
preferred stock. 

The new debenture stock was given distinctive rights and 
privileges as compared with those of the then existing issue 
of preferred stock. Its dividend rate, like that of the pre- 
ferred stock, was six per cent, and cumulative, but: (1) it 
was given equal voting rights with common in the event 
that the Corporation’s net earnings were not at least one 
and one-half times the amount required for the payment of 
debenture-stock dividends; (2) in the event of a six months’ 
default in debenture-stock dividends, it was to be given sole 
voting power, to the exclusion of the common stock; (3) no 
new debenture stock was to be issued unless the net assets 
of the Corporation above all direct liabilities other than 
capital stock should equal one and one-half times the 
amount of debenture stock outstanding after the new issue; 
(4) no mortgage was to be placed on any of the assets of the 
Corporation, other than purchase-money mortgages, the 
assumption of mortgage liabilities on property purchased, 
and the pledge of stocks for periods less than three years,

869,200 and $9,956,400, respectively, of these stocks.?

In the United Motors group of enterprises, the General 
Motors Corporation acquired a number of the most impor- 
tant parts- and accessories-producers in the country. The 
par value of the securities issued for this acquisition con- 
stituted approximately 78.6 per cent of the book value of 
the assets acquired, inclusive of intangible items valued at 
$21,536,155; excluding intangible assets, the General Motors 
paid approximately $1.28 in par value of its securities per 
dollar of net tangible assets. The net income of the United 
Motors Corporation in the year ended June 30, 1918, had 
been $6,926,851,? or about 15.7 per cent of the par value of 
General Motors’ investment. It is interesting to note that 
nearly one-half of the net tangible assets of the United 
Motors group had been built up through the reinvested 
profits of the period of two years and three months ended 
September 30, 1918.?

In its annual report for 1918, E. I. Du Pont de Nemours 
& Co. formally announced its acquisition of a ‘27.6 per cent 
interest in the common stock of the General Motors Cor- 
poration,’ and declared: 


We feel fortunate also in our partnership with Mr. William C. 
Durant, president of the General Motors Corporation and the father 
and leader of the motor industry not only in the United States, but 
in the world. The alliance leaves the management and general con- 
duct of the General Motors Corporation as heretofore except that 
the responsibility for financial management is now shared by the 
officers of our company. 


THE GENERAL MOTORS CORPORATION 191 


President Durant, in his annual report of 1918, recipro- 
cated by declaring: 

The Genera] Motors Corporation is to be congratulated upon the 
association with the E. I. Du Pont de Nemours & Company re- 
sulting from the acquisition of a large stock interest by that com- 
pany which insures financial stability and the codperation of the 
finest engineering organization in the country. 


11. THE Post-War Boom 


The year 1919 was one of unparalleled prosperity for the 
General Motors Corporation, as, indeed, for American in- 
dustry as a whole. Under the impetus of the post-war boom 
and of the accumulated demand for automobiles, the pro- 
duction of which, like that of other goods, had been curtailed 
during the War, the General Motors Corporation increased 
its output of cars, trucks, and tractors by nearly sixty per 
cent, as compared with the output of 1918. Its financial 
volume of sales, reflecting in part the large acquisitions of 
the year previous, rose above half a billion dollars — an in- 
crease of nearly ninety per cent. And the net profits of the 
Corporation jumped from less than $15,000,000 in 1918 to 
more than $60,000,000 — an increase of more than three 
hundred per cent. 

A tremendous expansion in the fixed investment of the 
Corporation accompanied these increases in its volume of 
business.! Approximately $20,000,000 was expended for 
plant additions to the car-producing factories. Work was 
begun on a $20,000,000 office building in Detroit, which ab- 
sorbed more than $4,000,000 during 1919. A sixty per cent 
stock interest was acquired in the Fisher Body Corporation, 
the largest producer of automobile bodies in the world, 
General Motors paying therefor approximately $5,800,000 
in cash and $21,851,000 in five-year serial notes.? In addi-

expended for a large housing-construction program, initiated 
for the benefit of the Corporation’s employees; the organiz- 
ation of the General Motors Acceptance Corporation to aid 
in financing distributors, dealers, and retail purchasers; and 
the acquisition of new or additional interests in nine parts- 
making enterprises and in the Guardian Refrigerator Com- 
pany (later, Frigidaire, producer of electric refrigerators), 
the Dayton Products Company (producer of detonators, 
pressure indicators, etc.), and the Domestic Engineering 
Company (producer of Delco-Light power plants). In all, 
in the single year 1919, the General Motors Corporation 
increased its ‘investments in unconsolidated companies’ by 
$50,558,960; expended $47,741,698 directly for real estate, 
plant, and equipment; acquired physical assets valued at 
$12,439,459, less $6,063,229 for depreciation, by consolida-

penditures of more than $79,000,000 were required to com- 
plete this expansion program.? 

In May, 1919, the Corporation Aur an issue of $30,000,000 
of debenture stock, from which it realized $25,425,000; it 
converted $28,638,780 of Liberty bonds into cash; and, of 
the profits of 1919, $38,468,431 was reinvested in its busi- 
ness.3 From these sources the Corporation procured most of 
latter, under a five-year voting trust. An agreement dated November 9, 
1917, provided that the General Motors Corporation purchase sub- 
stantially all its automobile bodies from the Fisher corporation for a 
period of ten years at prices equal to cost of production plus 17.6 per 
cent, and that the Fisher corporation distribute quarterly dividends 
aggregating at least $10 a year per share of common stock, so far as 
earnings permitted. The Fisher Body Corporation was consolidated 


with General Motors in 1926. (Cf. annual reports for 1919 and 1926, and 
Standard Statistics, Bulletin F 11.)

The expansion of the fixed capital investment of the Cor- 
poration was not accompanied by a corresponding increase 
in working capital. Despite the huge increases in the former 
and the tremendous growth in the Corporation’s volume of 
business, its net working assets at the end of 1919 were only 
$8,852,472 greater than at the end of the preceding year. 
The ratio of its current assets to current liabilities had fallen 
from 5.55 to I, to 3.84 to 1; and much of these current as- 
sets consisted of specialized inventories easily subject to 
‘freezing’ in the event of a falling-off in the rate of auto- 
mobile production. To facilitate the raising of new working 
capital, the Corporation’s stockholders, on June 12, 1919, 
had approved an increase in the authorized amounts of 
debenture and preferred stocks to $500,000,000 each. The 
original offering of the debenture stock underwritten just 
previously ($30,000,000), to which allusion has been made 
above, was for $50,000,000. Due, probably, to the heavy 
discount at which the Corporation was forced to issue this 
stock (15.25 per cent, to the underwriters 5), the remaining 

1 Ibid. 

® Commercial and Financial Chronicle, vol. 108, p. 2025, vol. 109, Pp. 176, 

3 Cf. annual reports for 1919 and 1920. 


194 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


$20,000,000 of the offer was withdrawn; but the need for 
additional working capital remained. 

On November 26, 1919, the board of directors voted to 
recommend to the stockholders the adoption of the follow- 
ing changes in the capitalization of the Corporation: ? 


1. Common Stock: That the authorized amount of common stock 
be changed from 5,000,000 shares of $100 par value to 50,000,000 
shares of no par value; and that 15,699,190 shares of the new stock 
be issued in exchange for the 1,569,919 shares of outstanding com- 
mon stock, at the rate of 10 shares of the new stock for each share of 
the old. 

2. Six per cent Debenture Stock: That the authorized amount 
of six per cent debenture stock be reduced from $500,000,000 to 
$90,000,000, par value. 

3. Seven per cent Debenture Stock: That $500,000,000 of seven 
per cent debenture stock be authorized; that this stock rank equally 
with the six per cent debenture and six per cent preferred stocks as 
respects priority in dividends and in assets in case of dissolution; 
that its dividends be cumulative; that in the case of default in divi- 
dend payments for six months, the seven per cent debenture stock 
share with the six per cent debenture stock the exclusive right to 
vote, as against the common and preferred stocks; that the two de- 
benture issues share equally with the common stock the right to vote 
in case the net earnings during any year be less than nine per cent of 
the par value of the outstanding six and seven per cent debenture 
issues; that the consent of three-fourths of -the debenture issues be 
required to enable the Corporation to mortgage its property (ex- 
cept for purchase-money mortgages maturing within three years); 
and that the seven per cent debenture stock be redeemable on any 
dividend date at $120 and accrued dividends per share. 

4. Preferred Stock: That no change be made in the preferred 
stock issue. 


In connection with these recommendations, President 
Durant announced that holders of the existing six per cent 
debenture and preferred stocks would receive subscription 
warrants permitting them to purchase two shares of the 
seven per cent debenture stock at par for each share of the 
older issues held, payment to be made either wholly in cash 
or fifty per cent in cash and fifty per cent in six per cent 


* General Motors listing application of June 23, 1919. | 


\

debenture or preferred stock.t In the same announcement, | 


explaining the purpose of the new debenture issue, President 
Durant declared: 


Large capital investments will be necessary which will be better 
financed by a seven per cent senior security than by a six per cent 
senior security. If all subscription rights are taken up, the plan will 
bring in $85,000,000 in cash, which, added to the present cash items, 
will make $175,000,000, enough to care for a gross business of 
$800,000,000 in 1920. 


The plan was ratified by the stockholders at a special 
meeting on January 6, 1920, and temporary certificates for 
the new debenture stock were issued immediately.? Despite 
special appeals to the stockholders, pointing out that 
holders of the existing preferred securities had an oppor- 
tunity to substitute a seven per cent return for a six per 
cent return by the investment of an additional $100 per 
share owned,3 only $10,998,700 par value of the offering of 
$217,602,400 was subscribed; and since $4,091,900 of these 
subscriptions was paid in the form of stock, the Corporation 
realized only $6,906,800 in cash from the offering, instead of 
the expected $85,000,000.4 

In this predicament, with many of the large units of its 
building program still in process of construction, and with

tion sought and procured the intermediation of J. P. Morgan 
& Co. On June 2, 1920, the board of directors voted to issue 
3,287,803 shares of the new no-par value common stock at 
$20 a share, the issue to be underwritten by J. P. Morgan &

12. THE Post-WaAR CRIsIS 


The proceeds of the sale of common stock netted the Gen- 
eral Motors Corporation about $60,000,000 by December 
31, 1920 (254,764 shares having not been fully paid for at 
that time); and the funds and financial backing thus ac- 
quired were of tremendous aid to the Corporation in meet- 
ing the emergency created by the sudden curtailment of the 
demand for its products that set in in August and Septem- 
ber, 1920. 

The year 1920 had begun very auspiciously. The Cor- 
poration had just completed the most successful year in its 
history. The demand for its cars had continued at a high 
level throughout the closing quarter of the year just ended; 
and this demand increased afresh in the early months of 
1920. Both volume of sales and net profits promised to 
exceed the record-breaking volume of 1919; and indeed, this 
promise was realized for financial volume of sales. On Feb- 
ruary 2, 1920, the Corporation paid a cash dividend of $3 a 
share to the common stockholders of record on December
