494,835. This figure includes, however, stock issued as a bonus to its 
employees, stock issued for the assets of the Chevrolet Motor Company 
of California, and stock issued as stock dividends on the common stock 

— all calculated at $20 a share. 


198 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


31, 1919; on May 1, it paid an initial quarterly dividend of 
25 cents in cash and one-fortieth of a share of common stock 
on its new no-par value common stock; it paid similar 
dividends on August 2 and on November 1, 1920. This was 
equivalent to an annual rate of return of $10 in cash, and 
(at a valuation of $20 per share of no-par value stock) of 
$20 in stock on the preceding common stock of $100 par 
value. (The no-par common stock sold at a ‘high’ of $42 
a share on March 26, 1920, and did not decline to less than 
$20 a share until after the week of September 21, 1920.) 

The early promise and actuality of an increased volume 
of business, however, led to heavy increases in the volume 
of inventories. Starting at the beginning of the year at 
$128,696,652 (as compared with $91,137,513 at the opening 
of 1919), the inventories mounted rapidly, reaching a total 
of $167,965,641 on April 30, and $209,000,000 by the end 
of October, when a tremendous decline in the demand for 
the Corporation's products had already set in.* 

The large increase in the financial volume of inventories 
occasioned concern as early as the month of March, 1920; 
and at that time, the Executive Committee approved a 
definite program of production with a view toward minimiz- 
ing the inventory requirements.? The administrative organ- 
ization of the Corporation, however, made it extremely 
difficult to enforce the rulings of the Executive Committee. 
While the Corporation had become chiefly an operating com- 
pany, the actual administration of its plants was in the hands 
of divisional managements, each concerned with the ex- 
panded production of its own product. In May, 1920, a 
special Inventory Allotment Committee was appointed to 
establish a more effective control over the inventory de- 
mands of the various divisions; but the rulings of this com- 
mittee obtained no more adherence on the part of the operat- 
ing divisions than had the more general injunctions of two 
months previous. Thus, in his annual report for 1922, Presi- 
dent Du Pont declared: 

t Annual report for 1922. * Thid, 


THE GENERAL MOTORS CORPORATION 199 


The report of the Inventory Allotment Committee was presented 
and approved before June 1, 1920. It was unfortunate that the rul- 
ings of the Executive and Finance Committees and their cautions 
remained unheeded. As a result, inventories reached a total of 
$209,000,000 at the end of October, 1920, exceeding by $60,000,000 
the allotments of the Executive and Finance Committees and by 
$100,000,000 the amount in actual use during the active summer of 
1922. This excess accounted for about 70 per cent of the borrowings 
at that time. 

It was doubly unfortunate that the spirit of the committee rulings 
was totally disregarded by a few of the Divisions, the losses of which, 
due to expanded inventories and commitments for the future, 
amounted to $48,579,872, or much more than the total operating de- 
ficit of the whole corporation during the year 1921. The operating 
losses of these Divisions during the liquidation and reconstruction 
period of 1921 added $15,330,938, making a total of $63,910,810 on 
their account. : 


Not only was the Corporation cailed upon to finance its 
growing volume of inventories, however, but the fulfillment 
of its program of fixed-plant expansion, involving the com- 
pletion of construction projects already under way, entailed 
a capital expenditure of approximately $79,161,951 in 1920, 
nearly $15,000,000 of which was required merely for its new 
office and administration building in Detroit. A sudden 
decline in sales in the late summer and fall of 1920, there- 
fore, created an exceedingly embarrassing situation. The 
extent of this decline and its effect, in view of the Corpora- 
tion’s greatly expanded investment in fixed plant and in- 
ventories, may be seen from Table 32, in which the Corpo- 
ration’s sales of cars and trucks are presented by months 
for 1919 and 1920.7 

On its face, the ability of the General Motors Corporation 
to weather this storm, the results of which were seriously 
aggravated by the obsolescence and decline in value of 
inventories, plant, and equipment purchased or constructed 
at ‘boom’ prices, was fairly remarkable. More than $100,- 
00,000 was written off the books of the Corporation be-

favoring factors may be noted and emphasized: 

(1) The Corporation’s program of fixed-capital expansion 
had been soundly financed: it had not entailed the flagrant 
absorption of working capital in fixed assets nor the use of 
short-term obligations for such additions. Indeed, except for 
purchase-money obligations and small mortgages assumed, 
it had not involved the creation of any funded debt whatever.

marized in Table 33.3

condition permitted considerable extension without serious

jeopardy. The ratio of current assets to current liabilities 
at the end of 1918 was 5.5 to 1; at the end of 1919, 3.84 to 
1; at the end of 1920, 2.24 tor. The last-named ratio reflects 
large ‘write-offs’ for inventory depreciation, and, like the 
others, allows for accruals of interest and of preferred and 
debenture dividends. We have several times observed, more- 
over, that the working-capital requirements of automobile 
producers were minimized by various practices peculiar to 
the industry. Finally, we may note that the new capital 
investment in the Corporation between 1918 and 1920 ex- 
ceeded the total of fixed-capital expenditures by more than 
$75,000,000. (See Table 33.) 

(3) The ability of the General Motors Corporation to 
withstand the situation occasioned by the sharp slump in 
its sales in the latter part of 1920 is not adequately explained, 

} however, by reference to the fact that its current assets at 


202 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


the worst were approximately two and one-quarter times the 
amount of its current liabilities. To the extent that these 
assets consisted of inventories that could not be liquidated 
presently, the working capital ratio is misleading. The 
actual cash position of the Corporation and its ability to 
borrow were, in fact, of far greater importance. In these 
emergency essentials, the Corporation was fortunate indeed. 
The net earnings of 1920, before special ‘write-offs’ and be- 
fore the setting-up of a ‘reserve for unforeseen contingen- 
cies,’ but after all other operating and income charges save 
dividends, had aggregated $67,273,313; the Corporation had 
realized approximately $60,000,000 in cash from the sale of 
common stock in July, 1920; and, with the support of the 
powerful financial group that had become identified with its 
financial management in connection with this stock issue, it 
was able to borrow a maximum (on October 13, 1920) of 
$82,784,824 on its short-term notes in the midst of the acute 
financial situation of late 1920." By reason, most immedi- 
ately, of these financial operations, the Corporation was able 
not only to maintain its solvency, but to pay full dividends 
on its debenture and preferred stocks, totaling $5,620,246, 
in 1920, and to disburse quarterly in 1921, from the earn- 
ings of 1920, a total of $17,893,288 in cash dividends on 
common stock.

The turn in the stock market came abruptly. Between 
March 27 and May 1, 1920, the stock dropped from $376 
to $295 a share (a stock dividend of two and one-half per 
cent accounted for some of this decline); and the new no- 
par value common stock, ten shares of which had been is- 
sued for each share of the old, was sold at $24 a share on 
May 24. From this time forward to the end of 1920, with 
few interruptions, the price of the stock continued to fall. 
The following table shows the decline in the stock-market 
quotations of General Motors common stock during the 
last eight months of 1920." 

During this continuous decline in the market value of 
General Motors common stock, William C. Durant, presi- 
dent of the Corporation, had been constantly buying the 
stock on his own account to support the market.? Not only 
did he support the market directly, but also, aided by certain

in his motor-car enterprises.

of Pierre S. Du Pont.

14. LIQUIDATION AND OPERATING REORGANIZATION 


The depression of 1921 was acute, if short-lived; and, as in 
the case of many another American enterprise that had ex- 
panded in undisciplined fashion during the boom period just 
preceding, the General Motors Corporation made the period 
of liquidation the occasion for a thoroughgoing houseclean- 
ing. 

No apologies were voiced by the management for the 
previous expansion program: 


... the troubles of the past years were not related to an ill-financed 
expansion program or to delay in receiving the proceeds of financing. 
It is certain that the funds provided before the close of the year 1920 
were sufficient to carry out the whole program and also to finance new 
business offered during the year 1921 and the first half of the year 
1922. It is equally certain that disregard for control of inventories 
and purchase commitments cost the Corporation a very large sum of 
money, of which the greater part might have been saved by proper 
safeguards in Divisions now differently managed. Further, it is im- 
portant to know that the financial misfortunes of the Corporation in 
the past were only slightly related to the manufacture and sale of its 
products, but that these misfortunes were directly related to loose 
and uncontrolled methods which are now corrected.? 


With respect to one element of the program, however, and 


* For the last quarter of each year —from the annual reports of the 
General Motors Corporation. 


2 President Du Pont in the Corporation’s annual report for 1922. 


THE GENERAL MOTORS CORPORATION 209 


one that developed into a veritable fiasco, President Du 
Pont placed the blame squarely on the shoulders of Durant: 
the Samson tractor enterprise. 

The Corporation had entered the business of manufactur- 
ing automotive farm tractors in 1917, following the lead of 
Henry Ford & Son. By October 31, 1920, it had invested 
nearly $30,000,000 in the Samson tractor plants. After 
the product had been finally developed, it was found that 
it could not be marketed profitably at the announced price 
of $650; and prices were raised only to discover that sales 
could not be made in competition with lower-priced. trac- 
tors.‘ Despite increasing operating losses, which aggre- 
gated $12,000,000 in four years, commitments had neverthe- 
_ less been made in 1920 for an output of 130,000 tractors. 
The liquidation of these commitments and of the division’s 
inventories resulted in a loss of more than $21,000,000; and 
the Corporation retired from the tractor field after net 
operating and liquidation losses aggregating more than $33,- 
000,000.2. The tractor venture had been initiated and 
administered by Durant, according to President Du Pont.’ 

Operating deficits during 1920 and 1921 were confined 
to only a few of the divisions, but nearly all of them suf- 
fered large inventory losses. In all, the Corporation was 
forced to write off more than $90,000,000 from its valuation 
accounts and to absorb operating deficits of more than $15,- 
000,000.4 Large as were these book losses, it is interesting 
to observe that the net profits of the three years, 1919, 1920, 
and 1922, were amply sufficient to absorb them and to 
leave a comfortable margin of net gain. 

A careful examination of the Corporation’s affairs dis- 
closed the fact that ten out of its thirty-four operating divi- 
sions had been responsible for nearly two-thirds of its gross 
losses, and that the other twenty-four, on the whole, had 
been satisfactorily conducted. In the table below, which 
shows the relative importance of the two groups, the latter 
is labeled Group I and the former, Group II.s

The divisions of Group II included, among others, the 
Oakland, Oldsmobile, Sheridan, and Scripps-Booth pas- 
senger-car units, and the Samson Tractor division. The 
Oakland and Oldsmobile divisions were radically reorgan- 
ized; the Sheridan and Scripps-Booth enterprises were 
liquidated and discontinued;? and the Samson plants, as 
noted before, were diverted to automobile manufacture. In 
order to release for other uses much of.its investment in the 
General Motors Building, Detroit, which had been con- 
structed at a total cost of $20,786,000, the Corporation 
organized a subsidiary, the General Motors Building Cor- 
poration, which sold to S. W. Strauss & Co. an issue of $12,- 
000,000 of seven per cent serial bonds secured by a first 
mortgage on the building and by an agreement with the 
General Motors Corporation whereby the latter leased the 
building at an annual rental sufficient to provide the interest 
and retirement requirements of the bond issue.?

3056 in 1921. (From the records of the Corporation.)

THE GENERAL MOTORS CORPORATION 211 


In September, 1921, new operating policies were an- 
nounced by the Corporation. The executive offices were 
removed from New York to Detroit. A close control over 
inventories and over capital expenditures was inaugurated 
by means of a centrally administered budget system. Inter- 
company cash remittances, which had previously caused a 
continuous ‘float’ of approximately $10,000,000, and segre- 
gated cash reserves and balances, which had been previously 
controlled by the various divisions without regard for the 
requirements of other units or of common needs, were re- 
placed by a system of debit and credit book entries and 
centralized administration of all cash balances.2, A number 
of the smaller manufacturing units were consolidated, and 
_ various inter-divisional bodies were established to integrate 
the policies of the different divisions with respect to pro- 
ducts, sales, purchasing, advertising, and other matters. 
The program of coérdinated but decentralized administra- 
tion of the Corporation’s numerous activities thus formally 
initiated was developed in detail in the years immediately 
ensuing. On the basis of its own past experience and of that 
of others, the Corporation established systems of forecast- 
ing and definite, though flexible, standards of capital in- 
vestment, inventory requirements (physical as well as fi- 
nancial), ‘normal’ rates of profits to be attained, and fixed 
and varying costs— all measured, chiefly, by volume of 
business. Along with the exercise of these quasi-scientific 
statistical tools of centralized control, however, it main- 
tained or increased the operating autonomy of its various 
divisions: each retained its own personnel and its own plants; 
and each retained control of its own policies, subject only 
to the veto of the president of the Corporation.

Albert Bradley, assistant treasurer, before the American Management 
Association in March, 1926.

15. MANUFACTURING EXPANSION 


The crisis and depression of 1920-21 were followed by a 
recovery as rapid, unexpected, and complete as the depres- 
sion had been acute. In each of the early months of 1922, 
the sales of General Motors vehicles were more than double 
those of the corresponding months of the preceding year; 
in June, the output of the Corporation reached a new 
monthly record, which was broken again in November. By 
the middle of the year, the Corporation had paid off all its 
bank loans, of which $48,974,996 had been outstanding six 
months before, and of which $82,784,824 had been outstand- 
ing on October 31, 1920.? In 1923 the Corporation brought 
out radically improved models in each of its lines of cars, and 
its output, sales, and profits, as was the case, indeed, with 
the automobile industry as a whole, rose above all previous 
levels. The following table, in which the Corporation’s 
sales of cars and trucks are presented by months for the 
years I919 to 1923, inclusive, reflects both the sharpness of 
the preceding slump and the rapidity and fullness of the 
recovery.

214. THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE {NDUSTRY 


At the end of 1923, the General Motors Corporation 
looked forward, with the completion of certain unfinished 
construction projects, to a manufacturing capacity of 1,300,- 
ooo cars and trucks a year.? Its volume of business in the 
year Just then ended had been the largest in its history; its 
motor-car products had been extensively remodeled, and 
realigned with respect to price; its manufacturing operations 
now included the production of much of the materials and 
parts required for its chief products, Through the successful 
financial intermediation of the General Motors Acceptance 
Corporation and by other means, its sales organization had 
been greatly improved. Its foreign subsidiaries (both manu- 
facturing and selling companies) were growing in impor- 
tance. And its financial condition, with an excess of current 
assets over current liabilities of more than $140,000,000, 
was one of great strength. The keynote of the Corporation’s 
policies, therefore, as reflected by its annual reports of 1923 
and 1924, was one of stabilization. Thus:? 

The problem from the operating standpoint has become one of in- 
ig efficiency in management and greater refinement in pro- 

UCT eas 

In the earlier history of the Corporation demand so far exceeded 
supply that very little consideration was needed on that point. In 
1921 a definite policy was adopted. The Corporation should estab- 
lish a complete line of motor cars from the lowest to the highest price 
that would justify quantity production.... 


And again:3 


The motor car industry... has now certainly passed the point 
where tremendous annual increases are possible: In 1909-production 
increased 100 per cent over 1908; in 1923 the increase was only 55 
per cent; 1924 produced fewer cars than 1923; while in 1925 a reason- 
able gain is anticipated. The gain will not be tremendous; the days 
of immense increases are past.... The Corporation’s plans do not 
contemplate such increases. 


The year 1924 was one of moderate recession in the whole 
automobile industry; the output and profits of the General 
Motors Corporation each fell off by about 26 per cent; and 
_ ® Annual report for 1923. 2 Ibsd. 3 Ibid., 1924. 


‘THE GENERAL MOTORS CORPORATION 215 


this accounts, no doubt, for the sober note sounded in the 
last few lines quoted from the Corporation’s 1924 report. 
The gains of the next three years, however, surpassed all ex- 
pectations; and, far from resting content with a modest rate 
of growth, the Corporation greatly expanded its plant capa- 
city, madea bid of $124,650,000 cash for Dodge Brothers, Inc., 
added two new lines of passenger cars, reorganized its truck 
manufacture, absorbed the Fisher Body Corporation, and 
vigorously expanded the operations of its parts-producing 
and other branches (including, particularly, the manufac- 
ture of the Frigidaire electric refrigerator, which is now pro- 
duced in greater volume than any other similar device). 
Both in 1925 and in 1926, the Corporation’s automobile out- 
put and financial volume of business reached new high 
levels; but its most striking gains took place in the volume of 
profits. The latter clearly reflected the new manufacturing 
efficiency that the Corporation had been progressively 
developing since 1922. In 1925, with output and dollar- 
sales exceeding those of 1923 (the best previous year) by ap- 
proximately 5 per cent, net profits rose by more than 73 per 
cent; and in 1926, when output and sales recorded a further 
increase of 47 and 44 per cent, respectively, over those of 
1925, net profits again displayed a disproportionate gain, ris- 
ing by more than 65 per cent over those of 1925. In 1927, 
when the aggregate American motor-vehicle output suffered 
a decline of 20 per cent, General Motors scored further sub- 
stantial gains. The Corporation’s output, sales, and profits 
for the four years ended in 1927 are summarized below:*

Despite the fact that this product was manifestly competing 
with the Ford car (the small price difference between the 
two vehicles was partly compensated for by the additional 
equipment provided with the Chevrolet), President Sloan 
of the General Motors Corporation denied, though some- 
what equivocably, the intention of the Corporation to 
dispute the lowest price-field with the Ford Motor Company. 
In an address to a convention of publicity men on September 
27, 1927, President Sloan declared :? 

If the past is any indication of the future, the new Ford car will be 
a car that will appeal to a great mass of people.... 

General Motors is in quite a different position. General Motors’ 


idea is to make a car of greater luxury than Ford —a car that 
properly belongs to the next higher class... . 


The object of the Ford Motor Company’s new car, however, 
be it noted, is precisely to yield greater luxury than the old. 
Mr. Sloan, moreover, did not fail to remark: 

To make a long story short, it seems to me that both Ford and 
General Motors are governed by the same economic principles: viz., 
to give the greatest possible value in their respective price classes. 

There is absolutely no reason why General Motors cannot to-day 
give a greater per dollar value than any one in the world. 


Of the Corporation’s various manufacturing divisions, 
only the truck branch had failed to make substantial gains. 
The field of the motor bus, in particular, had been preémpted 
by competitors, while the Corporation’s passenger-car divi- 
sions had surpassed the truck unit in the field of light 
commercial vehicles. In September, 1925, the Corporation 


* Annual report for 1927. 
* Quoted in the Detroit News of October 2, 1927. 


THE GENERAL MOTORS CORPORATION 217 


transferred the assets of its truck division, valued at about 
$11,000,000, and some $6,000,000 in cash, to the Yellow Cab 
Manufacturing Company, a large independent truck-pro- 
ducer.* The latter was reorganized as the Yellow Truck & 
Coach Manufacturing Company, its entire common stock 
issue, 800,000 shares of no-par value, going to the General 
Motors Corporation. The stockholders of the predecessor 
company received $15,000,000 par value of seven per cent 
preferred stock, and $6,000,000 par value of Class B stock 
in the new enterprise; the latter stock was given equal voting 
power with common, and preference over the latter for three 
years with respect to dividends of seven and one-half per 
cent.? In 1926 this Company realized about $14,000,000 in 
cash from the sale of $7,000,000 par value of its Class B 
stock; the issue was underwritten by the General Motors 
Corporation, which subscribed to $4,045,500 par value of 
the offering. The unsatisfactory character of the Compa- 
ny’s operations during 1926 has led to rumors of its impend- 
ing consolidation with the Corporation. 


16. RESULTS OF RECENT FINANCIAL OPERATIONS 


The ambitious merger and combination projects that 
marked the history of the General Motors enterprise up to 
1921 have played a relatively small rdle in the Corporation’s 
recent history. The only important acquisition of the last 
half-dozen years has been that of the Fisher Body Corpora- 
tion, which was absorbed in 1926, but control of which had 
been acquired in 1919. 

The Fisher Body Corporation had been incorporated in 
New York on August 21, 1916, to combine into an operating 
and holding company several body-manufacturing enter- 
prises that had been organized by the same interests in 
1908, 1910, and 1912. Until very recent years, virtually all 
automobile producers purchased ‘bodies’ from such in- 
dependent enterprises; and the original Fisher Companies, 
financed largely through reinvestment of earnings, had ex- 


* Annual report of 1925. 2 Ibid. 3 Ibid., 1926. 


218 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


panded rapidly. In 1919, as already noted, the Fisher Body 
Corporation increased its capitalization from 200,000 shares 
to 500,000 shares of common stock, selling the increase to 
the General Motors Corporation at $92 a share, and, at the 
same time, entering into an agreement with the latter 
whereby virtually all of the General Motors body require- 
ments were to be supplied by the Fisher company at cost 
plus 17.6 per cent.? This contract was a very profitable one 
for the Fisher company; and the volume of its profits 
probably had much to do with the reluctance displayed by 
the minority interests, which were closely held,? in agreeing 
to dispose of their holdings to the General Motors Corpora- 
tion. Such an agreement was made, however, in the spring 
of 1926. In return for their 39.92 per cent interest in the 
Fisher Body Corporation, the minority stockholders re- 
ceived 664,720 shares of the common stock of the General 
Motors Corporation (at the rate of one and one-half shares 
of the former to oneshare of the latter).3 The sale took place 
as of June 30, 1926, and the General Motors Corporation 
immediately took over the physical assets of the Fisher 
company. The net worth of the Fisher Body Corporation 
on March 31, 1926, was $92,277,010, including intangible 
assets of $3,405,614; its net earnings in 1925 aggregated $27,- 
057,425.4 The minority interests exchanged a book equity 
of approximately $36,836,982 in the Fisher Body Corpora- 
tion for a book equity of approximately $57,283,687 in 
General Motors, receiving 11.46 per cent of the latter’s entire 
common-stock issue.’ At this rate the Fisher Body Corpora- 
tion was valued at about $143,000,000; its 1925 earnings, it 
may be noted, were equal to a return of 18.9 per cent on

this valuation. On the other hand, the market value of the

The dividend policy of the General Motors Corporation 
in recent years has been marked by great liberality. In 
1924, as we have noted, the Corporation simplified its cap- 
ital structure, consolidating its four types of preferred and 
debenture stocks into a single issue of seven per cent cum- 
ulative preferred stock, and substituting one share of no- 
par value common stock, capitalized on its books at $50 
a share, for each four shares of the previous no-par value 

common which had been capitalized at $10 a share.t The 
regular dividend rate of $6 a share on the new common stock 
was soon multiplied by special disbursements, causing the 
‘market value of the stock to soar. In September, 1926, 
several months after the regular rate had been increased 
to $7, a 50 per cent stock dividend was declared, and the 
new $7 rate, augmented by special dividends, was retained. 
In the boom market of 1926 and 1927, the increased number 
of shares did not prevent the stock from again reaching 
prices of $250 and more a share; and another ‘split-up,’ this 
time the substitution of two shares of $25 par value for each 
share of no-par value common stock, was authorized on 
September 12, 1927. In the meantime, in February, 1927, 
in order ‘to strengthen the Corporation’s cash position,’? 
an issue of $25,000,000 par value of its seven per cent pre- 
ferred stock was sold, through J. P. Morgan & Co., at $120a 
share. This issue, in view of the Corporation’s enormous 
cash resources, evidently aroused critical comment, for 
President Sloan felt called upon to justify it in a special 
letter to the stockholders.3 Between 1922 and 1927, in- 
clusive, the cash dividend disbursements of the General

Motors Corporation aggregated $405,665,914, of which the 
common stockholders received $360,682,070. During 1927 
alone the Corporation’s payments to its common stock- 
holders totaled $134,836,081, a greater sum than had ever 
before been paid in a single year on the common stock of 
an industrial enterprise. General Motors’ profits in 1926 
and 1927 exceeded those of any other manufacturing com- 
pany. 

The leading executives, lesser salaried officials, and 
ordinary employees have been permitted to share in varying 
measure in the recent prosperity of the Corporation. Under 
a bonus plan inaugurated in 1918, ten per cent of the amount 
by which the net earnings of the Corporation exceeded seven 
per cent of the invested capital was employed annually for 
five years in purchasing General Motors common stock on 
the open market for gratuitous distribution to employees 
who, ‘ by their inventions, ability, industry, loyalty, or other 
exceptional service’ contributed in special degree to the 
Corporation’s success.t In 1923, this plan was modified to 
apply chiefly to the Corporation’s junior officials, its ap- 
propriations, determined as indicated above, being reduced 
to five per cent; and separate provision was made for the 
senior executives. About seventy of the latter were invited 
to form a Managers’ Securities Company, to which they 
contributed $5,000,000 in cash in return for the Company’s 
entire common stock; the General Motors Corporation 
agreed to contribute annually to this Company, for a term 
of eight years, a sum equal to five per cent of the amount by 
which the Corporation’s earnings should exceed seven per 
cent of its invested capital, these contributions to be in- 
vested in General Motors common stock.? To provide the 
Managers’ Securities Company with initial holdings of 
2,250,000 shares, the Corporation offered to purchase com- 
mon stock from its stockholders at $15 a share. Since

poration’s common stock, most of the Corporation’s leading 
executives have become exceedingly wealthy men. It is 
expected that this plan will be renewed at its expiration in 
1930.3 The ordinary employees of the Corporation are en-

drawn for home-purchasing are entitled to the bonus; and

the Corporation’s seven per cent preferred stock.s All the

Despite the large cash disbursements to stockholders and 
employees, the profits reinvested by the General Motors 
Corporation during the six years ended in 1927 aggregated 
about $275,000,000. In these six years, the Corporation’s 
net worth increased by $391,500,000, or by more than 100 
per cent. More than three-fifths of this increase resulted 
from reinvested profits, and most of the remainder, from the 
absorption of the Fisher Body Corporation. The expansion 
of General Motors in recent years, therefore, has been 
mainly due to the striking growth in its manufacturing 
operations, rather than to wholesale acquisitions of inde- 
pendent enterprises. In 1925, however, the Corporation 
narrowly missed absorbing another large automobile pro- 
ducer — Dodge Brothers, Inc. At the invitation of the 
latter’s owners, J. P. Morgan & Co., acting for the General 

* From the annual reports of the Corporation, 1922 to 1927, inclusive. 


THE GENERAL MOTORS CORPORATION 223 


Motors Corporation, submitted two bids for this enter- 
prise: a cash bid of $124,650,000, and a bid of $59,000,000 
cash and $90,000,000 of non-interest-bearing notes matur- 
ing serially over a period of nine years.t The enterprise was 
sold, however, to a syndicate of investment bankers, headed 
by Dillon, Read & Co., who offered $146,000,000 cash. 

At the beginning of 1928, the General Motors Corporation 
occupied a commanding position in the automobile industry 
of the world. In the year just ended, it had produced forty- 
four per cent of all of the motor cars manufactured in the 
United States and Canada, as against less than seventeen 
per cent only three years before. Its parts-producing 
branches not only supplied much of the needs of its own 
_ large variety of motor vehicles, but also conducted an ex- 
tensive business with independent manufacturers. A joint 
policy of integrating and diversifying its industrial interests 
had accompanied its recent ascendancy. Approximately 
one-half of the unrivaled volume of its profits in 1927 had 
been derived from other sources than its motor-car opera- 
tions as such — from the production of components, re- 
frigerators, lighting and power plants, etc., and from its 
banking and insurance business.3 The balance-sheet of the 
Corporation on December 31, 1927, presented on page 224, 
valued its net book assets at $760,000,000; the market value 
of its securities on March 9, 1928, was 2.77 billions. 


17. SUMMARY OF THE FINANCING OF GENERAL Motors 


William C. Durant was a vigorous promoter and salesman 
rather than an operating executive; 4 and the career of the

General Motors combination, until very recent years, has 
largely reflected his personality. His temperament and his 
unbounded confidence in the automobile industry led him 
to seek rapid growth. He desired a wide array of ‘makes’ at 
the outset in order to keep his eggs distributed in many 
baskets; he came to desire a balanced line of parts and ac- 
cessory enterprises; and, whenever the funds of his organiza- 
tion permitted or whenever suitable enterprises could be 
acquired by exchange of securities, he was ready to expand 
by acquisition. And he was content to rely for his control 
upon the support of large stockholders and upon his ability 
to ‘trade on the equity’ of the ‘uncontrolling stockholders,’ * 
ment. attributed to E. R. Stettinius, of J. P. Morgan & Co., by W. A. 
P. John, in a biographical article (Joc. cit.). 


t These suggestive expressions are employed and developed by Hast- 
ings Lyon in his Corporation Finance, Houghton Mifflin Company, 1916, 


THE GENERAL MOTORS CORPORATION 225 


rather than upon actual ownership of a majority stock in- 
terest. In all its larger aspects, the present General Motors 
Corporation is of Durant’s making. 

In its first two years of existence, the General Motors 
Company acquired control of some twenty automobile and 
parts-producing companies. In addition to the liberal issue 
of securities, the Combination paid out $6,347,993 in cash 
for such acquisitions, while it realized only $4,504,306 in 
cash from its sale of stock. It is clear, then, that no large 
amounts of working capital were obtained by the sale of 
_ securities, but that, on the contrary, current assets and earn- 
ings were employed for fixed-capital expansion. The net 
sales of the Combination in this two-year period totaled 
$78,460,054. It was able to handle such a large volume of 
business despite its small provision for working capital for 
reasons already considered: the prevailing business practices 
in the industry permitted advance deposits on dealers’ 
orders, sales for cash immediately upon completion of the 
short process of assembling, and credit from suppliers; and 
its current earnings were large. The working-capital burden, 
in other words, was largely shifted to others. 

The profits of the Combination during its first two years 
aggregated $19,339,865, of which $18,279,297 was rein- 
vested in the business. In order to realize its primary object 
— the production of a large variety of automobiles — the 
bulk of these earnings was employed for fixed-plant ex- 
pansion and for the large inventories required by its eleven 
‘makes’ of cars. Thus, in the single year ended September 
30, 1910, when it realized less than $2,000,000 from the sale 
of securities, and when new acquisitions costing only 
$1,229,230 in cash and preferred stock were made, the 
Combination’s fixed-plant investment rose from little more 
than $6,000,000 to more than $14,000,000, and its inven- 
tories rose from $8,520,920 to $19,802,483. Because of its 
unduly large diversion of current earnings and working 
capital into fixed plant and inventories, the Combination be- 
came involved in financial difficulties at the end of its second 


226 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


year; and a $15,000,000 note-issue, from which it realized 
about $12,500,000 in cash, was floated. 

The bankers’ voting trust during the next five years 
brought financial stability, though at some sacrifice of in- 
dustrial expansion. The number of ‘makes’ was reduced to 
six, additional issues of capital stock for properties were 
small, and profits were largely devoted to the attainment of 
a liquid financial condition. This last was not difficult, for 
we have noted how modest were the ordinary working- 
capital requirements of automobile producers. The General 
Motors Company ended the five-year period with its note- 
issue retired, with no bank indebtedness, with $15,000,000 
in cash, and with a net worth augmented by nearly $20,000,- 
000. The reinvested profits of these five years had totaled 
$31,351,090, but the bankers had charged $12,531,014 
against the surplus account for extraordinary devaluation of 
plants and inventories. 

If we assume that the original invested capital of the 
General Motors Company consisted of its nominal net 
worth (represented by capital stock and surplus) on Sep- 
tember 30, 1909, less the amount of profits reinvested during 
the fiscal year ended on that date, we find that 75.86 per 
cent of the Combination’s net worth at the end of 1917 had 
been derived from reinvested profits, after reducing the 
latter by the amount of extraordinary surplus charges. 
(The actual total of reinvested profits constituted more 
than 87 per cent of the Combination’s net worth on De- 
cember 31, 1917.) Even this striking figure allows nothing 
for the reinvested profits of the subsidiaries prior to their 
acquisition (and these accounted for the bulk of their net 
worth), nor does it include deductions for fictitious or over- 
valued assets represented by watered stock in the original 
capitalization. 

In the three years, 1918 to 1920, inclusive, new invest- 
ment from without became the principal source of the 
Combination’s capital expansion; in these three years the 
net worth increased by $316,551,469, or by 293 per cent.

It is pertinent to observe that the property acquisitions of 
the General Motors Corporation during these three years 
consisted principally of enterprises that had built up their 
net worth largely by reinvestment of profits. We have noted 
that the Chevrolet and United Motors companies, which 
were the most important of the acquisitions, had originally 
issued large amounts of securities — half or more of their 
initial capitalizations — for promotion services; and that, 
in spite of this fact, substantially one-half of their net book 
assets at the time of their consolidation with the General 
Motors Corporation had been derived from direct reinvest- 
ment of their earnings. Such considerations require empha- 
sis because the absorption of going concerns by any enter- 
prise tends to disguise and to minimize the full réle of re- 
invested profits as a source of invested capital. 

In 1921, operating deficits, dividends, and ‘special write- 
offs’ reduced the net worth of the General Motors Corpora- 
tion by $65,459,057. In the five years following, however, 
because of a remarkable growth in the operations and manu- 
facturing efficiency of its constituent units, and the re- 
sultant increase in profits, the net worth of the Corporation 
increased by nearly 75 per cent, of which more than two- 
thirds came from reinvested earnings. The absorption of 
the Fisher Body Corporation, which accounted for nearly 
all of the remaining third, is subject to the same comment 
as that made above with respect to the Chevrolet and 
United Motors acquisitions. 


228 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


We have already called attention to the enormous cumu- 
lative effect of early promotion ‘water’ or business mis- 
judgment in the case of the Heany Lamp companies: the 
$5,908,500 par value of common stock issued for these du- 
bious assets in 1910 (exclusive of the $1,111,000 par value 
of preferred stock and $112,450 cash also paid for them) 
now represents more than 2,381,000 shares of the Corpora- 
tion’s $25 par value common stock, with a market value in 
excess of $320,000,000. The bankers, also, it will be re- 
called, received a bonus of $5,169,200 par value of preferred 
stock early in 1911, and exchanged much or most of this for 
common, offering the latter as a 20 per cent bonus to mem- 
bers of the underwriting syndicate of the note-issue; this 
stock, too, now represents an enormous interest in the 
General Motors Corporation. And so with other blocks of 
securities issued directly or indirectly for promotion and 
financing services, both in connection with the original 
formation of the Combination and with subsequent con- 
solidations. 

Disregarding all such considerations, and without going 
behind the formal book figures, we find that of the net worth 
of the General Motors Corporation on December 31, 1926 — 
$634,257,318 — approximately 55 per cent had been derived 
from investment from without by way of cash, property, 
and services exchanged for securities (including more than 
$64,290,000 of recognized intangibles); about 41.5 per cent 
had resulted from the direct reinvestment of profits; and 
about 3.5 per cent, net, from surplus transactions reflecting 
realized gains and losses in the value of the Corporation’s 
assets. At the end of 1927, reinvested profits accounted 
for 47 per cent of the net worth. 

Turning now to the working-capital aspects of the Cor- 
poration’s financing, we may note that it is significant of the 
essential liquidity of its operations that its borrowings from 
banks have been due, in nearly all cases, to special causes 
rather than to the routine nature of its business. Thus, in 
the three years beginning with 1915, when the difficulties of 


~THE GENERAL MOTORS CORPORATION 229 


the original Combination had been eliminated, the Com- 
pany was able to finance a large and sharply mounting 
volume of business without any bank borrowing whatever. 
The crisis and depression of 1920-21, coming on the heels of 
its enormous expansion program, caused a great and acute 
need for banking accommodation, but we have seen how 
rapidly these loans were repaid. Despite the great growth 
in the volume of the Corporation’s operations in 1925, 1926, 
and 1927, no sign of bank indebtedness appeared in its fi- 
nancial statements. The short cycle of manufacture, credit 
from outside suppliers, a manufacturing program attuned to 
current retail sales, and receipt of cash for its vehicles im- 
mediately upon production, make it possible for the Cor- 
poration to conduct the vast volume of its operations with- 
out any borrowing whatever in ordinary times. 

We conclude this review of the financing of the General 
Motors Corporation by appending tables which reflect in 
summary fashion various aspects of the Corporation’s his- 
tory: Table 41 lists the record of receipts from sales, net 
profits applicable to capital stock, and net worth (net 
equities of capital stock at the beginning of each year), by 
years. Table 42 presents the amounts and disposition of the 
Corporation’s earnings by years. Table 43 gives the per- 
centages of profits to net worth and of profits reinvested, by 
years. Table 44 compares the ratio of sales to net worth, the 
percentage of profits to sales, and the percentage of profits to 
net worth, by years.

CHAPTER V 
OTHER LEADING AUTOMOBILE PRODUCERS 


WE have seen that virtually all of the present invested capi- 
tal of the Ford Motor Company has been derived from the 
direct reinvestment of profits; and that not far from half of 
the present net worth of the General Motors Corporation 
has been similarly derived. In the case of the latter enter- 
prise, moreover, we have noted that the acquisition of full- 
grown business units which themselves owed much to this 
source of capital has operated to disguise and to minimize 
the full rdle of reinvested profits as a source of the Corpora- 
tion’s present net worth. The combined net tangible assets 
of the Ford Motor Company and the General Motors Cor- 
poration at the end of 1926 constituted about 56.7 per cent 
of the total tangible capital investment of American auto- 
mobile producers; and of the total combined net worth of the 
two enterprises on this date, only 27.6 per cent had been de- 
rived from original or subsequent investments from without 
of cash, property, or services (including promotion and 
financing services). The conditions that have made pos- 
sible so large a reliance upon reinvested profits for the pro- 
visioning of capital have been analyzed. It remains to be 
seen to what extent other leading producers of automobiles 
have similarly relied upon direct reinvestment of profits as a 
source of capital. 

Our choice of enterprises for this purpose has been 
based almost entirely upon present importance in the in- 
dustry as measured by volume of invested capital, except 
that several important producers, particularly the Willys- 
Overland Company and the Chrysler Corporation, have been 


* The National Automobile Chamber of Commerce, basing its esti- 
mate chiefly upon reports from its members, estimated the net tangible 
invested capital of American automobile producers in 1926 at $2,089,- 
498,325. Cf. Facts and Figures of the Automobile Industry, 1927. 


OTHER LEADING AUTOMOBILE PRODUCERS 235 


omitted from consideration because adequate data respect- 
ing their histories have been inaccessible. The enterprises 
discussed below, however, together with the Ford and Gen- 
eral Motors companies, constitute a large majority of the 
important producers. The combined net tangible assets of 
all eight producers at the end of 1926 represented nearly 
eighty per cent of the total tangible capital employed by 
all American automobile producers.' 

Our discussion of the six enterprises to be reviewed below 
will be confined to a bare outline of their financial histories, 
with emphasis only upon the growth in their net worth; and 
we shall rely largely upon a purely tabular presentation of 
the data. 


I. THE STUDEBAKER CORPORATION 


The present Studebaker Corporation is the immediate 
successor of an enterprise which, after a long career in the 
carriage and wagon industry, entered upon automobile pro- 
duction by diverting some of its resources to the new 
product. The larger part of the present invested capital 
of the Corporation has been derived from profits rein- 
vested in the business since its entrance into the automo- 
bile industry. 

The present Corporation was organized on February 14, 
I9II, to consolidate the Studebaker Brothers Manufactur- 
ing Company and the Everitt-Metzger-Flanders Company. 
The latter had been incorporated in Michigan on August 4, 
1908, to produce and sell an automobile known as the 
‘EMF’. Its operations during the two and one-half years 
of its existence had been highly successful, resulting in a to- 
tal output of 23,432 automobiles, in net sales of $21,236,415, 
and in net profits of $3,214,082. Its net worth on December 
31, 1910, was $3,649,258, of which $2,649,258 represented 

accumulated earnings.? The Studebaker Brothers Manu- 


_ *® See Table 1, above. 
* A. R. Erskine, president, History of the Studebaker Corporation, p. 21 
ff., published by the Corporation. 


236 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


facturing Company had been incorporated in Indiana on 
March 26, 1868, to manufacture carriages and wagons; in 
1905, it had begun to produce a small line of high-priced 
electric and gasoline vehicles; in 1909, it acquired sales rights 
to the ‘E M F’ car; shortly after, it purchased an extensive 
interest in the Everitt-Metzger-Flanders Company; and in 
1911, the two enterprises were merged into the present Cor- 
poration.* 

Unlike other automobile producers, the Studebaker Cor- 
poration started operations with a relatively large fixed- 
plant investment. The net tangible assets of the two pre- 
decessors totaled $15,354,121 in value, of which $9,811,990 
represented real estate, plant, and equipment.” An issue of 
$13,500,000 par value of seven per cent preferred stock, from 
which the Corporation realized $8,328,602 in cash, was sold 
through Goldman, Sachs & Co., to provide working capital. 
Nevertheless, the Corporation started operations with bank 
loans totaling $10,257,237-4 Against the physical assets of 
the predecessor companies, plus ‘good-will’ nicely calculated 
at $19,807,276.64, the Corporation issued $30,000,000 par 
value of common stock, $2,068,400 of which was returned 
to the treasury.5 

The end of the first year found the Company pressed for 
working capital; and in February, 1912, it funded its bank 
loans by the issue of $8,000,000 of five per cent ten-year 
serial gold notes. The next four years, however, brought a 
great improvement in the Company’s financial position: the 
note-issue was easily retired; net profits of $12,519,607 were 
reinvested in the business; the bank indebtedness was en- 
tirely eliminated; and $2,548,654 was set aside for the re- 
tirement of preferred stock.? | 


: A. R. Erskine, president, History of the Studebaker Corporation, p. 21 
ff., published by the Corporation. 

2 Erskine, op. cit., p. 95. 3 Ibid., p. 37 ff. 

4 Ibid., p. 95. s Ibid., p. 37 ff.

of $506,632 on the Corporation’s balance-sheet of December 31, 1912. 

7 Annual reports, 1912-15, inclusive. ‘ 


OTHER LEADING AUTOMOBILE PRODUCERS 237 


At the close of the World War, the Studebaker Corpora- 
tion, like most other automobile producers, initiated a large 
program of plant expansion; and to fund its current in- 
debtedness, which had again mounted to more than $10,000,- 
000, it sold an issue of $15,000,000 of ten-year seven per cent 
serial gold notes, dated January 1, 1919. This note-issue was 
called for redemption at the end of the year, however, and 
was replaced by an issue of $15,000,000 par value of com- 
mon stock, which was underwritten at $105 a share.t_ De- 
spite a jump of more than one hundred per cent in its fixed- 
plant investment during 1919 and 1920, the Corporation 

succeeded in reducing rather than increasing the volume of 

its inventories.2, Hence, it was one of the few automobile 
producers to escape serious reverses in the crisis and depres- 
‘sion of 1920-21. Indeed, and quite remarkably, its output, 
financial volume of sales, and net profits showed uninter- 
rupted increases between 1918 and 1923. On May 5, 1920, 
and December 29, 1922, stock dividends of $15,000,000 par 
value each were declared; and on April 1, 1924, two and one- 
half shares of no-par value common stock were substituted 
for each of the 750,000 shares of $100 par value common 
stock then outstanding. The business of carriage, wagon, 
and harness manufacture, which the Corporation had main- 
tained in diminishing measure, was discontinued in 1921. 
The results of the Corporation’s operations in very recent 
years have been somewhat erratic, both output and profits 
fluctuating irregularly. To maintain and to increase the 
importance of its position in the industry, the Corpora- 
tion has recently reduced the prices of its models sharply, 
bringing most of them into the moderate price class, and 
has introduced the ‘Erskine Six,’ a car selling at less than 
$1000. 

The history of the invested capital of the Studebaker 
Corporation may be briefly related. Its net worth on Decem- 
ber 31, 1926, was $119,033,833, made up of the following 
equities:

retains unchanged on its books, we may say that 68.3 per 
cent of the present net tangible capital of the Corporation 
has been derived from reinvested profits, after reducing the 
latter by all surplus charges, including financing fees. Most

The results of the operations of the Studebaker Corpora- 
tion are summarized in Tables 45 and 46." 


2. DopcE BroTu_ers, INC. 


The sale of the assets of Dodge Brothers, Inc., to a group 
of investment bankers in April, 1925, for a net cash price of 
$146,000,000, brought into renewed public notice the ex- 
traordinary profits of successful automobile producers. And 
the feat of Dillon, Read & Co., the principal purchasers, in 
shifting their investment to the public within two weeks at 
a gross cash profit of $13,250,000, while retaining for them- 
selves all the voting stock of the enterprise, can only be 
described as a startling piece of financial legerdemain — 
and all the more striking because it was effected with the 
utmost frankness. 

Dodge Brothers, Inc., was originally incorporated in 
Michigan on July 17, 1914, with an authorized capitaliza- 
tion of $5,000,000 of common stock, all of which, save for 
qualifying directors’ shares, was subscribed and paid for in 
cash by John F. and Horace E. Dodge.? For twelve years 
previous, the two Dodge brothers had been engaged in the 
manufacture of automobile parts for various producers, 
notably for the Ford Motor Company.: Such was the reputa- 
tion of their organization that when they announced, in 
1914, that they would produce and market a ‘ Dodge’ auto- 
mobile, they received more than 22,000 applications for 
dealers’ contracts.3 The Company started operations with 
the production of a single model of chassis on which several 
varieties of ‘bodies’ were mounted; and, like the Ford Motor 
Company, but unlike most other producers, the Company 
refrained from enlarging its line of products and from intro- 
ducing radical annual changes in its vehicles. The original 


* Both tables were compiled from the annual reports of the Corporation, 
which, in recent years, have contained summaries of the operations since 
incorporation. 


? Articles of incorporation filed with the Michigan Secretary of State. 
3 From data obtained by the writer from officers of the Company. 


OTHER LEADING AUTOMOBILE PRODUCERS 241 


price of the Dodge touring car was $785; during the post- 
war period the price rose as high as $1285; at the end of 1924, 
it was $885. 

The operations of the Company were markedly successful 
from the start. The Dodge car was sufficiently low in price 
and satisfactory in character to attract and retain a large 
following. In consequence, like the Ford Motor Company, 
Dodge Brothers, Inc., was able to concentrate its efforts 
upon means of enlarging output and economizing manu- 
facturing costs. Within a few years, the Company rose to 
third place among American automobile producers in unit 
output of vehicles. Despite a considerable measure of in- 
tegration of its operations in the interval, the productivity 
_ of its factory employees, as measured by physical volume of 
finished vehicles per worker, more than tripled between 
1916 and 1925." 

The fixed-capital expansion of the Company was financed 
entirely by reinvested profits. Even the original capital of 
$5,000,000 had been chiefly the result of profits accumulated 
by the Dodge brothers in their parts-making business; and 
the Company was able to meet its increasing working-capital 
requirements without any bank borrowing whatever.? The 
net physical assets of Dodge Brothers, Inc., on April 1, 1925, 
were valued at $80,658,682.3 

John F. Dodge and Horace E. Dodge died during 1920; 
and for the next five years the Company was administered 
by the legal representatives of their heirs. Early in 1925, 
the latter opened private negotiations with various interests 
looking to the sale of the enterprise. The Company was 
first offered to Henry Ford, it is said; 4 then to promoters 
who wished to combine it with the Packard Motor Car 
Company, the Hudson Motor Car Company and the Briggs 

t From a typewritten syllabus issued by Dillon, Read & Co. in April, 
1925. 

2 Ihid. 

3 This figure reflects a reappraisal; see prospectuses issued by Dillon, 
Read & Co. in April, 1925. ; 

4 George B. Hassett in the Detroit News of April 1, 1925. 


242 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


Manufacturing Company (body-manufacturers); then, 
when two of the tentative constituents of the projected 
merger refused to join, to the General Motors Corporation.’ 
At this point, Dillon, Read & Co., who had been supporting 
the merger project, offered a cash bid for the Company. 
After a spirited contest between J. P. Morgan & Co., repre- 
senting General Motors, and Dillon, Read & Co. — a contest 
which is said to have started with an initial bid of $90,000,- 
ooo — the assets of Dodge Brothers, Inc., were sold to 
the latter for a gross price of $166,000,000 and a net cash 
price of $146,000,000: the sellers were permitted to retain 
$14,000,000 of the Company’s cash assets and were given 
rights to $6,000,000 more of accumulated dividends, these 
sums reducing the price actually paid by Dillon, Read 
& Co. to $146,000,000.? 

The sale took place as of April 1, 1925. If the net worth 
of Dodge Brothers, Inc., on this date be measured by the 
net price paid for its assets, plus the cash retained by the 
sellers, but without allowance for the latter’s rights to 
$6,000,000 more of dividends, the net worth was $160,000,- 
ooo. In its eleven years of operations, the Company had 
paid out cash dividends in excess of $29,000,000;3 and, 
solely through reinvested profits and through the apprecia- 
tion in the market value of its business, its net worth had 
been multiplied thirty-two times. 

Dillon, Read & Co. proceeded immediately to reincor- 
porate the Company in Maryland (on April 8, 1925), and to 
sell to the investing public a sufficient volume of the new 
Corporation’s securities to yield them a handsome immediate 
cash profit, while yet retaining full control of the enterprise. 
This they did in the following manner: they realized 
$74,250,000 in cash (gross, without deduction for marketing 


t George B. Hassett in the Detroit News of April 1, 1925. 

2 Announcement of Clarence L. Dillon, Associated Press of April 8, 
1925. 

3 This sum represents dividends paid only between 1921 and 1924; 
the previous dividend disbursements are not known. 


OTHER LEADING AUTOMOBILE PRODUCERS 243 


expense) from the sale of $75,000,000 of the Company’s six 
per cent debenture bonds, which were offered at $99 and 
interest; they realized $85,000,000 more in cash (gross) from 
the sale of 850,000 shares of no-par value, non-voting, pre- 
ference stock bearing cumulative dividends of $7 ashare, and 
850,000 shares of no-par value, non-voting, Class A common 
stock, which were sold at the rate of one share of each for a 
joint price of $100; and they retained for themselves and 
their associates 650,000 shares of Class A common stock 
and the entire issue of 500,000 shares of Class B common 
stock, which latter class was given sole voting power in the 
Corporation.? In other words, the investing public, which 
absorbed the offerings with surprising alacrity,? paid $159,- 
250,000 in cash for the bonds, preferred stock, and 56.67 per 
cent of the non-voting common stock of Dodge Brothers, 
Inc., while the promoters and underwriters compensated 
themselves with 57.5 per cent of the Corporation’s entire 
common stock, including all of its voting stock. 

The prospectuses issued by Dillon, Read & Co. frankly 
declared that the preference and common stocks of the Com- 
pany were being issued ‘almost entirely against the estab- 
lished earning power.’ Under ordinary procedure, the bal- 
ance-sheet of the Company would have reflected this fact 
by containing a large valuation for ‘good-will’ and other 
intangible assets; and such a treatment would have afforded 
a minimum of deception. Instead, however, the bankers 
took full advantage of the opportunities for disguise made 
possible by the legalization of ‘no-par value’ securities; and, 
as a result, they were able to present an initial balance-sheet 
for the Company that contained a valuation of only one 
dollar for intangible assets. This they effected by placing 
such arbitrarily low nominal values upon the Company’s 
capital stocks as to reduce the total nominal capital lia- 
bilities to a figure consonant with the value of the net physi- 


m = Cf. prospectuses of Dillon, Read & Co. issued in April, 1925, and their 
announcements, through the Associated Press, of April 10 and II, 1925. 


2 Cf. Associated Press reports of April 10 and II, 1925. 


244 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


cal assets. In other words, instead of inflating property 
values by the insertion of a large ‘good-will’ item, they de- 
flated the capital liabilities to only a fraction of their market 
value. Thus, the preference and Class A common stock sold 
to investors for $85,000,000 were carried on the books at a 
total value of only $935,000! The preference stock was given 
a book value of $1 a share; the two classes of common stock, 
Io cents a share. Hence, while the asset side of the Dodge 
Brothers balance-sheet is innocent of the deception sup- 
posed to inhere in the inclusion of intangible items, the lia- 
bility side is highly (though not technically) misrepresenta- 
tive of the true state of affairs. Perhaps one reason for the 
adoption of this procedure was a desire to minimize the ap- 
parent quantity of securities obtained by the bankers for 
their promotion and underwriting services: the 650,000 
shares of Class A common stock and the 500,000 shares of 
Class B common stock that they received, constituting a 
clear majority of the entire common-stock issues, were given 
a total book value of $115,000!

at the beginning of its operations was substantially as fol-

We have noted that only the Class B common stock was 
given voting power; in other respects, both classes of com- 
mon stock were granted equal rights. The preference stock, 
which was made redeemable at $105 a share upon sixty 
days’ notice at the option of the Company, was given no 
measure of even contingent control. The debenture bonds 
‘were made convertible into Class A common stock at the 
option of the holders until a maximum of $30,000,000 had 
been so converted. For this purpose, the Class A common 
stock was to be valued at $30 a share for the first $5,000,000 
of debentures converted; and at $35, $40, $50, $60, and $70, 
a share, respectively, for the succeeding blocks of $5,000,000 
of debentures converted.

$8,250,000 of five per cent notes maturing serially in three

The nominal net worth of Dodge Brothers, Inc., on De-

the net worth by the price paid for the predecessor company, 
plus subsequent capital additions derived from reinvested 
profits and from the sale of $8,250,000 of three-year notes, 
the figure becomes $193,821,389. All but $13,250,000 of 
either total has been derived from reinvested profits and 
from appreciation in the value of the tangible and intangible 
assets of the enterprise.

3. THE PACKARD Motor CAR COMPANY 


The Packard Motor Car Company, through its predeces- 
sor of the same name, is one of the oldest of American auto- 
mobile manufacturers, the original company having been 
founded in 1900. The enterprise has confined its efforts to 
the production of relatively expensive vehicles and motors 
of exceptionally high quality; and, through its subsidiaries, 
it has engaged to a substantial extent in the wholesale and 
retail marketing of its products. In consequence, the Com- 
pany’s capital requirements have been relatively greater 
than those of other automobile producers. 

The present Company was incorporated in Michigan on 
September 3, 1909, to succeed a West Virginia corporation 

t Annual reports for 1925 and 1926. 


248 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY. | 


of the same name. The latter had been formed on October. 
24, 1900; in 1903, it had come into the control of a group of 
Detroit capitalists, who transferred the business from War- 
ren, Ohio, to Detroit;* the paid-in capital at this time was 
$400,000, all represented by common stock.? Shortly after 
its removal, the Company sold an issue of $250,000 of five- 
year notes, the purchasers of which received a bonus of 
$250,000 par value of common stock.3 In the next five years, 
through the reinvestment of profits, the net worth of the 
enterprise increased to $5,842,869. The West Virginia cor- 
poration was now dissolved, and its stockholders were given 
$160 par value of preferred, and $160 par value of common, 
stock in the newly organized Michigan company for each 
share of common stock of the predecessor.‘ 

The Michigan corporation started with a capitalization of 
$10,000,000, evenly divided between common and preferred 
stock. Except for the original investment of $400,000, 
all of its net worth at this time had been derived from the 
reinvested profits of its predecessor. In March, 1910, the 
Company sold to Hayden, Stone & Co. $250,000 par value 
of preferred stock, accompanied by a bonus of an equal 
amount of common, these securities having come into its 
treasury from the treasury holdings of the West Virginia 
Company.’ In the following month, a group of the Com- 
pany’s stockholders sold $1,000,000 par value of preferred 
stock to William A. Read & Co., who marketed it at $108 a 
share.® 

Because the quality of its product required the direct 
production of many of the components, the Company was 
frequently pressed for additional capital. Early in 1912, it

‘sold $2,000,000 of five-year debenture notes; an additional 
$1,000,000 was sold in 1914; two years later, $3,000,000 par

year (1916), in order to refund its maturing note-issue and 
to provide further working capital, the Company issued 
$5,000,000 of three-year five per cent notes; and in June, 
1919, it sold an additional $7,500,000 par value of preferred 
stock (the authorized limit of which had been raised to 
$20,000,000), using part of the proceeds to retire the 
$5,000,000 note-issue maturing October 15, 1919." The cri- 
sis and depression of 1920-21 forced it to borrow $10,000,- 
000 more, eight per cent debenture bonds being issued. ‘The 
rapid recovery of the two years following, however, enabled 
the Company to call the bonds for redemption on April 14, 
1923. | 

Marked improvements in manufacturing efficiency, the 
market success of its new six- and eight-cylinder automo- 
biles, and substantial increases in the demand for its avia- 
tion and marine motors, enabled the Company to expand 
sharply the volume of its business in 1925 and 1926. The 
net profits of 1925 reached $12,191,081, as compared with 
profits of $4,805,175 during the year previous; and those of 
1926 totaled $15,843,587. Throughout 1925, the Company 
purchased its preferred stock in the open market, and, at 
the end of the year, it called in the outstanding balance at 
a premium of $10 a share, these measures requiring a cash 
expenditure of $12,780,447.’ 

The net worth of the Packard Motor Car Company on 
August 31, 1926, was $46,390,944. The gross reinvested 
profits of the enterprise between 1903 and 1926 aggregated 
$54,014,634, or some $7,600,000 more than its present net 
worth. The difference is accounted for principally by the 

‘retirement of the original preferred stock of 1909, and of 
subsequent issues, through reinvested profits. In addition, 
the enterprise paid out cash dividends of more than $35,000,- 
000 between 1903 and 1926. The capital structure of the

very recently, has been forced to resort to the financial

252 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


have been relatively greater than those of other automobile 
producers because (1) it has specialized in the production of 
high-priced vehicles, the manufacture of which permits a 
slower rate of inventory turnover and a lesser reliance upon 
the products of independent parts-makers; and (2) it has 
undertaken the wholesale and retail marketing, through 
subsidiaries, of a substantial proportion of its output (35 
per cent in 1917 and 40 per cent in 1921 7), which policy has 
prevented it from realizing cash for its product immediately 
upon production, in contrast with the policy of most other 
automobile producers.

predecessor, by years, are summarized in Tables 48 and 49. 


4. THe NAsH Motors CoMPANY 


The Nash Motors Company is an example of a compara- 
tively recent entry into the automobile-manufacturing 
business that has attained a position of considerable im- 
portance through an expansion financed entirely out of re- 
invested profits. 

In May, 1916, James J. Storrow and Charles W. Nash, 
formerly chairman of the board of directors and president, 
respectively, of the General Motors Company, purchased 
control of the Thomas B. Jeffery Company, of Kenosha, 
Wisconsin, for which they paid about $5,000,000.? On July 
29, 1916, they organized the Nash Motors Company, in- 
corporated in Maryland, to which they transferred the as- 
sets and business of the Jeffery company in return for 
$5,000,000 par value of preferred stock and 49,990 shares of 
no-par value common stock; the remaining ten shares of the 


authorized common stock were sold for $500 cash. The — 


:

5. THE Hupson Motor CAR COMPANY 


The Hudson Motor Car Company affords another ex- 
ample of a leading automobile-manufacturing enterprise 
whose present invested capital has been built up almost 
entirely through the reinvestment of profits. 

The Company was incorporated in Michigan on Feb- 
ruary 24, 1909, to succeed to a partnership formed a few 
months earlier by a group of men who had previously 
been connected with the Olds Motor Works and the E. R. 
Thomas Company.! Nearly all of the original stockholders 
are still active in the direction of the enterprise. The Com- 
pany started with $15,000 in cash; models, patterns, and 
dies valued at $25,000; and subscriptions later paid in cash 
aggregating $58,990. Its initial product was a medium- 
priced passenger car; in 1917, a lower-priced vehicle, the 
‘Essex,’ was added. 


t The partners, and the original stockholders of the Hudson Motor 
Car Company were: Roy D. Chapin, Hugh Chalmers, H. E. Coffin, R. B. 
Jackson, F. O. Bezner, J. J. Brady, J. L. Hudson, and Lee Counselman. 
Cf. the Company’s listing application to the New York Stock Exchange, 
June 7, 1922. 

2 Ibid.; cf. also articles of incorporation filed with the Michigan 
Secretary of State. 


256 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY - ~ 


Except for $106,000 par value of capital stock sold to its 
employees in 1913, and 10,000 shares of no-par value stock 
given to its factory manager in 1925 for ‘cash and services,’ * 
the expansion of the Company has been financed entirely 
through reinvested profits. This statement is nominally in- 
accurate. Essex Motors, a Michigan corporation, was incor- 
porated by the stockholders of the Hudson Company on 
September 16, 1917, ‘to satisfy a demand from the distrib- 
utors of the Hudson Motor Car Company for a small and 
more inexpensive car... .’? The Essex car was manufactured 
in the Hudson plants, but the latter Company did not for- 
mally acquire the capital stock of Essex Motors in its own - 
name until May 27, 1922, when it issued 200,000 shares of its 
no-par value common stock for the same.’ Prior to this con- 
solidation, the operations of the Essex company, which had 
started with a capital of $250,000 in cash and $250,000 in 
‘property,’ had resulted in a total net loss of $732,794.4 

The Hudson Motor Car Company was one of the first to 
anticipate the recent market trend toward enclosed rather 
than open automobiles. By reason of marked economies in 
manufacture, it was able to market ‘closed’ models of both 
of its lines of cars at relatively low prices, its Essex car, in 
particular, becoming an important contender in the low- 
price class. Hence, in very recent years, the volume of its 
business has expanded considerably. 

The net worth of the Hudson Motor Car Company on 
December 31, 1926, was $43,078,016, nearly all of which had 
been derived from reinvested profits. Up to this date the 
net profits of the Company had aggregated $65,270,534, of 
which $22,817,854 had been disbursed in cash dividends, and 
$42,452,680, retained in the business. The capital structure 
of the Company has consisted only of common stock, the 


t Judging from the income accounts and balance-sheets of the Com- 
pany for 1924 and 1925, the Company received $125,000 for the 10,000 
shares.

6. THE REO Motor Car COMPANY 


The Reo Motor Car Company is a long-established auto- 
mobile-producing enterprise which has reached its present 
size by relatively gradual growth. During the more than 
twenty-two years of its existence it has omitted the payment 
of cash dividends in only a single year; and its present net 
worth, except for an original investment of $170,000 in cash 
and $130,000 in ‘property,’? has been built up exclusively 
out of reinvested profits. 

The Company was named after Ransom E. Olds, of 
Lansing, Michigan, who, we have observed, was one of the 
pioneer American automobile inventors and manufacturers. 
In 1904, after severing his connection with the Olds Motor 
Works, of Detroit, Olds had decided to retire from the auto- 
mobile business.? He was investigating peat fields in north- 
ern Michigan when he received a telegram from a number of 
Lansing business men inviting him to head a new automo- 
bile enterprise in which he would be given a controlling in- 
terest for his services. The Reo Motor Car Company was 
incorporated in Michigan on August 17, 1904; Olds was 
given $50,000 par value of preferred stock, which was soon 
converted into common, and $260,000 par value of common 
stock; the remaining stock, $240,000 par value of common, 


* The ‘property’ consisted of the patents, inventions, models, etc., 
of R. E. Olds, valued at $105,000, and $25,000 of real estate. Cf. articles 
of incorporation of the R. E. Olds Company, the original name of the 
enterprise. 

?7From data obtained from Mr. Olds in an interview in Lansing, 

Michigan, December 24, 1924. 


OTHER LEADING AUTOMOBILE PRODUCERS 259 


was subscribed by Lansing citizens, who, however, were 
eventually called upon to pay only fifty per cent of their 
subscriptions. 

Whereas Olds had previously been the first to produce 
a low-priced automobile — the ‘Merry Oldsmobile’ — he 
now contented himself with the more limited market open 
to a medium-priced vehicle. The Company prospered stead- 
ily, though not extravagantly. A large proportion of its 
earnings, particularly in its earlier years, was paid out in 
dividends, but its reinvested profits alone were sufficient to 
finance a gradual, then more marked, expansion. On Octo- 
ber 8, 1910, the Company organized a subsidiary, the Reo 
Motor Truck Company, to produce commercial vehicles; 
in 1913, it distributed to its stockholders, pro-rata, its hold- 
ings in this subsidiary; but in 1916, it reacquired the stock of 
the latter, paying share for share in its own common stock.” 
Like only a few other automobile producers, the Reo Com- 
pany established a number of retail sales branches, sepa- 
rately incorporated, in various cities. In very recent years, 
the Company’s truck business has exceeded that of its pas- 
senger cars;3 but in 1927 it redesigned its passenger cars and 
added a new line of lower-priced automobiles (retailing at 
prices just above $1000), with a resultant large increase in 
the popularity of its passenger products. 3 

In its twenty-two years of operations up to August 31, 
1926, the Reo Motor Car Company obtained aggregate net 
profits of $48,931,712;4 it paid out $21,903,429 to its stock- 
holders;s and, including the reinvested gains of its truck 
subsidiary prior to the consolidation, its reinvested profits

trucks and 73,152 passenger automobiles.

of the profits of the Reo Motor Truck Company, 1913-16. 

s Exclusive of dividends paid by the Reo Motor Truck Company.
