7. THE FINANCING OF THE SIX PRODUCERS 


The financial practices and policies of the six enterprises 
reviewed in this chapter are widely representative of vir- 
tually all the types of financing employed by successful 
American automobile producers. 

The Studebaker Corporation alone, of the six, started 
with a relatively large investment in fixed plant — an invest- 
ment derived, chiefly, from the earnings of the Studebaker 
wagon and E M F automobile enterprises. Up until 1926, 
only two of the six companies, the Studebaker and Packard, 
had found it necessary to resort to the financial markets to 
meet expanding capital requirements. With the exception 
of the Dodge enterprise since 1925, they are the only ones, 
likewise, which have had frequent resort to borrowed funds 
for long or short term periods. Two of the six had been able 
to retire original issues of preferred stock out of earnings; 
two others (and the Dodge Company prior to 1925) had 
issued only a single type of security, common stock; and 
the present invested capital of all of them, and very par- 
ticularly of five out of the six, has been largely derived 
from reinvested profits. 

There is no need to reiterate the conditions that have 
facilitated the expansion of these companies and of others 
with so little reliance upon additional capital, either fixed 
or working, other than that provided by reinvested earn- 
ings. It may be repeated that the two enterprises that have 
looked to outside sources for additional capital at times have 
been those that sought an early integration of their opera- 


t Part of the original investment, as well as of subsequent reinvested 
earnings, has been reduced in the accounts by elimination of intangibles 
and other surplus charges. 


OTHER LEADING AUTOMOBILE PRODUCERS 263 


tions. The others, engaging in the direct manufacture of com- 
ponents only as increasing resources permitted, and selling 
almost exclusively to independent distributors and dealers, 
have been carried along by the stream of incoming cash and 
by their reinvested profits. 


CHAPTER VI 


SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS: THE MOBILITY OF 
CAPITAL 


I, SUMMARY 


THE purpose of the present study was to answer, within 
somewhat limited scope, the following question: 

Given the existing organization of industry, and given a new im- 
portant product, how have the producers of this product come to 
command the economic resources required for its extensive pro- 
duction? 


More particularly, it was purposed to illustrate, by reference 
to the history of the American automobile industry, some 
of the specific processes whereby mobility in the employ- 
ment of capital is effected. 

The results of our inquiry require no extended elucida- 
tion. We have found that the fixed- and working-capital 
requirements of automobile manufacture were met, initially, 
by a wide diffusion of the capital burdens among a host of 
subsidiary, ancillary, and contributory enterprises; that, 
through such means, the entrepreneurs of the new industry 
were enabled to produce a maximum volume of product with 
a minimum capital contribution of their own. This was 
made possible, in part, by the large profits of successful 
automobile producers, profits which permitted them to out- 
bid producers of other goods for the services and resources 
of materials-suppliers, parts-makers, and marketing enter- 
prises. In part, this shifting and diffusion of capital burdens 
was facilitated by the technological character of the new 
product: the automobile made no impossible demands upon 
the capital equipment, technical process, and business prac- 
tices of then-existing contributory enterprises. 

The expansion of automobile manufacture was financed 
by a large growth in the capital resources of both the final 


SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS 265 


producers and their suppliers and distributors. Despite the 
fact that the latter’s resources were not nominally controlled 
by the automobile producers proper, the effect was much 
the same as if they had been. Indeed, the producers ex- 
ercised a considerable measure of even direct control over 
the business of parts-makers and sales-dealers: uniform re- 
tail prices and service policies, to take a single example, were 
established at the factories for all dealers. Only meager at- 
tention has been directed to the sources of the capital expan- 
sion of parts-makers and distributing agents, but sufficient 
evidence has been presented, perhaps, to suggest the im- 
portant réle of reinvested profits. The careers of eight of the 
leading producers of automobiles have been studied in some 
detail. We have found that the greater part of the growth in 
their capital resources was derived directly from reinvested 
profits; and that this source accounts for much the greater 
part of their present invested capital. 

While most of the statistical data presented in this study 
was obtained from original or official records, large allow- 
ances must be made for loose, arbitrary, and divergent ac- 
counting practices; and, in a few cases, for the omission of 
significant data.? In consequence, aggregate computations 
based upon these materials must be subject to some measure 
of inaccuracy. Nevertheless, the following table, in which 
the net tangible invested capital at the end of 1926 of each 
of the eight companies studied is compared with its net ag- 
gregate reinvested earnings, gives a rough, though conserv- 
ative, measure of the extent to which the present tangible 
capital investment of these producers has been derived from 
reinvested profits.

Il. GENERAL CONCLUSIONS 


It would be unwise to generalize very broadly on the basis 
of these findings. The capital requirements of different 
branches of industry vary greatly and must be met in dif- 
ferent ways. Railroad construction and operation, for ex- 
ample, could scarcely be financed in a manner analogous to 
the financing of the automobile industry. Nevertheless, our 
investigation has revealed the importance of certain general 
aspects of the process of capital mobility that are com- 
monly disregarded in the theoretical analysis of this subject. 


A. INDIRECT DIVERSION OF CAPITAL 


The réle of profits as a guide to the employment of eco- 
nomic resources is often described only in large and direct 
terms: large profits serve to attract entrepreneurs and capi- 
and, in some cases, fictitious or doubtful assets, acquired probably as 


a means of stock-watering, are accepted at face value as capital contri- 
butions. 


SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS 267 


talists, and, through them, the material resources required 
for the production of any good; hence, the productive fac- 
tors, particularly capital, tend to flow automatically to the 
industries most needing them, the importance of their need 
(and the risks involved) being roughly measured by the rate 
of return offered. 

This summary view, while valid in a general way, suggests 
a directness in capital movements that was certainly lacking 
in the development of the automobile industry. In the ab- 
sence of further analysis, it ignores the specialized, concrete, 
durable character of most capital goods: it implies that 
capital consists of a liquid fund of immediately mobile re- 
sources. And it postulates, without examination, the exist- 
ence of practices and institutions that insure the adequate 
mobility of capital. 

The early classical economists, who were much more em- 
pirical in outlook than many of their followers, implicitly 
recognized the obstacle to capital mobility that inheres in 
the specialized character of capital goods by distinguishing 
between ‘fixed’ and ‘circulating’ capital. Many later writ- 
ers, however, intent upon theoretical uniformity, or pre- 
occupied unduly with the easy transfer of liquid funds in 
the organized financial markets, have brushed over this diffi- 
culty by regarding capital as a fund of ‘free,’ ‘uninvested’ 
resources. This concept of capital, particularly as developed 
by J. B..Clark,! is useful for many purposes; but, since it 
presupposes the essential mobility of capital, it commonly 
leads to a complete neglect of the specific means whereby 
such mobility is in fact effected. Clark’s highly valuable 
work, and the otherwise excellent study by Gustav Cassel, 
‘The Nature and Necessity of Interest,’ among others, 
suffer from this defect, in the opinion of the present writer. 

In more specific analyses of the immediate processes of 
capital mobility, attention is often confined to two con- 
spicuous sources of capital for new or growing industries: 
(1) a portion of the temporarily idle capital and credit of 

t Essentials of Economic Theory, particularly chapters x1 and XVIII. 


268 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


business men is held in the form of money and bank deposits, 
and these, particularly when guided by organized financial 
institutions, are highly mobile; and (2) new additions to 
capital, through savings, commonly take the form of money, 
initially, and in this form such capital too may be readily 
directed into new profitable channels. As was first empha- 
sized by Walter Bagehot,? however, the larger part of a com- 
munity’s capital at any moment is in the form of specialized 
durable instruments — machines, roads, buildings, etc. — 
and the essential problem is to explain how capital so em- 
bodied may be transferred to new uses. A partial answer was 
given by Bagehot: concrete capital goods are destructible; 
the prevailing practice of the business world is to maintain 
‘capital’ intact, however short or long the life of particular 
machines; this maintenance of ‘capital’ frequently requires 
the substitution of new instruments, designed for new uses, 
for the old ones; and the rate at which particular forms of 
capital goods are ‘used up’ is partly determined by the 
changing forms in which ‘capital’ is demanded.? In the 
opinion of the late Alfred Marshall, not less than one-fourth 
of the total stock of capital, ‘even in a country in which the 
prevailing forms of capital are as durable as in England,’ 
must be replaced annually.3 This great replacement fund, 
which at one time or another largely takes pecuniary form, 
makes possible a considerable mobility of capital. The re- 
sults of the present study, in the opinion of the writer, per- 
mit us to extend and to refine this analysis. 

The réle of profits in the direction of productive resources 
is not limited to the direct attraction of capital to profitable 
industries. A new or growing industry may divert existing 
capital resources, through the purchase of materials and 
sub-products, without formally attracting to it the owner- 
ship of these resources; and such indirect diversion of capital 
to new uses is, in fact, one of the most important means 
whereby capital is transferred from one employment to 

t Economic Studies, p. 45 ff. 4 Ibid. 

3 Principles of Economics, 8th ed., p. 592. 


SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS 269 


another. An enormous proportion of this country’s capital 
is invested in mines, railroads, iron and steel manufacture, 
textile mills, and other primary industries, as well as in 
general merchandising and financial establishments, the 
products and services of which are at the command of all 
profitable industries. The capital so invested is, in effect, 
constantly mobile among the various products employing 
its services. The proportion of such capital that may be 
diverted to any new good is extremely elastic; it may be 
increased readily and sharply without formal change of 
ownership; and such indirect diversion of capital does not 
require a movement of liquid funds guided by organized 
financial institutions. Large profits serve in this connection 
to enable new or growing industries to outbid competitors 
for the services of capital equipment owned by others, and 
thus, in effect, though not on the surface, to mobilize such 
capital for new purposes. 

It was in this way, most largely, that the early producers 
of automobiles met the capital requirements of their pro- 
duct. Lands and buildings were frequently rented rather 
than purchased; independent enterprises, supplying their 
own capital equipment, produced virtually all of the com- 
ponents and completed virtually all of the physical assemb- 
ling; sales of automobiles were made to independent dis- 
tributors who maintained their own establishments; and, 
whereas the latter paid cash for the vehicles immediately 
upon production, the suppliers extended credit to the auto- 
mobile producers. Thus, the capital burdens of automobile 
manufacture were largely shifted to, and borne by, owners of 
capital who remained essentially outside of the new industry. 
The producers of automobiles were entrepreneurs par excel- 
lence; they alone were effectively responsible for the product, 
but their own capital contributions were at a minimum. 
The new industry readily obtained the services, but not the 
ownership, of large quantities of capital. 


270 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


B. CApitaL MOBILITY AND RISK 


This indirect process of capital mobility, which is far 
more widespread than has been formally recognized, has 
important effects upon the deterrent influence of risk in the 
financing of new and speculative industries. The flow of 
capital into such industries is commonly said to be greatly 
impeded by the large risks necessarily involved; and many 
writers appear to assume that the capital available for such 
industries is limited to the resources of speculatively in- 
clined individuals, or, at any rate, to funds more or less 
definitely earmarked for speculative purposes. In the case 
of American automobile manufacture, however, the develop- 
ment of the industry did not wait upon the direct assump- 
tion of large risks by the owners of the bulk of the capital 
that became diverted to its use; and the industry was able 
to tap far richer sources of capital than the so-called specu- 
lative fund. 

The explanation of this fact has already been anticipated. 
The wide distribution of the capital burdens of automobile 
manufacture involved a considerable diffusion of the risks 
of the industry, and therefore greatly diminished their deter- 
rent influence. Speculative as the industry appeared, poten- 
tial producers did not face the necessity of risking large 
sums of capital in their operations: the immediate manufac- 
ture of the product was shared by numerous independent 
enterprises which supplied the capital required for the man- 
ufacturing operations as such, and which assumed the im- 
mediate subsidiary risks involved therein. On the other 
hand, responsibility for the final product, and the essential 
risks of final design, integration of the purchased compo- 
nents, and factory sales, did not face those who actually, 
though indirectly, provided the bulk of the capital employed. 
Resources that would not be directly committed to the 
fortunes of automobile production proper were nevertheless 
attracted to and then supplied by hundreds of enterprises 
engaged in providing materials, components, and sales facil- 
ities for the new product. Mobility in the employment of 


SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS ort 


capital did not in this case require a wholesale assumption 
of new risks, nor, on the surface, a radical diversion of ex- 
isting capital. In other words, there was a far greater di- 
vorce between the functions of responsibility-taking and 
capital contribution than is commonly thought possible in 
new and speculative industries. In consequence, the diver- 
sion of capital to the automobile industry was greatly facil- 
itated. 

Such distribution of the capital burdens of particular 
industries, involving a partial diffusion and effective reduc- 
tion of risks, is far more common than is usually recognized, 
and accounts for a considerable measure of the actual 
mobility of capital. 


C. RELATION TO INDUSTRIAL TECHNIQUE 


The ability to shift and widely distribute the capital 
burdens involved in the production of any good depends to 
a large extent upon the relation of the product to the prevail- 
ing industrial technique. A product capable of employing 
materials, machines, buildings, and business institutions 
in common use will, other things being equal, require less 
capital on the part of its final producer than one that re- 
quires brand new materials, machines and equipment made 
of metals not now produced, and buildings of great peculiar- 
ity in design and construction. In the former case, the pro- 
ducer avails himself of capital resources already existing 
but owned by others, by purchasing only their current 
services as these are reflected in the intermediate products 
that he buys from their owners. In the latter case, while 
the producer may conceivably induce others to provide and 
to continue to own the peculiar capital goods that he re- 
quires, the probability is vastly greater that he himself will 
be forced to supply much of the capital necessary for the 
intermediate operations involved in the production of his 
product. Different products vary considerably in this re- 
spect, but that the former situation is, on the whole, more 
common than the latter, can scarcely be doubted. _ 


272 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


The automobile industry was particularly fortunate in 
this respect. Partly by reason of the time of its develop- 
ment, and partly by reason of the character of the product, 
it inherited an industrial technique peculiarly congenial to 
its requirements. The prior development of the rifle, sewing- 
machine, carriage and wagon, and other industries, had 
brought into existence numerous well-established wood- and 
metal-working enterprises which were equipped with special- 
ized tools and machines for the large-scale manufacture of 
a wide variety of sub-products. Carriage and wagon manu- 
facture, in particular, was in the hands of a great number of 
such enterprises. The resources and technique of these pro- 
ducers of components, as well as those of suppliers of primary 
materials, were easily mobilized for the automobile. 

A new product may command capital resources not only 
by diverting these from other goods, but also by effecting 
the utilization of hitherto idle utilities residing in existing 
capital equipment. Such increases in the efficiency of cap- 
ital are tantamount to absolute additions to the stock of 
capital; and while they are rarely reflected in pecuniary 
measurements, they may be of great practical importance, 
particularly in a period of improving technology. Some of 
the capital early devoted to automobile production came 
from the idle resources of machine shops and similar estab- 
lishments, which added automobile components to their 
lines of products without, at first, diminishing their output 
of other goods or increasing their capital equipment. It is 
often observed that productive capacity is rarely employed 
to its fullest extent: the actual output of American industry 
as a whole in 1923, according to the Census of Manufactures, 
for example, was only 72.2 per cent of the possible output, 
and in 1921, only 57.1 per cent.t The capacity of American 
automobile factories has been enormously increased in recent 
years without commensurate additions to the capital em- 
ployed, through the extensive use of mechanical power, 
machine-tools, scientific factory management, and other 

t Census of Manufactures, 1923, p. 1201. 


SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS . 273 


such means. Improvements in financial practice that permit 
the various reserve funds of industry to be reduced in size 
and given fuller employment, and improvements in trans- 
portation that make possible significant reductions in in- 
ventories of all kinds, storage facilities, goods-in-transit, 
etc., similarly release capital for new or extended uses. 
These considerations are intended to suggest that a com- 
munity may finance a considerable expansion in the volume 
and variety of product without a concomitant and propor- 
tional increase in the absolute volume of capital employed. 
There can be little doubt that the great growth of the Amer- 
ican automobile industry has been financed by the country 
as a whole through just such economies, in significant though 
uncertain measure, rather than entirely through new savings 
and the diversion of capital from other products. 

Speaking broadly, we may say that a wide distribution 
of capital among industries supplying raw materials, sub- 
products, and various kinds of services, and an improving 
technology, facilitate the mobility of capital. The concen- 
tration of capital in highly integrated industries, on the 
other hand, would seem to render capital less mobile. If, 
twenty-five or thirty years ago, industrial practice had re- 
quired that each final product be produced by a highly 
integrated enterprise that directly controlled the sources of 
the raw materials and all the intermediate manufacturing 
stages, entrance into the automobile industry necessarily 
would have entailed a considerable measure of concentrated 
risk. Indirect diversion of capital, with its attendant dif- 
fusion of risks, would have been virtually impossible. In 
contrast with what has actually taken place, the develop- 
ment of automobile manufacture must have waited upon 
the direct commitment of large quantities of capital to the 
major risks of a new industry. 


D. PROFITS AND CAPITAL 


The striking reliance of the leading automobile producers 
upon reinvested profits as a source of capital is to be attrib- 


274. THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


uted partly to the difficulty of procuring fresh capital for 
direct investment in a new industry, and partly to the 
ability of the controlling stockholders to retain undiminished 
ownership of their enterprises by indirectly shifting much of 
the capital burdens to others when expedient. The remark- 
able extent to which the present invested capital of automo- 
bile producers has been derived from their reinvested profits 
suggests the possible widespread importance of this source 
of new capital. Any more positive statement of this kind 
applied to industry generally must necessarily be based upon 
a wide range of inductive studies; but there is much evi- 
dence already to suggest that the importance of this source 
of capital is very great indeed.* 

This fact, if generally true, is significant alike for pro- 
blems of economic theory and for questions of public policy. 
Do corporate savings commonly entail the same psychic 
sacrifice that is imputed to individual savings? A substan- 
tial measure of retention of profits is fostered in all continu- 
ing enterprises by the fear of future reverses and by the de- 
sirability of providing against possible changes in products 
and markets. Such saving takes place automatically, as it 
were, being firmly grounded in the prevailing standards of 
judicious corporate administration; and it is interrupted 
only at times of exceptional reverses. In the case of new or 
growing industries, moreover, substantial further reinvest- 
ment of earnings by the individual enterprises is powerfully 
fostered because such reinvestment is often needed merely to 
maintain competitive positions. To the extent that corpor- 
ate reinvestment of profits is an expected and standard pro- 
cedure, discounted by investors, the abstinence of the indi- 
vidual investor, while real, is little more voluntary, con- 
scious, or direct than that of the taxpayer, part of whose 
income is diverted by government to the construction of 
public buildings, roads, etc. 


* In 1922, only 50 per cent of the net book profits of American cor- 
porations was distributed in dividends, according to Senate Document 
Number 85, 68th Congress, First Session, Distributed and Undistributed 
Profits of Corporations. 


SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS 275 


Large as have been the reinvested profits of automobile 
producers, their cash dividend disbursements have been no 
less striking. No extreme deprivation of current income on 
the part of the individual stockholder accompanied the 
building-up of great corporate surpluses: the payment of 
substantial cash dividends was the rule from the first. The 
annual net profits of automobile producers, until very recent 
years, have borne little relation to the amounts of capital 
actually risked. The rewards of their initiative might con- 
ceivably have been much smaller, and yet have sutticed none 
the less to attract their energies. It would seem that great 
corporate profits might be taxed far more heavily than they 
are now, without gross injustice and without stifling busi- 
ness initiative. 

Any policy of heavy taxation of business profits must 
reckon with ulterior consequences, however. Since profits 
constitute an important source of capital, any substantial 
public appropriation of them must result either in a slower 
accumulation of capital or in a diversion of capital from 
ordinary industry to publicly owned capital improvements. 
The latter use of profits taxes might indeed yield indirect, as 
well as direct, benefits by making possible a more extensive 
employment of government construction projects as a 
“balance wheel’ to mitigate the severity of business fluctua- 
tions. It is clear, in any event, that there is a close connec- 
tion between capital accumulation and profits taxation. 

It cannot be ignored, moreover, that profits function not 
only as a stimulus and reward to enterprise, but also as a 
guide to the employment of economic resources. The ready, 
if indirect, flow of capital and labor into the automobile 
industry, for example, took place largely in response to the 
promise of unusual profits. Had all but a modest rate of re- 
turn been subject to public appropriation, the diversion of 
productive resources into the new industry would have been 
slow and uncertain — unless, indeed, equivalent stimulus 
had been provided under a policy of centralized regulation of 
industrial development. In fact, a policy of radical public 


276 THE AMERICAN AUTOMOBILE INDUSTRY 


appropriation of large business profits would seem to imply 
some measure of centralized control of business activity, for, 
under the existing organization of industry, profits consti- 
tute the chief guide for, and regulator of, the distribution 
of productive resources. 


THE END
